1 
 
Foreword 
 
Welcome weirdos, 
 
Grubby fantasy has always been the awkward third cousin of high and 
heroic fantasy. It isn’t bleak enough to be truly ‘dark’ fantasy so let’s not 
get confused here. Its presence on the tabletop goes back much further 
than we generally care to admit. Tales of ratcatchers, gong farmers, 
jesters, and other like weirdos facing down the inimical cosmic horrors 
that would unmake this world has a surprising amount of precedent in 
our chosen hobby.  
 
So, there must be some flecks of magic in this genre if it keeps us coming 
back with such regularity. This book you are reading aims to capture 
and fuse as many of those flecks as possible: taking traditional fantasy, 
mixing in a dash of gallows humour, a pinch of class prejudice, a 
smattering of mutating sorcery and just the finest grains of heroism as 
final seasoning. This is all to provide you - the betentacled gamemaster - 
with satisfying campaigns of grubby fantasy for many years to come.  
 
Mud, Blood & Rats is the product of years of notes, playtesting and 
unrequited (gaming) desires and I hope it brings you as much grim joy 
in playing it as it has brought me in writing it.  
 
G. A Mitchell, 2026 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The cultists, mutants, and miscellaneous madmen all 
gathered about the jewel, chanting to their dark gods in 
growing expectation and ecstasy. So soon. So soon would 
their demon lord Azabael, be called forth from the 
nether regions of the world. Victory was at hand. They 
could taste the corruption in the air.  
 
But what’s this? Witchfinders at the door. The smell of 
black powder. The crack of pistols. The taste of one’s 
own blood… 
 
 
 
Excerpt from The Saga of the Black Jewel – Vol. III  
 
 
 


2 
 
Introduction
 
Welcome to Mud, Blood & Rats 
Welcome to Mud, Blood & Rats, a standalone setting guide for the 
Journeyman, Expert, Master roleplaying game. As ever, the design 
goals of JEM remain the same. They are to: 
 
▶ Provide a classless, skills-based system that is easy to read.  
▶ Ensure a unified mechanic is used for task resolution that involves 
rolling polyhedral dice and aiming to roll high. 
▶ Ensure all character abilities, such as spells and special attacks are 
handled with a single resource pool: Fatigue Points. 
 
What is ‘Grubby Fantasy’? 
Unlike some other versions of JEM (most notably, Advanced 
Fantasy), Mud, Blood & Rats seeks to allow a game master to create 
a fantasy campaign that is grim, grimy and gruesome. Grubby fantasy 
finds its roots in British roleplaying, comedy and European fantasy. 
As a genre, it allows players to explore an unreal world that is like 
our own, but darker and more fearful. The following tenets make up 
a grubby fantasy world, and Mud, Blood and Rats, seeks to emulate 
and convey these: 
 
▶ Life is cheap: In this setting guide, characters are fragile and may 
die. Sometimes the arbitrary nature of combat may result in an 
unexpected, pointless, or even slightly undignified death of a 
character within the game world. 
▶ Power is danger: Most of the characters will be rather powerless 
peasants: ratcatchers, labourers and servants. They will be pitted 
against the powerful, who have a proclivity for corruption. 
Characters such as mad noblemen and insane daemon-worshipping 
sorcerers make up the villainy of such a setting. 
▶ Morality is embodied: Characters in a grubby fantasy world must 
embody their morality as best as they can, as their sins can take on 
manifest form. Those of poor morality or proximity to evil might 
mutate, and their physiology and souls may become warped and 
tainted. 
▶ The end is nigh: In such a setting, it is only expected that most 
folk fear that the end of days is fast approaching. This of course, is 
up to you as the game master, but there should be a general sense 
of foreboding that things could and might soon end for good and for 
all. 
▶ Humour is necessary: A grubby fantasy setting can be 
oppressively bleak if expressed incorrectly. To punctuate the 
darkness, such a setting naturally lends itself to farcical gallows 
humour and dark comedy. 
 
Dice 
The game uses several dice. They are mostly your standard 
roleplaying game dice but in case you are unfamiliar with these, 
they are explained below: 
 
▶ D3: A die whose outcome must be 1-3. Achieve this by rolling a 
d6 and halving the number. 
▶ D4: A four-sided die. 
▶ D6: A six-sided die. These are used frequently for ‘armour saves’ 
and it’s recommended each player has several to hand. 
▶ D8: An eight-sided die. 
▶ D10: A ten-sided die. 
▶ D12: A twelve-sided die.  
▶ D20: A twenty-sided die. This is by far the most common die 
used in the game. 
▶ D100: A hundred-sided die. This can be achieved by rolling 
2d10s of a different colour, with one representing the ‘tens’ and the 
other corresponding to the ‘units’. 
 
Game Master and Player Character 
One person playing the game will be the ‘game master’ (sometimes 
abbreviated to the GM). The game master runs the scenario, plays 
all the allies, and monsters and describes the world and the 
outcome of player actions. On the other hand, most people playing 
will be referred to as ‘players’. Players have one ‘player character’ 
each, that is, a creature within the game world that can try and 
influence the outcome of the narrative or moment with their 
actions.  
Player Character Traits 
Core Attributes and Skills 
Each character in the game has five core attributes and 30 skills 
appropriately designed and prepared for use in a Mud, Blood & Rats 
campaign. 
 
Proficiency Levels 
For each core attribute and skill, a character will be one of six 
proficiency levels, known as: Untrained (0), Trained (1), 
Journeyman (2), Expert (3), Master (4) or Grandmaster (5).  
 
Untrained means the character possesses no formal education or 
even raw talent with the skill or attribute, Trained reflects 
generalised training and most skills or attributes that have any 
training, will likely be of Trained proficiency level. Journeyman is 
reflective of a skill that a character has spent some time working on. 
Expert will be reserved for just a handful of skills that someone at 
the height of their profession might have achieved. A skill of the 
Master proficiency level reflects many years of highly focused 
specialisation. Lastly, few if any skills will reach the level of 
Grandmaster, which might reflect a once in a generation level of 
competency at a craft. 
 
The target number to succeed on a skill check that a character has 
the Journeyman proficiency level for example will be the same for 
all journeyman skills. This target number is defined by the 
character’s current experience level as described later. 
 
Talents 
As well as attributes and skills, characters gain access to talents, 
starting with two at first level and unlocking more as they gain 
experience. Talents reflect specific qualities a character has, and 
some talents have requirements before they can be taken. They may 
improve skills, offer access to supernatural abilities, or reduce the 
cost of certain skill or power usages. 
 
Hit Points (HP) 
A character will have a number of hit points as determined by their 
level (this is usually 1 per level up to 9th level, but certain talents and 
attribute levels may adjust this). When a character falls to 0HP, they 
are unconscious and dying. A character naturally heals 1HP per 
day. 
 
Fatigue Points (FP) 
Fatigue points represent the characters own internal energy and grit. 
It is spent to perform weapon-shattering blows, accomplish various 
martial feats of arms, and cast magical spells and the like. FP 
increases as characters level up. To recover all FP, a character must 
rest for at least an hour, as part of a short rest or for eight hours as 
part of a long rest. 
 
A character may also ‘Flip’ FP to reroll the dice of a normal test. 
 
Grit and Grime  
Grit represents a character’s ability to fight on no matter the odds 
and the discretion of the gods to protect this mortal. It is the most 
precious resource a character has as it is a mixture of their 
providence and unbroken will. Player characters may spend a point 
of grit to avoid a blow that would kill or maim them or to 
automatically succeed a test. 
 
Grime on the other hand represents the growing stain on one’s 
soul which if left unchecked may lead to mutation and madness. 
Characters may choose to accrue grime points in order to gain 
rerolls and bonuses to tests, but if this number raises too high, 
then they will be punished, mind, body and soul.  
 
 


 
3 
 
Core Attributes 
In MB&R each character has five core attributes which reflect their 
different central traits. These are as follows: Strength (STR); Agility 
(AGI); Endurance (END); Intellect (INT) and Personality (PER). 
 
Strength (STR) 
This reflects a character’s physical ability to bring brute force to bear. 
It is commonly used for lifting things, moving obstacles out of the 
way and other acts of physique.  
 
A character who has training in Strength gains a bonus to damage 
caused by melee attack tests, and determines the number of carry 
slots a character has, as listed on the table below:

Agility (AGI) 
This reflects a character’s hand-eye co-ordination as well as their fine 
motor skills, flexibility, and general grace. 
 
A character who has training in Agility gains a bonus to damage 
caused by weapons that require a ranged attack test. In addition, 
they gain an innate power save as a result of their lightning reflexes. 
This power save applies to all hit locations.

‘Come you spirits that tend on 
mortal thoughts! Come you 
murdering ministers from where 
you wait on mischief! Come 
thick night and pall thee in the 
blackest smoke of hell!’ 
 
 
 
Lady McBetac, Countess of 
Crannogshire, a lunar cycle before 
she was arrested by witch hunters 
and burned. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Endurance (END) 
Endurance is a character’s capacity to keep going even when things 
are difficult. It is used in hostile environments to test perseverance, 
and it directly affects a character’s ability to stay in a fight. 
 
A character who has higher Endurance benefits from increased HP 
at certain levels. If a character is entitled to bonus HP, then they gain 
these bonus hit points at levels 1, 3, 5, 7 and 9. If a character later 
gains these END bonuses, they may add them to their character 
retroactively.

Intellect (INT) 
Intellect is a character’s mental capacity for learning and articulation. 
It used for recalling information, especially data learned in a formal 
setting. 
 
A character who has higher Intellect benefits from lower XP 
requirements to level up. 
 
INTELLECT BONUSES

-15% 
 
Personality (PER) 
Personality is a character’s personal charisma and willpower. It 
reflects an ability to resist charms, both supernatural and mundane, 
and protects a character from many of the more deleterious forms of 
magical attack. 
 
A character who has higher Personality benefits from the ability to 
have more followers in a campaign. Followers are friends and 
retainers that will accompany a character into dangerous situations 
and adventure sites. In addition, characters with a higher PER score 
receives bonuses on Reaction Rolls.

4 
 
Creating Characters 
Creating characters for Mud, Blood & Rats is a step-by-step process. 
To complete this it is recommended you print the character sheet 
provided but, in a pinch, you can muddle through with some paper 
and a pencil.  
 
1. Determine a Heritage 
A character’s heritage represents their background. In MB&R this 
determines the character’s species. There are four possible species: 
humans, elves, dwarfs, and halflings. The heritage will determine 
what the proficiency level of your Core Attributes are at the start of 
the game, and they may offer other abilities such as skill training, 
certain talents, and access to talent trees. In addition, it will list the 
Grit points and Grime limit of the character. 
 
At the start of the game, players are encouraged to roll to see what 
their character’s heritage is. 
 
2. Determine a Career and Starting Skills 
A character should now determine their starting career. This is 
usually rolled randomly but may be chosen. A character gains all the 
listed gear under their career and may immediately choose two of 
their career’s major skills to improve by +1 proficiency level (up 
to the maximum listed value.  
 
3. Choose Starting Talents 
The character then chooses two talents from their career to gain. 
Neither of these can be the Change Career talent. 
 
4. Note Starting Equipment 
A character’s starting career will list some gear. For the character’s 
first career, they gain these pieces of equipment now and should 
note down what they have received. The Equipment, Gear and 
Services section (p.53) gives rules for each piece of starting 
equipment. 
5. Choose an Experience Path 
There are two experience paths in MB&R. A character’s experience 
path determines their general outlook and broadly helps determine 
how specialised they are in their trained skills. 
 
▶ Jack: The Jack experience path means the target numbers for 
untrained skills will generally be lower, representing a willingness 
to have a go at everything. However, the other proficiency levels will 
generally be higher, meaning they will never be quite as good at any 
one thing as a Specialist.  
▶ Specialist: The Specialist has very high target numbers for 
untrained skills, meaning that if they lack training in a specific area, 
it will be very hard for them to act competently. However, where 
they do have training, they will almost always outclass a Jack, even 
of the same level. 
 
6. Note Hit Points 
Note the character’s hit points, which are usually 4 at 1st level, 
depending on a character’s END and specific talents they may have 
chosen. 
 
7. Note Fatigue Points 
Note the character’s fatigue points. These are a generic resource used 
to activate special abilities such as particular combat moves or 
supernatural powers. Note that this is usually 6. 
 
8. Choose Virtue, Vice and Ambition 
A character should choose a virtue, a vice and a starting ambition 
for their character. Leaning into virtues and vices as well as 
achieving ambitions grant extra experience points. The Game 
Mastery section (p.81) covers these in more detail.  
 
9. Name the Character 
All characters need a name as befits their status as adventurers. 
Examples are given in the appendices.

6 
 
Creating Characters: Mud, Blood & Rats
Introduction 
The following information is to be used to create characters that will 
exist in a grim, grimy world of grubby fantasy. This style of fantasy 
and heroic story telling finds its own lineage in the pulp sword and 
sorceries of the first half of the 20th Century, before mutating itself 
due to the influence (or corruption) of British television comedies, 
comics and games of the second half of that same century. More 
modern examples of grubby fantasy settings may be found in 
contemporary examples of Central European speculative fiction. 
Over the coming pages you will find rules for creating characters – 
their heritage and as well as their careers, talents and gear. 
 
Determining a Heritage 
As a general rule, a player does not choose their own heritage in 
Mud, Blood & Rats and instead rolls on the table below to 
determine what their heritage is. If a player allows their heritage to 
be determined randomly then the required amount of experience 
points to reach each new level is reduced by -5% xp. Such a bonus 
may be stacked with bonuses gained from INT experience point 
modifiers and those from also having a random career. 
 
 
Determining a Starting Career 
Like heritages, a player does not generally choose their own 
starting career and instead rolls on the table below to determine 
what their character’s career is. If a player allows their career to be 
determined randomly then the required amount of experience 
points to reach each new level is reduced by -10% xp. If a player 
rolls and is really dissatisfied with the outcome, they may roll once 
more, giving them a choice of two careers, but the bonus is 
reduced from -10% to -5%. Such a bonuses may be stacked with 
bonuses gained from INT experience point modifiers and those 
from also having a random heritage. A player who is still 
dissatisfied may of course choose their starting career, reducing the 
bonus to -0%. If a player chooses a career for their character, it 
may only be a basic career. 
 
Once the career has been determined, a character gains all the 
listed gear under their career and may immediately choose two 
talents from their career to gain.

Heritages 
7 
 
Heritages 
The following heritages are available for characters within the game. 
Broadly speaking they are a character’s species and give the 
character access to certain abilities and skills. 
 
Elf 
Elves are ancient beyond comparison and view the other races, 
especially humans, as unkempt savages, incapable of higher art and 
civilisation. An elf is lithe and delicately bodied with black, almond 
shaped eyes and pointed ears. 
 
MOVEMENT AND SIZE 
▶ An elf has a movement of 30’ per round, 150’ per turn and 24 
miles per day. 
▶ An elf is of medium size. 
 
STARTING CORE ATTRIBUTES: 
Elves start with the following proficiency levels in their core 
attributes: 
▶ +1 Proficiency Level (Trained) in INT and PER. 
▶ +2 Proficiency Levels (Journeyman) in AGI. 
 
STARTING SKILLS: 
Elves start with the following proficiency levels in their skills: 
▶ +1 Proficiency Level (Trained) in the Composure skill. 
▶ +1 Proficiency Level (Trained) in the Lore (History) skill. 
▶ +1 Proficiency Level (Trained) in the Perception skill. 
▶ +1 Proficiency Level (Trained) in the Melee (Any) skill. 
▶ +1 Proficiency Level (Trained) in the Ranged (Hand-Drawn) 
skill. 
 
STARTING GRIT AND GRIME LIMIT: 
Elves start with the following Grit Points and Grime Limit. 
▶ Elves begin with 1 Grit Point. 
▶ Elves begin with a Grime Limit of 8. 
 
STARTING LANGUAGES: 
Elves start with the following languages, knowing them as first 
languages: 
▶ Common 
▶ Elvish 
 
HERITAGE TALENT: LOW LIGHT VISION 
An elf has low light vision and therefore can see in very dim light 
(but not total darkness) up to 60’. 
 
HERITAGE TALENT: IMMACULATE FORM 
If an elf ever gains a mutation, then they do not roll to see whether 
they gain a psychological or physical mutation: they automatically 
gain a psychological mutation in such a situation. 
 
 
Dwarf 
Dwarfs are a dour folk who stand a mere four feet tall on average. 
They are proud and traditionalist to the point of hubris. Each male 
dwarf sprouts a long, flowing beard as a symbol of his honour and 
prestige. 
 
MOVEMENT AND SIZE 
▶ A dwarf has a movement of 25’ per round, 125’ per turn and 20 
miles per day. 
▶ A dwarf is of small size. 
 
STARTING CORE ATTRIBUTES: 
Dwarfs start with the following proficiency levels in their core 
attributes: 
▶ +1 Proficiency Level (Trained) in STR and PER. 
▶ +2 Proficiency Levels (Journeyman) in END. 
 
STARTING SKILLS: 
Dwarfs start with the following proficiency levels in their skills: 
▶ +2 Proficiency Levels (Journeyman) in the Composure skill. 
▶ +1 Proficiency Level (Trained) in the Consume Alcohol skill. 
▶ +1 Proficiency Level (Trained) in the Melee (One-Handed) skill. 
▶ +1 Proficiency Level (Trained) in the Ranged (Crossbow) skill. 
▶ +1 Proficiency Level (Trained) in the Set/Remove Traps skill. 
 
STARTING GRIT AND GRIME LIMIT: 
Dwarfs start with the following Grit Points and Grime Limit. 
▶ Dwarfs begin with 1 Grit Point. 
▶ Dwarfs begin with a Grime Limit of 12. 
 
STARTING LANGUAGES: 
Dwarfs start with the following languages, knowing them as first 
languages: 
▶ Common 
▶ Dwarfish 
 
HERITAGE TALENT: LOW LIGHT VISION 
A dwarf has low light vision and therefore can see in very dim light 
(but not total darkness) up to 60’. 
 
 


Heritages 
8 
 
 
Halfling 
Halflings are short humanoids standing around three feet tall. They 
are well-integrated into human societies and often take on roles as 
merchants and chefs. Due to some antediluvial providence, halfling 
physiology is surprisingly resistant to the mutagenic effects of 
accumulated grime.  
 
MOVEMENT AND SIZE 
▶ A halfling has a movement of 25’ per round, 125’ per turn and 20 
miles per day. 
▶ A halfling is of small size. 
 
STARTING CORE ATTRIBUTES: 
Halflings start with the following proficiency levels in their core 
attributes: 
▶ +1 Proficiency Level (Trained) in AGI and INT. 
▶ +2 Proficiency Levels (Journeyman) in PER. 
 
STARTING SKILLS: 
Halflings start with the following proficiency levels in their skills: 
 
▶ +1 Proficiency Level (Trained) in the Gossip skill. 
▶ +1 Proficiency Level (Trained) in the Haggle skill. 
▶ +1 Proficiency Level (Trained) in the Sleight of Hand skill. 
▶ +1 Proficiency Level (Trained) in the Craft (Cookery) skill. 
 
STARTING GRIT AND GRIME LIMIT: 
Halflings start with the following Grit Points and Grime Limit. 
▶ Halflings begin with 2 Grit Points. 
▶ Halflings begin with a Grime Limit of 16. 
 
STARTING LANGUAGES: 
Halflings start with the following languages: 
▶ Common 
 
HERITAGE TALENT: RANDOM 
Halflings begin play with one random talent from the Random Talent 
table below.

Human 
Where dwarfs may be hardy and honourable, elves graceful and 
learned, humans boast one singular advantage: they are numerous. 
By far the most populous species in the known world, humans are 
a changeable humanoid race capable of deep greed, cruelty and 
wilful ignorance. As a cosmic joke, the gods have also given them 
an unpredictable capacity for kindness, mercy and charity. 
 
MOVEMENT AND SIZE 
▶ A human has a movement of 30’ per round, 150’ per turn and 24 
miles per day. 
▶ An human is of medium size. 
 
STARTING CORE ATTRIBUTES: 
Humans start with the following proficiency levels in their core 
attributes: 
▶ +1 Proficiency Level (Trained) in PER. 
▶ +1 Proficiency Level (Trained) in any one core attribute of their 
choice. 
▶ +2 Proficiency Levels (Journeyman) in any one core attribute of 
their choice. 
 
STARTING SKILLS: 
Humans start with the following proficiency levels in their skills: 
▶ +1 Proficiency Level (Trained) in any skill of their choice from 
the Human Skill Pool below. 
▶ +1 Proficiency Level (Trained) in any skill of their choice from 
the Human Skill Pool below. 
▶ +2 Proficiency Levels (Journeyman) in any skill of their choice 
from the Human Skill Pool below. 
 
HUMAN SKILL POOL 
▶ Athletics (Any) 
▶ Intimidate 
▶ Armour 
▶ Melee (Any) 
▶ Charm 
▶ Perception 
▶ Consume Alcohol 
▶ Perform (Any) 
▶ Deception 
▶ Pray 
▶ Gambling 
▶ Ranged (H-D or X-Bow) 
▶ Gossip 
▶ Stealth (Any) 
▶ Haggle 
▶ Survival 
 
STARTING GRIT AND GRIME LIMIT: 
Humans start with the following Grit Points and Grime Limit. 
▶ Humans begin with 3 Grit Points. 
▶ Humans begin with a Grime Limit of 8. 
 
STARTING LANGUAGES: 
Humans start with the following languages: 
▶ Common 
 
HERITAGE TALENT: RESOLUTE 
When a tie is rolled on the initiative die in combat, a character with 
this talent acts first. 
 
HERITAGE TALENT: RANDOM 
Humans begin play with one random talent from the Random Talent 
table below. 
 


 
9 
 
Careers
Careers are a character’s profession. As a character gains experience 
points and levels up, they gain talents. Whenever a character gains 
a talent, the must choose one from the talents available to their 
current career. Every career has Major and Minor skills. The Major 
Skill Training and Minor Skill Training talents available to all 
careers allow them to increase these skills by a certain amount. Each 
skill will list a maximum it can be increased by the Major and Minor 
Skill Training talents.  
 
Talents are special abilities, training and qualities that a character 
acquires as they level up in Mud, Blood & Rats and a character’s 
career determines what talents they may choose as they gain levels.  
Basic and Advanced Careers 
After the career’s name it will either have a level or ‘basic’ listed in 
parenthesis. A listed level means that this career is an advanced 
career. Advanced careers can only be entered from another career 
that lists this new career in its career exits. Similarly, the character 
must be of at least the level listed in parenthesis before they can 
move to this career. 
 
Basic careers do not have any level requirements and every basic 
career is considered an exit for every career (basic or advanced). A 
complete reference table of them can be found below:

Barber surgery is the art of healing. Barber surgeons are employed

while their methods vary, they are capable of solving many ailments.

Beggar (Basic) 
Beggars are amongst the lowest orders in society. With no home to 
go to, the swarms of panhandlers and vagrants pester passersby on 
the streets of large cities, hoping that they bestow a trifling coin or 
two. Beggars are largely seen as a moral and societal pest, a scourge 
upon the hard-working folk of society and are thusly ignored by 
many folk of the world.

Burgher (Advanced: Level 1) 
Burghers are full citizens of the nations in which they live. They 
comprise a stratum of merchants and shop owners and barkeeps 
comprising a growing middle-class within civil society. Burghers 
often work as low-ranking merchants and tradesfolk, mingling with 
both the upper and lower orders of society.

In the countryside beyond every large settlement one can find 
charcoal burners. Forming enormous well-crafted pyres and 
specially designed kilns, charcoal burners are specialists, turning 
timber into charcoal. They are necessary to ensure the continued 
warmth of homes and power of industry, especially in places where 
coal is hard to obtain.

Coachman (Basic) 
Coachmen are drivers of wagons and stagecoaches. The are often 
armed and ready with large, blunderbuss-type weapons that are used

find themselves protecting their cargoes from marauding bandits.

Peasant (Basic) 
Peasants make up an enormous part of the population, especially in 
agrarian regions. Most peasants work a variety of jobs that culminate 
in land management through farming, fishing, felling trees and 
raising of livestock. Peasants, despite their poor prospects for 
advancement, often live fulfilling and interesting lives.

35 
 
Skills
Below is a detailed list of all the skill descriptions used in a campaign 
of Mud, Blood & Rats. 
 
Animal Handling 
Animals are well-integrated into the lives and livelihoods of the 
common folk of the world. Whether it be using them as draft animals 
or raising them for food, the presence of animals within society is 
unavoidable. Animal Handling allows a character to calm a terrified 
horse, deal with a particularly savage hound or maintain a degree of 
separation when needing to slaughter the family pig at the start of 
winter. 
 
Armour 
Wearing armour is a highly involved affair and not only is its 
production a highly skilled and sought after ability, but getting the 
most out of the armour by its wearer is a skill in of itself. Armour 
allows a character to wear a greater variety of protective equipment. 
It is also used to allow a character to get into and out of particularly 
complex suits of armour.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Me? I never much liked 
halflings. It’s not so much the 
fact I have to keep me hand 
on me wallet, so much as I 
can’t stand the smell. Put 
some bloody shoes on! 
 
 
Ernst Reinnman, most 
prestigious fishmonger of the 
Kringeldorf Altstadt.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Athletics (Choose Type) 
Athletics is used to cover a variety of physical feats that a character 
may attempt. It is likely that without it, certain situations such as 
swimming across a river, may prove trivial for some characters but 
incredibly dangerous for others. 
 
It has several specialisms, as defined below: 
▶ Climb:  Used for scaling sheer surfaces such as rock faces, tower 
walls and trees. 
▶ Swim:  This allows a character to stay afloat and direct their own 
movement in open water. If a character has Untrained Athletics 
(Swim) then they are assumed to not be able to swim at all. 
▶ Throw:  Used for throwing spears, stones and other such missiles. 
 
Charm 
Charm represents a character’s ability to schmooze their way 
through difficult situations. It is a broadly defined type of charisma 
that can be used to influence others by making them feel good about 
themselves. Charm is the domain of conmen, seducers and 
salespeople the world over. 
 
 
 
Composure 
The world is fully of terrifying, inimical things. From mutilated 
corpses to having to face down a mutated, three-armed troll, all 
characters will need to keep their cool from time to time. 
Composure is a measure of a character’s self-control when faced 
with terror and sometimes even disgust. 
 
Consume Alcohol 
Consume Alcohol is used to resist the effects of alcoholic beverages. 
It may also be used to measure a character’s ability to imbibe more 
potent brews and narcotics at the game master’s discretion. When 
drinking alcoholic beverages, characters should use the rules found 
in the Taverns and Carousing section (p.115) of this book to 
adjudicate what happens to them. 
 
Craft (Choose Type) 
Craft is a specific knowledge required to make something out of raw 
materials. Many careers rely on Craft in order to make their living 
as tradesfolk. Specialisms include Armour, Brewing, Carpentry, 
Cookery, Jewellery, Weapons, etc. 
 
Deception 
Deception is a character’s ability to lie believably. It is incredibly 
useful in certain situations but when detected will often cause 
offence or worse depending on the situation. 
 
Dodge 
Dodge is a martially trained ability to avoid incoming blows. It is 
particularly useful against attacks that deal incredibly high damage 
and would otherwise be best avoided entirely, regardless of the 
armour being worn. 
 


Skills 
36 
 
Drive (Choose Type) 
Drive is the ability to control and direct a vehicle of some kind. It 
has several specialisms, as defined below: 
▶ Riding: This allows a character to direct a creature from the 
saddle, such as riding a horse. 
▶ Cart: This allows a character to direct a horse-drawn cart, carriage 
or similar vehicle. 
▶ Rowing: This allows a character to control smaller vessels such as 
rowboats. 
▶ Sailing: This allows a character control a large, sea-worthy vessel 
or particularly large river barges. 
 
Gambling 
A seedy way for a character to make a bit of extra coin in towns, the 
Gambling skill is used to play dice, card, and other wager-based 
games. 
 
Gossip 
Gossip is a catch-all skill for carousing and chatting with locals. It 
can be extremely useful for helping investigate mysteries, follow up 
rumours and find new opportunities for work. 
 
Haggle 
Haggle is the ability to discuss and negotiate sales and purchases 
with merchants. It can be useful to ensure the character receives the 
best price for treasure they are selling. When the skill is used in this 
way, it is usually an opposed haggle vs haggle test. 
 
If a character successfully haggles, then the purchase price of an 
item should be reduced by 1d10% x the character’s proficiency levels 
in haggle. The same goes for increasing the sale price of an item. 
 
Healing (Choose Type) 
Healing is a difficult business. Not only is it treating wounds caused 
by weapons, bites and other such dangers, but it is also used to lance 
boils, treat fevers and minister to the chronically ill. There are two 
specialisms of Healing, as detailed below: 
 
▶ First Aid: This is the practical application of aid to a wound. A 
character may spend 2FP to test Healing (First Aid) on another 
creature. If the test is a success, then the creature gains 2HP. A 
creature can only receive this form of first aid once per day. A 
character may also test Healing (First Aid) to remove a bleeding 
condition from a creature, at the cost of 1FP per condition removed.  
▶ Surgery: Bones break, teeth rot, arrows break off in wounds. 
These are but a few things that a character may require surgery for. 
Surgery is a difficult process, but certain brutal and malingering 
injuries will not get better until they have been treated with a 
successful Healing (Surgery) test. 
Intimidate 
Intimidate is a skill that relies on an imbalance of power and a 
general malice to force people to do what you want. Often preening 
nobles, officious, high-ranking servants and even tavern thugs rely 
on this skill. 
 
Language (Choose Type) 
A character is assumed to be able to speak their own language 
fluently. However, this skill is used to learn and speak other 
languages within the game world.  
▶ Untrained: Cannot understand the target language in any way. 
▶ Trained: May be able to speak the language and read with some 
difficulty (provided they can read at all). It is obvious to any native 
speaker that this individual cannot speak the language fluently. 
▶ Journeyman: Can speak the target language and read it with some 
fluency, though with a pronounced foreign accent. They will struggle 
with academic forms and vocabulary of the target language.  
▶ Expert: Can speak the target language and read it fluently, 
including at an academic level. It will not be obvious to everyone 
that it is not their first language, though traces and hints of the accent 
may remain in certain circumstances. 
▶ Master: Can speak the target language and read it fluently, 
including at an academic level. They will speak with a perfect local 
accent.  
▶ Grandmaster: Can speak the target language and read it fluently, 
including at an academic level. They will speak with a perfect local 
accent and their vocabulary and expression exceeds that of most 
native speakers. 
 
Lore (Choose Type) 
Lore is the specific technical information of a precise field of study. 
In many ways, a Lore specialism could be anything from collectable 
coins to advanced metallurgy. Specialisms commonly include 
Herblore, Nature, History, Magic, Mysticism, Religion or a region 
within the game world. 
 
Melee (Choose Type) 
Melee is the ability to fight in hand-to-hand combat against similarly 
armed opponents. It is a core skill for adventurers who find 
themselves in fights a lot. It has three specialisms: 
▶ Brawl: This allows a character to fight with their bare hands. 
▶ One-Handed (1-H): This allows a character to fight with one-
handed weapons such as clubs and swords. 
▶ Two-Handed (2-H):  This allows a character to wield larger two-
handed weapons such as zweihänders and halberds. 
 
Pathfinding 
Pathfinding is the process of making trails, reading maps and find 
a route through difficult wilderness places such as bogs and jungle. 
It may also be used in dense urban environments to orientate 
oneself.  
 
Perception 
This is a mixture of a character’s ability to spot hidden objects, listen 
in on conversations and examine an area for clues. Perception is 
used when a character wishes to search a place or notice danger. 
 
Perform (Choose Type) 
Perform is the trained ability to complete a task, often in a way that 
is a spectacle, such as public speaking, dancing or sword-
swallowing. It may also be used for various other tasks that rely on 
a blend of taught expertise and natural aptitude, such as comedy or 
torture. 
 
Persuasion 
Persuasion broadly represents a character’s ability to charm, 
influence or otherwise convince a person into doing what the 
character wants them to do. It might be to waive a toll fee or 
convince a lord to evacuate his people in the face of imminent 
danger. 
 


Skills 
37 
 
Pray 
The gods are real, or at least some of them are and Pray is how a 
mortal interacts with them. A pray test may be used by a character 
to gain an insight from their patron deity about one thing that 
troubles them.  The Invocations and Blessings section (p.64) of this 
book details praying in more detail. 
 
Ranged (Choose Type) 
Ranged is the ability to use missile weapons such as bows, 
crossbows and throwing daggers against foes. It has the following 
specialisms: 
▶ Black Powder (Blckpwdr): This is the operational use of 
gunpowder weapons such as pistols, handguns and long guns. 
▶ Crossbows (X-Bow): This is the operation of crossbows and 
ballistae. 
▶ Hand-Drawn (H-D): More traditional ranged weapons such as 
longbows may be considered hand-drawn. 
▶ Mechanical (Mech): Weird alchemical and motorised weapons 
such as crank guns and dragon-breath cannons are considered 
mechanical. 
 
Set/Remove Traps 
This is the skill of laying and disabling of mechanical traps and 
devices. It may be useful when searching a mad wizard’s lair in order 
to locate the trigger or tripwire of a trap that will plunge the room 
into a conflagration, or it may be used to lay snares and bear traps 
by the characters to use against their enemies. 
 
Sleight of Hand 
This allows a character to pick a creature’s pockets, unlock doors 
with specialist tools and otherwise perform dextrous activities with 
their hands.  
 
Sorcery 
Magic is real as is the power it lends those who wield it. However, 
magic is also dangerous. Channelling and directing the flows of 
aetheric energy, a practice known as sorcery, is a gravely hazardous 
art that imperils a person’s soul. A higher proficiency in Sorcery 
allows a character to cast more potent spells The Magic and Spells 
section (p.68) of this book details spellcasting in more detail. 
Status 
Status is a character’s relative wealth and social standing. It can be 
used by a noble character to force their way into places that they 
shouldn’t. It can also be used to bully peasants and command respect 
from your lessers. If a character works for a week, the income they 
receive is based on their status. During this week, a character does 
not pay for living costs, as this is calculated to be included in this 
work: 
 
STATUS EARNINGS 
Status 
Money Per Week 
U 
1d6 pfennigs 
T 
4d6 pfennigs 
J 
1d6 shillings 
E 
4d6 shillings 
M 
2 gold pieces 
G 
1d6+2 gold pieces 
 
Stealth (Choose Type) 
Stealth allows a character to slip from view of most enemies, 
meaning they can move or attack stealthily. Attacks may reveal the 
character at the discretion of the game master. Stealth can also be 
used to sneak in and out of places where characters should not be. 
It has the following specialisms: 
▶ Urban: This is the process of hiding in urban environments such 
as city and towns. 
▶ Wilderness: This is the process of hiding in wilderness 
environments such as woods and mountains. 
 
Survival 
Survival is the ability to eke out an existence in inhospitable places. 
It will never furnish a character with food but might prevent them 
from freezing to death or dying of thirst. It can be used to scout a 
suitable place to rest.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


 
38 
 
Talents
Talent Descriptions 
Below is a detailed list of all the talents available to player characters. 
All talents are either passive, meaning they are always working or 
they are active, which means they require the character to activate 
them. If they are active, the talent will list which step in combat they 
may be activated. A talent will also tell you the maximum number of 
times it may be taken by a character. 
 
Above the Rabble 
PASSIVE 
MAX: 1 
▶ Once per day the character may spend 3FP to ignore the cost of 
one transaction worth up to 1gp. In narrative terms, the money is 
still spent, but the character’s recorded money on their character 
sheet does not decrease. 
▶ The character also gains +1 proficiency level in a Minor Skill of

Battle of Wills (Type) 
ACTIVE (OTHER ACTIONS STEP) 
MAX: Unlimited (One Per Type) 
▶ The character may attempt to cow an opponent into submission 
by a show of aggressive, dominating willpower. As an action the 
character may spend 4FP and lock eyes with a target they can see. 
The character then rolls an opposed Intimidate vs PER test. On a 
success, the target immediately calms and understands the character 
is the more powerful creature in the dynamic. If the character fails 
this test, the target cannot be subject to Battle of Wills again this 
day. 
▶ If the character succeeds the Intimidate vs PER test they may 
spend an additional 2FP to give the target a frightened condition. 
▶ This talent will always have a type of creature listed after it. The 
talent only works on this type of creature, with common types 
explained below: 
Types of Creatures 
▶ Animals: Wild animals, not monsters. This type is usually listed 
under the creature’s statblock. 
▶ Criminals: Members of the criminal class – everyone from bandits 
and highwaymen to smugglers and corrupt officials. 
▶ Guilds: Any humanoid living within society that is of the middle 
classes, usually of Journeyman and Expert Status.  
▶ Heretic: Heretics are those people who have turned from the 
prevailing religion of the game master’s setting.  
▶ Peasants: Any humanoid living within society that is of the lower 
classes, usually of Untrained and Trained Status.  
▶ Monsters: Monstrous creatures. This type is usually listed under 
the creature’s statblock. 
▶ Mutants: Any creature that has a mutation of some kind.  
▶ Nobility: Any humanoid living within society that is of the middle 
classes, usually of Master and Grandmaster Status.  
▶ Spellcasters: Any creature that can cast a spell.  
▶ Vermin: A particular type of animal or monster that composes 
leeches, ticks, rats and other unsavoury things. This type is usually 
listed under the creature’s statblock.

other holy men and women.

classes, usually of Master and Grandmaster Status.

▶ When the character should gain a physical mutation, reroll the d6 
used to determine whether the mutation is physical or mental, 
accepting the second result. 
▶ The character also gains +1 proficiency level in a Minor Skill of 
their career, up to the career’s limit. 
 
Synthesise Clues 
PASSIVE 
MAX: 1 
▶ The character may now burn FP on Perception tests. 
▶ The character also gains +1 proficiency level in a Minor Skill of 
their career, up to the career’s limit. 
 
Take Them Alive 
PASSIVE 
MAX: 1 
▶ The character may spend 2FP when making an attack test to 
automatically hit one of the target’s legs or arms (attacker’s choice) 
without having to make a hit-location roll. 
 
Targeteer 
PASSIVE 
MAX: 1 
▶ When the character’s side has the initiative in combat, then the 
character automatically succeeds all ranged attack tests. 
 
The Final Enemy 
PASSIVE 
MAX: 2 
▶ The character now deals +1 damage with attacks made against 
verkin type creatures for each time this talent has been taken. 
▶ The character also gains +1 proficiency level in a Minor Skill of 
their career, up to the career’s limit. 
 
This Time Next Year 
PASSIVE 
MAX: 1 
▶ When the character burns FP on Haggle tests, they gain a +2 
bonus for every 1FP spent. 
Requirements and Restrictions 
The character must have the Wheeler and Dealer talent before they 
can take this talent. If they do not have the Wheeler and Dealer 
talent, then any opportunity in which they could gain This Time 
Next Year they may instead gain Wheeler and Dealer, even if it is 
not listed as a talent for their current career. 
  
Throw Voice 
ACTIVE (OTHER ACTIONS STEP) 
MAX: 1 
▶ The character may spend 2FP to ‘throw’ their voice up to 60’, 
making it seem like it is coming from a location other than 
themselves. 
▶ The character also gains +1 proficiency level in a Minor Skill of 
their career, up to the career’s limit.

PASSIVE 
MAX: 1 
▶ The gains +1 proficiency level in Survival and +1HP to their 
maximum.

PASSIVE 
MAX: 1 
▶ The character may now burn FP on Composure tests. 
▶ The character also gains +1 proficiency level in a Minor Skill of 
their career, up to the career’s limit. 
 
Urchin 
PASSIVE 
MAX: 1 
▶ The character may now burn FP on Stealth (Urban) tests.

is increased to +2 if this talent is taken a second time.

Whenever the character may take this talent, they may take the 
Accurate Strike or Brutal Smash talent instead, even if they are not 
listed as a talent for the character’s current career.

damage is increased to +2 if this talent is taken a second time. 
Special Quality 
Whenever the character may take this talent, they may take the

Whirlwind Blade 
ACTIVE (MELEE ATTACKS STEP)

▶ The character must be wielding a two-handed weapon to perform

The character must have the Cleave Through Foes talent before

PASSIVE

The character must have the Bushcraft talent before they can take 
this talent. If they do not have the Bushcraft talent, then any 
opportunity in which they could gain Wild Folk, they may instead 
gain Bushcraft, even if it is not listed as a talent for their current 
career. 
 
 
 


 
53 
 
Equipment, Gear and Services
Below is a guide to all of the items typically found in a campaign of 
Mud, Blood & Rats as well as full rules on how to use them in the 
game. 
 
Coins and Money 
Coins are minted by the various kingdoms and financial institutions 
of the world. They follow an imperial system and most people run 
their lives in pfennigs and shillings, with the gold piece really only 
used by the wealthiest in society. 
 
In Mud, Blood & Rats there are three main types of coin: 
 
▶ A pfennig (d) is worth 1/12 of a shilling and1/240 of a gold piece. 
▶ A shilling (s) is worth 1/20 of a gold piece. 
▶ A gold piece (gp). 
 
A coin or jewel, such as a diamond, can be stacked into groups of 
100 per carry slot of encumbrance. 
 
Reading Costs 
As stated above, most transactions within the game will happen on 
the scale of shillings and pfennigs. Thusly, often prices are given as 
a pair of numbers split by a parenthesis. If this is the case, the cost 
of an item is listed as the shillings (s) first, followed by the 
pfennigs (d) second. For example, an item with a listed cost of 2/6 
means the item costs two shillings and six pfennigs. Items that are 
given in gold pieces (gp) will be listed as such. 
 
 
 
Rarity and Locating Merchants 
Some goods and services are easier to find than others. When in a 
settlement and searching for a particular item to purchase, a 
character should make a Challenging (+0) Haggle test to 
determine whether they can find a supplier of the item or not. This 
is not a negotiation of price, which may happen as a result of 
further Haggle tests. In place of Haggle, a Hard (-3) Gossip test 
may be made. 
 
There are certain modifiers to the test, based on the rarity of the 
item and the settlement size in which it is being looked for, based 
on the table opposite: 
 
 
 
 
ITEM RARITY AND SETTLEMENT SIZE 
Rarity/Settlement Size 
Haggle Test Mod. 
Common Rarity 
+0 
Uncommon Rarity 
-3 
Rare Rarity 
-6 
Exotic Rarity 
-9 
Settlement is a single castle or outpost 
+0 
Settlement is a village 
+3 
Settlement is a town 
+6 
Settlement is a city 
+9 
Lodgings and Services 
Living Costs 
All characters must play living costs. The rules below determine the 
cost of specific items and services. In general, however, a character 
must spend the following on their living costs, based on their status:  
 
LIVING COSTS TABLE 
Status 
Cost/Day 
Untrained 
1d x half character level 
Trained 
3d x half character level 
Journeyman 
6d x half character level 
Expert 
1s x half character level 
Master 
2s x half character level 
Grandmaster 
3s x half character level 
 
This useful abstraction helps game masters calculate quickly how 
much a player character should be spending. 
 
The justification for the increase with level is that while characters 
start out inexperienced, ill-equipped (and poor), they eventually 
become more competent, successful (and rich). A level 1 character 
who specialises in fighting is going to be paying for cheap ale and 
will be sleeping in common rooms. They will also probably clean 
their armour and maintain their weapons themselves. A similar 
character who is level 5 will be consuming better ale, eating better 
food, sleeping in better lodgings and might even be paying a retainer 
to maintain their adventuring equipment. As characters gain more 
experience and money, it’s only natural that they generally expect 
more, and therefore their living costs increase. 
 
This is also a useful way for a game master to maintain a healthy 
way of slowly draining a party’s funds, giving them reason to 
continue adventuring.

▶ Arrow, Quiver. A quiver containing arrows. Can be used as 
ammunition for bows. They have a d6 as the ammo die. 
▶ Backpack. A leather backpack that can be worn without issue. 
Provides 4 extra carrying capacity slots without taking up one itself. 
▶ Bedroll. A bedroll with padded lining and a pillow. Can be used 
to provide some comfort when out adventuring. 
▶ Bolt, Quiver. A quiver containing bolts. Can be used as 
ammunition for crossbows. They have a d6 as the ammo die. 
▶ Career Kit. A career kit is a set of tools commonly used in a career. 
For a barber surgeon is may be scalpels and twine, for a rat catcher 
may be small vermin traps, etc. 
▶ Clothing. Ordinary clothing worn by most people. 
▶ Clothing, Fine. Courtly clothing worn by nobles and other 
officials. Ostentatious and impressive. 
▶ Crowbar. Sometimes called a prybar, a crowbar is a hardened 
piece of steel that’s used to pry open doors and other sealed areas. 
▶ Grappling Hook. A three-pronged steel hook with a hoop at one 
end for a rope. Grappling hooks are useful when casting a rope up 
a cliff or other sheer surface. 
▶ Holy Water. A vial of water blessed by a priest. Holy water has 
many uses, but to certain monstrous horrors, such as vampires and 
werewolves, holy water has a caustic, deeply corrosive effect. 
 
 
 
 
▶ Lantern, Bullseye. Bullseye lanterns are very useful as their light 
shines out in a 40’ cone from one side of the lantern. This cone can 
be shut off at a moment’s notice with an in-built sliding mechanism. 
A bullseye lantern consumes 1 pint of oil of every two hours (12 
turns). 
▶ Lantern, Hooded. Hooded lanterns are used to create light that 
shines out in a 20’ radius from all sides of the lantern side of the 
lantern. This light can be shut off at a moment’s notice with an in-
built sliding mechanism. A hooded lantern consumes 1 pint of oil of 
every two hours (12 turns). 
▶ Oil. A single pint of oil can be used to fuel a lantern. It may be 
spilled on the ground or used to cover a weapon. Lantern oil is 
flammable (though not particularly explosive). 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
v 
Keep yer hands to yerself while you’re 
lookin’ in my shoppe! I am a great 
purveyor of many things – you after the 
vampire in the woods? Well I gots the 
garlick you need. Wantin’ oil to go into 
the tunnels beneath the burg? Look no 
further. And remember, all o’ this can be 
purchased at a fair and reasonable price, 
see! 
 
 
Old Gertrud, shoppekeeper on 
the Geltplatz 
 
 
 
▶ Pole, 10’. A ten-foot pole is incredibly useful. It can be used to 
vault over pits, probe for traps or any other multitude of activities. 
▶ Powder and shot. Powder and shot for blackpowder weapons. 
They have a d6 as the ammo die. 
▶ Rations. A mixture of hard tack, cheese, oatmeal and salted meat. 
A character can sustain themselves off a single ration per day. 
▶ Rope (Hempen). A hemp rope is study and has many uses such 
as in climbing, tying up prisoners or swinging from roof beams. 
▶ Rope (Silk). A silk rope has all the functionality of a hemp rope 
but is much, much stronger. 
▶ Sack (Small). A cloth bag that can hold up to 4 carrying capacity 
slots worth of items. However, when it contains any items, it must 
be carried with two hands. 
▶ Sack (Large). A cloth bag that can hold up to 8 carrying capacity 
slots worth of items. However, when it contains any items, it must 
be carried with two hands. 
▶ Sack (Large). A cloth bag that can hold up to 8 carrying capacity 
slots worth of items. However, when it contains any items, it must 
be carried with two hands. 
▶ Tent. A large tent used in camping to protect individuals from the 
harshness of weather. Up to two people may sleep in a single tent. 
▶ Tinder Box. A small wooden case containing dried tinder as well 
as a piece of flint and striking steel. Can be used to start fires. 
▶ Torches. A length of wood crowned with fabric soaked in pitch. 
When lit, torches provide a light in a 30’ radius and burn out after 
one hour. 
▶ Waterskin. A treated leather bag which serves as a vessel for 
water. Extremely useful when travelling in arid climes. 
 
Ammunition and Ammo Dice 
Tracking every single crossbow bolt and arrow can be onerous. 
Therefore, you do not track ammunition per shot, instead after every 
combat where the character discharged a shot with the weapon, you 
should roll the ammunition dice type for the specified ammunition. 
This is given in parentheses next to the ammunition. A natural 1 
indicates that quiver of ammunition has run out.

Poultices and Poisons 
Within the known world there are all sorts of poultices that can be 
used to cure illness and heal wounds. Conversely, there is an equal 
number of malign poisons that can destroy the body. 
 
Poultices 
The below table contains poultices available to characters. Poultices 
are not the magical potions available in other games but instead 
careful mixtures of mundane ingredients that produce wondrous 
effects. The Craft (Medicine) skill is required to make poultices, 
with the creation time, ingredient cost, ingredient rarity, and 
proficiency level required to create the poultice listed below.

▶ Antivenin. A mixture of herbs and neutralising salts prepared to 
render any poison harmless. Can be applied to a poisoned wound 
or drunk. 
 
Creation Time: two days. 
 
Ingredients Cost: 2s-10s (GM choice). 
 
Ingredient Rarity: Rare-Exotic (GM choice). 
 
Craft (Medicine) Prof to Create: Master. 
 
▶ Droplets of Purity. A small elixir that purifies a pint of brackish, 
undrinkable water when dripped in. A single set of Droplets of 
Purity has enough drops for 6 uses. 
 
Creation Time: half an hour. 
 
Ingredients Cost: 3s. 
 
Ingredient Rarity: Uncommon. 
 
Craft (Medicine) Prof to Create: Expert. 
 
▶ Hair of the Black Dog. A foul-smelling brew that causes sudden 
vomiting, sweating and crying. This continues for ten minutes, 
causing the drinker 1 poison damage. At the end of it, the effects of 
any hangover they are suffering instantly vanish. 
 
Creation Time: one day. 
 
Ingredients Cost: 4s. 
 
Ingredient Rarity: Rare. 
 
Craft (Medicine) Prof to Create: Master. 
 
▶ Lachryma Somni. A milky white liquid that helps people sleep 
and, if administered in high enough doses, numbs all pain. Too 
much can push the imbiber’s body into a mortally relaxed state close 
to (or even achieving) death. 
 
Creation Time: two days. 
 
Ingredients Cost: 1gp. 
 
Ingredient Rarity: Rare. 
 
Craft (Medicine) Prof to Create: Master. 
 
▶ Poultice, Healing. A non-magical brew of herbs that restores 
1d4+1HP when drunk. 
 
Creation Time: half an hour. 
 
Ingredients Cost: 2s. 
 
Ingredient Rarity: Common. 
 
Craft (Medicine) Prof to Create: Journeyman. 
 
▶ Poultice, Respite. A non-magical brew of herbs that restores 
1d4+1FP when drunk. 
 
Creation Time: half an hour. 
 
Ingredients Cost: 2s. 
 
Ingredient Rarity: Common. 
 
Craft (Medicine) Prof to Create: Journeyman.

▶ Adder Venom. The venom milked from various adders and 
snakes. If ingested, it inflicts 4 poisoned conditions. If it is smeared 
on a weapon which is used to attack a foe, it inflicts 2 poisoned 
conditions. Adder venom may have both a taste and scent, revealing 
its presence, depending on the adder it comes from. 
 
Creation Time: one day. 
 
Ingredients Cost: 10s (GM choice). 
 
Ingredient Rarity: Rare-Exotic (GM choice). 
 
Craft (Poison) Prof to Create: Journeyman. 
 
▶ Blindworm’s Sting. Blindworms can cause nauseating exhaustion 
if their venom is ingested. If ingested, it inflicts 4 drained conditions. 
If it is smeared on a weapon which is used to attack a foe, it inflicts 
2 drained conditions. Blindworm’s sting has no scent but a bitter, 
acrid taste. 
 
Creation Time: one day. 
 
Ingredients Cost: 1gp. 
 
Ingredient Rarity: Rare. 
 
Craft (Poison) Prof to Create: Master. 
▶ Burblefrog Scrape. Burblefrogs are found deep within far-off 
jungles. Scraping the slime from their backs produces a narcotic 
poison that induces terrifying hallucinations. If ingested the 
consumer must make a very hard (-6) save vs END or gain a 
frightened condition that lasts for 1 hour. If it is smeared on a 
weapon which is used to attack a foe, the target must make a hard 
(-3) save vs END or gain a frightened condition that lasts for 10 
minutes hour. Burblefrog scrape is flavourless but smells strongly of 
sweet petrichor. 
 
Creation Time: one day. 
 
Ingredients Cost: 1gp. 
 
Ingredient Rarity: Exotic. 
 
Craft (Poison) Prof to Create: Master. 
 
▶ Lotus Venom. Lotus plants are often the source of strange and 
exotic poisons, many of which are highly dangerous. If ingested, it 
inflicts 10 poisoned conditions. If it is smeared on a weapon which 
is used to attack a foe, it inflicts 4 poisoned conditions. Generally, 
lotus poisons are tasteless and scentless. 
 
Creation Time: one day. 
 
Ingredients Cost: 4gp. 
 
Ingredient Rarity: Exotic. 
 
Craft (Poison) Prof to Create: Grandmaster. 
 
▶ Spittlefish Extract. Spittlefish are a large variety of fish species 
that are covered in venomous spines. If ingested, it inflicts 6 
poisoned conditions. If it is smeared on a weapon which is used to 
attack a foe, it inflicts 3 poisoned conditions. Spittlefish extract may 
have both a taste and scent, revealing its presence, depending on 
the species of spittlefish it comes from. 
 
Creation Time: one day. 
 
Ingredients Cost: 2gp. 
 
Ingredient Rarity: Rare. 
 
Craft (Poison) Prof to Create: Expert.

Melee Weapon Descriptions 
All melee weapons have a number of qualities, as explained below: 
▶ Cost: The cost of the weapon. 
▶ Damage: Damage caused by each successful hit of the weapon. 
▶ Stack: The number of this type of weapon that can be stacked 
per single carrying capacity slot. 
▶ Reach: How close the wielder must be to hit an enemy with this 
weapon. Note that 5’ means adjacent in all practical terms. 
▶ Traits: Any traits that the weapon has. The meaning of these 
traits is explained at the end of this section. 
▶ Rarity: The rarity of this item when used to determine its 
availability for purchase.

Ranged Weapon Descriptions 
All ranged weapons have a number of qualities, as explained 
below: 
▶ Cost: The cost of the weapon. 
▶ Damage: Damage caused by each successful hit of the weapon. 
▶ Range: The effective range of the weapon’s attacks. 
▶ Stack: The number of this type of weapon that can be stacked 
per single carrying capacity slot. 
▶ Mag: The number of shots in the weapon before it must be 
reloaded. 
▶ Shots: When the weapon is fired, this is the maximum number 
of shots that may be fired in a single action. Each shot requires a 
separate attack test. 
▶ Traits: Any traits that the weapon has. The meaning of these 
traits is explained at the end of this section. 
▶ Rarity: The rarity of this item when used to determine its 
availability for purchase.

Armour 
Below are the tables for armour. Every piece of armour takes up one 
carrying capacity slot.  
 
Armour Descriptions 
All Armour has a number of qualities, as explained below: 
▶ Cost: The cost of the armour. 
▶ Armour Save: The armour save afforded by wearing this piece 
of armour. 
▶ Locations: The locations of the body protected by wearing this 
piece of armour. 
▶ Traits: Any traits that the armour has. The meaning of these 
traits is explained at the end of this section. 
▶ Prof. Level: The proficiency level of the Armour skill required 
to wear this piece of armour.  
▶ Rarity: The rarity of this item when used to determine its 
availability for purchase.

Traits 
Armour Traits 
Each piece of armour has a number of traits as listed in its entry. 
These are detailed below: 
▶ Block: This piece of armour can be used to perform blocks. 
▶ Front-Facing: This piece of armour only protects the character 
from attacks in the front and side, not from the rear. 
▶ Under Armour: This piece of armour may be worn under any 
other piece of armour, granting a +1 bonus to all armour saves on 
the listed locations. 
 
Weapon Traits 
Each weapon has a number of traits as listed in its entry. These are 
detailed below: 
▶ Balanced: This weapon is curiously designed to allow its user to 
make the most of its weight. Instead of the STR melee damage 
bonus, attacks from this weapon may use the AGI missile damage 
bonus, even though it is a melee weapon. 
▶ Blast: This weapon’s target area is more than a single creature. 
When shooting this weapon, choose a point to shoot at and on a 
success all creatures within the listed Blast radius are hit on the 
body for the usual damage. Roll hit locations on each affected target 
as usual. 
▶ Charge: This weapon is meant to be used from the back of a 
mount. When it is used on a charging steed, the damage from this 
weapon is doubled. 
▶ Flamberge: When an enemy attempts to parry an attack from this 
weapon, they suffer a -3 to their melee test to parry. 
▶ Flexible: This weapon is designed to allow a person to use either 
one hand or two when wielding it. If two hands are used, the 
weapon gains the qualities listed in parentheses. 
▶ Lacerate: This weapon multiplies its damage by five against 
targets that use HP and not SP (structure points).  
▶ Offhand: This weapon is light enough that can be used in the 
offhand with another weapon. In this way, a character may dual 
wield weapons. 
▶ Parry: This weapon is partially designed to deflect blows. It may 
be used to perform parries. 
▶ Penetrate: This weapon penetrates through enemies, hitting those 
behind. Any enemy directly behind the target within the penetrate 
value of the weapon is also hit. 
▶ Pistol: This weapon acts as a pistol. A character may use their 
Melee (One-Handed) skill to make attack tests against creatures 
who are directly adjacent to them with this weapon. 
▶ Reload: This weapon requires time to reload. For each level of 
Reload, it takes an additional consecutive action to reload. 
▶ Rend: This weapon tears through armour. For each level of Rend, 
the target’s Armour saves are made a -1. 
▶ Scatter: When this weapon is thrown it lands regardless. If the 
attack test fails, then roll a d8 and use it to randomly determine the 
direction. It scatters 1d4 x 5’. 
▶ Siege: This weapon can deal damage to structure points of 
vehicles and buildings as well as normal to normal hp. 
▶ Two-Handed: This weapon is heavy and requires two hands to 
wield in combat. All weapons listed as melee (two-handed) 
automatically have this quality. 
▶ Unwieldy: This weapon is hard to move and aim. When aiming 
at anything capable of movement, the attack test suffers a -3 
penalty. 
 


Equipment, Gear and Services 
60 
 
Henchmen and Hirelings 
It can often be helpful in a game of Mud, Blood & Rats for the 
player characters to seek out the help of others. Characters may 
only have a number of hirelings equal to or less than their follower 
limit, determined by their PER proficiency level. This includes 
animals such as horses and small dogs (useful for ratcatchers!). 
These hirelings often take the forms of peasants, guards, hunters 
and other such characters whose statistics may be found in the 
Bestiary section. 
 
Approaching A Recruit 
A game master may wish to call for appropriate Gossip tests for a 
character to be able to locate a potential recruit. Obviously, if the 
hireling is an animal, it is simply purchased by the character. This 
system provides a procedure for hiring help. The recruiter should 
test Persuade to convince the target to join them. The Persuade 
test should be made with the appropriate modifiers, as based on 
the table below. This test modifier also becomes the hireling’s 
Loyalty Modifier (see below). 
 
RECRUIT OFFER/LOYALTY TABLE 
Offer to Hireling 
Modifier to Test  
Half a pfennig a day/ level of the hireling. 
-9 
1d a day/ level of the hireling. 
-6 
2d a day/ level of the hireling. 
-3 
3d a day/ level of the hireling. 
+0 
6d a day/ level of the hireling. 
+3 
9d a day/ level of the hireling. 
+6 
1s a day/ level of the hireling. 
+9 
 
It is perfectly possible that any such offer may be further 
moderated. The expectation a hireling does not contribute to 
combat would make it easier for a character to recruit them 
(usually a +6 bonus), though this does not apply to future loyalty 
tests. 
 
Animal Loyalty 
Animals do not have a loyalty rating as they are owned by the 
character and will (probably) not attempt to leave in the same way 
that other NPCs will. 
 
Loyalty Tests and Modifiers 
Each hireling has a Loyalty Modifier. This is the test modifier for 
the initial Persuasion test made to recruit the hireling. There are 
certain situations when a hireling should test their loyalty. To do 
this, the character who employs them should save vs PER, with 
the hireling’s Loyalty Modifier as a bonus or penalty to the save 
vs PER. If the loyalty test is failed, then the hireling chooses to 
leave the characters employ, at least for the time being. If the 
loyalty test is a success, then the hireling remains in the 
character’s employ. A loyalty test should be made in the following 
circumstances: 
▶ After significant danger: After a moment of significant peril, such 
as a particularly fraught combat or if the hireling is reduced to 0HP. 
This does not mean that a loyalty test should be made after every 
combat.  
▶ In response to maltreatment: If the hireling is mistreated by their 
employer, or their employer does not intervene if they are being 
mistreated by others, then a loyalty test should be made, with an 
additional -6 modifier. Examples of this include constant ridicule of 
the hireling or designating the hireling to be the person who opens 
every door in the dungeon. 
▶ After the completion of a quest or a period of significant 
imperilment: After a quest is completed a hireling may have 
thoughts as to doing something else with their time, especially if 
they had acquired some treasure of if the adventure involved a 
protracted and serious risk of harm or death.  
 
Hireling Experience and Levels 
As a hireling takes part in encounters, they will gain experience 
points. They count as a creature in combat in order to determine 
what their share of experience points should be.  
 
A hireling gains levels at the exact same rate as a player character, 
requiring the same amount of experience points for each level. A 
hireling that is higher than level 1 when hired starts with the 
minimum amount of experience points required to have achieved 
that level. Similar, a creature that is lower than level 1 when hired 
required 100xp to achieve level 1. 
 
As a hireling gains levels, their target numbers reduce, and their hit 
points increase, as based on the tables below:

Hireling Skills and Attributes 
As a hireling gains levels, they gain core attributes, major skills and 
minor skills. What a hireling gains each level is based on the table 
below. When a hireling gains a core attribute, they may put up 
any of their core attributes by one proficiency level. When they 
gain a minor skill, they choose one from the Minor skills of their 
current career to increase by one proficiency level (this is totally 
uncapped unlike a player character’s access to skills). When they 
gain a major skill, they choose one from the Major skills of their 
current career to increase by one proficiency level (this is totally 
uncapped unlike a player character’s access to skills). 
 
Hireling Careers 
A hireling has a career when they are hired just like characters do. 
This is decided upon by the game master. A hireling may change 
career whenever they level up. 
 
Animal Hirelings 
When an animal owned by the character gains a level they use all 
the normal information when levelling up. However, they do not 
have a career. Instead, use the animal major and minor skill 
pools below when needed.

62 
 
Trading and Mercantile
In a grubby fantasy game characters are often looking for their next 
shilling. It is quite possible that in such a campaign that the 
characters will eventually gain access to a wagon, river barge or even 
a sailing ship. With such a vehicle they could easily begin to engage 
in speculative trade within the known world and the burgeoning 
middle classes of such settings have grown fat off the increasingly 
organised mercantile systems. 
 
Speculative Trade 
The easiest form of trade that the characters may become involved 
in is speculative trade. This is where they buy cargo in one place 
as cheap as possible and then sell it elsewhere for the largest 
possible margin. This is what groups working for themselves rather 
than the large guilds tend to do. Those in the employ of guilds may 
of course simply freight cargo from one place to another for a fixed 
price, though it is far more like that your rag-tag group of grubby 
adventurers will be engaging in the former form of trade. 
 
There are many different types of cargo that characters may wish 
to buy and sell and it often found in the real world in many 
different measurements. Stone, wood, ale, wine, spices and any 
other commodities are often measured in using different systems 
but for the sake of clarity, all cargo in Mud, Blood & Rats is 
measured in tons.  
 
The Trade System 
A comprehensive system is provided for game masters wishing to 
adjudicate trade using the steps below: 
 
0. Obtain a Trade Charter 
Before a vehicle can be used to move goods for the purposes of 
trade it must be granted a trade charter within the realm or 
kingdom it intends to operate in. This is effectively a sort of license 
and is specific to the vehicle being used by the individuals to trade 
goods. A trade charter may be managed by the government or 
particularly influential guilds and obtaining a trade charter usually 
costs roughly one shilling per ton of cargo the owners wish to haul 
with the vehicle. For example, the owners of a river barge with 100 
tons of cargo capacity would usually have to pay 100 shillings (or 5 
gold pieces) to obtain a charter for their barge. Sometimes guilds 
may ask favours or assign small tasks before a charter will be 
bestowed and sometimes charters are only good for a fixed period 
time such as one year. Due to the one-off nature of obtaining a 
charter, this is step 0. 
 
1. Locate Available Goods and Seller 
One character attempting to find a seller from which to purchase 
trade goods should test Lore (Business). On a success a seller is 
located. This usually takes about half a day. On a failure, a seller 
cannot be found this day. If a seller is successfully located, the 
character immediately rolls on the Available Goods table below to 
determine how many tons of cargo are available to purchase. One 
roll is made, taking into account all the relevant trade tags. 
2. Determine Buying Price 
Once a seller has been located, the price of the cargo is determined 
by completing the following steps: 
2a. The base price of the cargo is listed below per ton. Multiply 
this by the number of tons available (or the amount the characters 
wish to buy.) 
2b. Modify the price by the percentage listed under the Buying 
Price Table, taking into account all the trade tags of the settlement. 
2c. This is further modified by the Haggle and Lore (Business) 
proficiency level of the main negotiating character with the 
modifier listed below on the Buying Price table. This is the final 
price. 
 
3. Locate Buyer 
Once the characters have moved the cargo to a different 
settlement, they may attempt to sell their goods. One character 
attempting to find a buyer to sell their goods to by testing Lore 
(Business). On a success a buyer is located. This usually takes 
about half a day. On a failure, a buyer cannot be found this day. 
 
4. Determine Selling Price 
Once a buyer has been located, the price of the cargo is 
determined by completing the following steps: 
4a. The base price of the cargo is listed below per ton. Multiply 
this by the number of tons available (or the amount the characters 
wish to sell.) 
4b. Modify the price by the percentage listed under the Selling 
Price Table, taking into account all the trade tags of the settlement. 
4c. This is further modified by the Haggle and Lore (Business) 
proficiency level of the main negotiating character with the 
modifier listed below on the Selling Price table. This is the final 
price. 
 
BASE PRICE TABLE
