Game Mastery 
84 
 
Grit 
Grit represents the bonds of fate working to champion a particular 
character. It is a natural force that flows towards the younger races 
such as humans more readily. A character never regains spent grit 
points, unless the game master wishes to award them for moments 
of absolute and astonishing heroism.  A character may spend Grit 
points in several situations, as defined below: 
▶ Pass a Test or Save: A character may spend a grit point to 
automatically pass a test or save they had to make. 
▶ Ignore a Critical Hit: A character may spend a grit point ignore 
the damage and all the effects of a critical hit. They may do this even 
after they have learned what the effects would be 
▶ Survive Death: A character may spend a grit point to not die when 
they normally would. In such a situation the game master is 
encouraged to work with the player to alter the fiction of the game 
world to ensure that the character didn’t really die. Maybe the orc’s 
blow missed the character’s skull or as they tumbled over the edge 
of the tower’s balcony, they realised a pillow merchant was moving 
his wagon beneath them. 
▶ Ignore a Mutation: A character may spend a grit point to not gain 
a mutation that they have just rolled. Their grime points still return 
to 0. 
 
 
 
 
Grime 
There are dark forces in the world, and they seek to gain the souls 
of mortals into their power. This is represented by a character’s 
grime points and grime limits. Some races are more naturally 
resistant to the inimical effects of grime. 
 
Gaining Grime Points 
There are two primary ways in which a character may gain a grime 
point: 
▶ Exposure to Grimy Forces: A game master may deem that a 
character exposed to withering magick or demonic forces must 
immediately save vs PER or gain a grime point (or a number of 
grime points).  
▶ Faustian Agreements: The world is a scary place and the 
murdering spirits that linger just beyond this world persistently 
tempt the good folk with aid in exchange for the acquisition of 
grime. A character may gain grime points instead of spending FP at 
any time. It may be the character is out of FP and needs to flip a 
test. In exchange for 1 grime point, they may flip the test. Grime 
can be gained this way instead of spending FP on anything the 
character may usually use FP for. 
 
Reaching the Grime Limit 
When a character reaches their grime limit, they must immediately 
make a hard (-3) save vs PER. On either a success or failure, 
reduce the character’s current grime points to 0. On a success, 
nothing happens. On a failure, the character gains either a mental 
or physical mutation. Roll 1d6. On a 1-3, the character gains a 
physical mutation. On a 4-6, the character gains a mental mutation. 
To see what mutation is gained, roll on the table below. A 
character may never flip or burn FP on this save vs PER under any 
circumstances. 
 
PHYSICAL MUTATION 
D20 
Result 
1 
The character grows goat horns. They grow at the same rate as their hair. 
2 
One of the character’s hands (determined randomly) becomes that of a small baby. This renders it useless in most situations such as 
those that involve holding items such as weapons or climbing ladders. 
3 
One of the character’s arms and attached hand (determined randomly) is transformed into an octopus-like tentacle. It can be used like 
a normal limb in most situations. 
4 
The character breaks out in weeping pustules that cause no damage but hurt horribly and never go away. They must save vs END after 
every extended rest or gain 2 drained conditions.  
5 
The character’s tongue becomes forked, like that of a snake. 
6 
The character grows hair everywhere. No part of their anatomy is left hairless. If completely shaved, roll a d6 after each extended rest. 
On a ‘1’ the hair has grown back. 
7 
The character’s skin becomes waxy and slightly translucent. In bright light it’s possible to faintly see the character’s organs through 
their skin and their blood moving in their veins. 
8 
The character’s feet become cloven hooves. 
9 
Roll a d6. A strange mouth that speaks in the same voice as the character appears on their body: 1) the sole of one of their feet; 2; the 
palm of one hand; 3) in one armpit; 4) where their naval was; 5) on their back; 6) on one buttock. 
10 
The character grows a long furry tail like that of a cat. 
11 
One of the character’s eyes becomes painfully enlarged. It becomes a lurid purple colour. The character can see the flows of magic as 
though they had the mage sight talent. 
12 
A disgusting stench like that of off milk mixed with pig swill emanates from the character. The game master should devise suitable 
situational penalties (and maybe even bonuses) to the character’s interpersonal skill tests. 
13 
The character’s tongue becomes that of a toad, which they can use to shoot up to three feet long. However, this tongue takes up space 
and they speak in a peculiar, slobbering accent. 
14 
One of the character’s arms and attached hand (determined randomly) is transformed into a crab-like claw. It can be used like a normal 
limb in most situations but cannot be used for fine manipulation such as writing or pulling a trigger. 
15 
A single large horn, like that of a rhinoceros, protrudes from the character’s head. 
16 
The character’s skin becomes a metallic colour. They gain a 6+ innate power save on all of their hit locations. If they already have a 
power save, then this mutation does not improve it. 
17 
Roll a d6. A third eye through which the character can see appears on their body: 1) the sole of one of their feet; 2; the palm of one 
hand; 3) on the back of their head; 4) where their naval was; 5) on their back; 6) in the centre of their forehead. 
18 
The character’s mouth becomes a series of ringed teeth like that of a lamprey. They may use this mouth to drink a person’s blood. 
19 
The character’s face is completely hidden by a single sheet of skin that grows over it. They can still see through this skin but cannot 
speak. 
20 
The character becomes corpulently fat. This unnatural weight can never be shifted. All creatures apart from ogres with this mutation 
half their move speed. 


Game Mastery 
85 
 
MENTAL MUTATION 
D20 
Result 
1 
The character becomes nihilistic and hateful. When they must help someone or something, they must make a hard (-3) save vs PER or 
completely ignore the request. 
2 
The character becomes addicted to arson. Once per day, if they are alone and with access to fire-starting equipment, they must make a 
hard (-3) save vs PER or start a fire. 
3 
The character develops an insane bloodlust. The character may never attempt to take prisoners once a fight has started. If they ever 
have prisoners under their power, they must make a hard (-3) save vs PER or attempt to kill the prisoners. 
4 
The character develops a penchant for sadism. Whenever the character finds themselves in a position of power over another creature, 
be it an animal, monster or fellow person, they must make a hard (-3) save vs PER or attempt to torture the unfortunate creature. 
5 
Roll a d6. The character develops an intense fear of a particular type of thing, immediately gaining a frightened condition in their 
presence: 1) cats; 2; dogs; 3) cattle; 4) fish; 5) mushrooms; 6) halflings. 
6 
The character is very gluttonous. They must always be using at least two of their carrying capacity slots to carry food. Whenever they 
are offered food or drink, they must make a hard (-3) save vs PER or attempt to consume the food/drink. 
7 
This character hates spending money, for any reason and at any time. Whenever a character is required to spend their own money, they 
must make a hard (-3) save vs PER or refuse to spend the money under any circumstances.  
8 
The character develops kleptomania. Whenever the character is in the presence of small, pocketable items that belong to others they 
must make a hard (-3) save vs PER or attempt to steal the item. 
9 
After each extended rest the character hard (-3) save vs PER or forget the events that occurred since the last extended rest. 
10 
The character believes they are a werewolf, transforming each night into their wolf form. This is not true. Each night the character must 
make a hard (-3) save vs PER or remove their clothes and rush into the nearest woodland, howling and baying at the moon. 
11 
The character is afflicted by horrible nightmares. They must save vs END after every extended rest or gain 2 drained conditions. 
12 
The character becomes paranoid that their own reflection intends to kill them and take their place. They will never under any 
circumstances allow themselves to see their own reflection. 
13 
The character becomes an alcoholic to try and numb their growing madness. They must always be using at least two of their carrying 
capacity slots to carry booze. Whenever they are offered booze, they must make a hard (-3) save vs PER or partake. 
14 
The character secretly believes one of their closest allies is working against them. Determine randomly from among the character’s 
travelling companions who is the subject of this paranoia. The character will never help them from now on. 
15 
The character becomes vaguely stupid. This may present as amiable confusion or general mooncalfism and imbecility. All of their Lore 
skills become Untrained and can never be improved. The same happens to their Intellect core attribute. 
16 
The character believes they are the rightful king/queen of wherever they are living. They act as though their Status is Grandmaster 
(even if it isn’t). The character must make hard (-3) save vs PER in all social situations or assert this delusion as self-evident truth. 
17 
The character suffers from narcolepsy. Whenever they critically fail a test or save then they must immediately make a very hard (-6) 
save vs END or fall asleep immediately.  
18 
The character develops a lecherous streak. Whenever they find themselves in the presence of an NPC they find even vaguely attractive, 
they must make hard (-3) save vs PER or bring all of their charms to bear as best they can, regardless of the social consequences. 
19 
The character believes that they are in fact one of their own companions (determine randomly). They believe that their companion is a 
doppelganger attempting to replace them. They will try to destroy this threat to their person at the right time as to not draw attention. 
20 
The character becomes agoraphobic. Before going outside, they must make hard (-3) save vs PER or refuse to go outside. 
Dissolution of Mind and Body 
The body can only take so many mutations before what is left of 
the original person is gone. There is a reason the wild and 
forgotten places of the world are full of such miserable mutated 
creatures – those men and women whose bodies and minds have 
been so totally ruined by grime that they can hardly be considered 
people at all. Mutants are roundly and (mostly) justifiably hated by 
the good folk of the world due to their capricious nature and the 
undeniable mark of corruption that defines each and every mutant. 
 
If a character ever gains more than four physical mutations or six 
mutations of any sort, then their story is over: they are a gibbering 
mess whose very souls have been frayed and wrecked by the 
inimical nature of grime. Such a character becomes an NPC mutant 
under the control of the game master. If the character survives this 
initial moment of confrontation between themselves and the other 
characters then they should attempt to flee into the wilderness, 
seeking out other mutants to live with. 
 
Elves have the benefit of never accruing physical mutations, 
meaning that as their minds warp and begin to disintegrate, they 
can often exist within society for longer before their communities 
have realised what is happening. 
 
Many games do not have the degeneration of a character into a 
monstrous NPC as even a possibility. You are cautioned to remind 
the players that Mud, Blood & Rats is not standard high fantasy fare 
and that over-reliance on their ability to take Grime points to 
reroll tests and saves may prompt a swifter end to their characters 
than then they really desire. 
 
 


Game Mastery 
86 
 
Time 
Time in game is particularly important. Several units of time are used 
to measure the progress of the party and the use of certain abilities. 
Mud, Blood & Rats commonly uses the following units of time: 
▶ Instantaneous: If a spell or other action is listed as happening 
instantaneously, it means it happens immediately, without delay. 
▶ Round: A round represents 1 minute. It is a common measure of 
action-usage in combat. 
▶ Turn: A turn represents 10 minutes of in game time. It is assumed 
that most exploration within the game takes place in turns, and that 
most combats are completed within 1 turn. 
 
Resting 
To restore HP and FP, a character generally must be able to rest, as 
detailed below: 
 
Extended Rests 
An extended rest is usually an 8-hour period. During this time, it is 
assumed that a character has time to eat, drink, and sleep 
comfortably (perhaps in a bedroll, tent, or a ship’s stateroom). After 
an extended rest, a character receives 1 hit point provided they had 
access to rations. It is assumed that characters will generally be 
asleep for roughly 6 hours of the 8. A character may only take one 
Extended Rest per day. 
 
Short Rests 
A short rest is a bit of breathing space. It might be time to check 
armour dents, change swords, clean gun barrels and grab something 
to eat. A short rest takes 1 hour, after which a character regains all 
of their lost Fatigue Points. A character may only gain the benefits 
of one short rest per day. 
 
Encumbrance 
Encumbrance is a measurement of what characters can carry. 
Encumbrance is represented by slots. Almost all equipment and 
carried items take one slot. Some items, however, may be stacked 
into multiples of a single slot. For example, 100 coins may occupy a 
single carry slot. A general rule, items never take more than one slot.  
 
Optional Rule: Encumbered or Unencumbered 
For most campaigns, it may not be within the game master’s 
interest to have how much a character is carrying affect their other 
abilities. However, in some campaigns such as those that focus on 
survivalism or progression tied closely to items, it may be worth 
considering how much a character is carrying and how that affects 
their overall functionality. If that is the case, it is recommended to 
use the following rules: 
▶ Unencumbered: If a character is using half or fewer of their total 
carrying capacity slots, then they are considered unencumbered and 
behave normally. Backpack and other container slots are ignored 
when calculating this. 
▶ Lightly Encumbered: If a character is using more than half of 
their total carrying capacity slots but not all of them, then they are 
lightly encumbered. A lightly encumbered character reduces their 
movement by 33% (rounded down) but otherwise acts normally. 
▶ Heavily Encumbered: If a character is using all of their total 
carrying capacity slots then they are heavily encumbered. A heavily 
encumbered character reduces their movement by 66% (rounded 
down) and suffers a -3 penalty to all tests that require movement, 
and all attack tests. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Combat and Danger 
Combat is the cut and thrust of battle and proceeds as detailed here. 
Combat is split into a series of rounds (1-minute blocks of time) 
During a round of combat, each character can make one Move and 
one Action. 
 
Actions 
Actions are a number of things a character can do in combat, such 
as attacking with a sword, casting a spell, shooting a crossbow or 
closing a portal to the Plane of Shadows.  
 
The Combat Round 
A combat round is broken into the following steps: 
 
Combat Steps Each Round 
1. Initiative Step 
2. Move Step 
3. Missile Attacks Step 
4. Supernatural Abilities Step 
5. Melee Attacks Step 
6. Other Actions Step 
 
1. Initiative Step 
In this step, the players roll 1d6 and the game master rolls 1d6 for 
the enemies. The highest roll resolves their declared actions first 
(Steps 3 to 7), then the losing side does the same. A tie should be 
rerolled unless a character as a talent that affects this. 
 
2. Move Step 
Each creature may move up to its listed move value. 
 
3. Missile Attacks Step 
Weapons which use the ranged skill can now be used, such a bows, 
throwing daggers, and crossbows. 
 
4. Supernatural Abilities Step 
This is when characters use special actions that are supernatural. 
This might be a using an invocation or a wizard casting Arcane 
Arrow. 
 
5. Melee Attacks Step 
Melee weapons may now be used, such as swords and laser blades. 
It is also when natural weapons such as claws are most commonly 
used. 
 
6. Other Actions Step 
This is when actions such as drinking potions or administering first 
aid. A creature that took no other action may also move again, up to 
their listed move value. A creature that has not yet moved may do 
so now. 
 
 


Game Mastery 
87 
 
Attacks and Damage 
Below are the rules for making attacks and damaging opponents in 
a game of Mud, Blood & Rats. 
 
Making an Attack 
If a character has declared they are making an attack, then in the 
appropriate Combat Step they are able to do so. Resolve using the 
following: 
 
1. Make a Challenging (+0) test with the appropriate skill for the 
weapon (which is listed in the weapon entry) This is called the 
attack test. For example, for attacks with a club, this is the Melee 
(One-Handed) skill. 
 
2. If the attack test is a success, then the attack has scored a hit. All 
attack tests made against a character on 0HP are automatically a 
critical hit. 
 
3. Determine the location of the hit by rolling 1d12 and consult the 
table below. After a couple of combats, players will generally 
become capable of rolling their attack test and hit location roll at the 
same time. 
 
HIT LOCATION TABLE 
D12 
Location 
1 
Left Leg 
2 
Right Leg 
3 
Left Arm 
4 
Right Arm 
5-11 
Body 
12 
Head 
 
3. The target makes an armour save against the armour of the hit 
location by rolling 1d6 for each damage received and aiming to reach 
or beat the value of their armour. Each armour has a specific number 
that it also saves on, such as leather armour needing a 5+. This 
means a 5 or a 6 will result in a success. For each failed armour 
save, reduced the target’s hit points by one. Talents that affect saves 
do not affect armour saves, unless it is specifically mentioned. 
 
A natural roll of a 6 means that the strike has been deflected, 
regardless of any other modifiers. If a natural roll of 1 is made, then 
the damage is applied regardless of any other modifiers. 
 
Optional Rule: Simple Hit Locations 
Tracking the hit location of every single strike can be onerous at 
times. If game masters wish to forgo this extra step at the sacrifice 
of this granularity, then they may choose to. If they do this, then 
all hits are made against the body for the purposes of armour 
saves. Critical hits should still roll a hit-location. 
 
Disengages and Attacks of Opportunity 
When a creature is adjacent (within 5’) of another creature in combat, 
they can be assumed to be ‘engaged’. This means that if an engaged 
creature leaves this 5’ adjacency, then the creature they are moving 
away from may make a free attack test with a melee weapon. This 
is called an attack of opportunity. A creature may declare it is using 
its action for a round to disengage, which means it may move away 
without provoking an attack of opportunity, but then it may not 
use its action to do anything else. 
 
Size of Combatants and Creatures 
There are six different categories of size: tiny, small, medium, large, 
huge and gargantuan.  
▶ Tiny creatures take up a negligible amount of space and up to 
four tiny creatures can fit in a 5’x5’ area.  
▶ Small creatures are about the size of a child and take up 5’x5’ 
space. 
▶ Medium creatures are about the size of an adult and take up 5’x5’ 
space.  
▶ Large creatures take up 10’x10’ space. Huge creatures take up 
15’x15’ space.  
▶ Gargantuan creatures take up to or more than a 20’x20’ space. 
 
Rend and Power Saves 
Many weapons and creature attacks will list a Rend value, such as 
Rend (1). This is a negative penalty applied to armour saves 
(including cover saves). For example, if a character has a 4+ armour 
save and is struck by an attack that has Rend (1) then they would 
need to roll a 5+ to succeed. Remember, a natural 6 on an armour 
save always succeeds, regardless of other modifiers (and a natural 1 
always fails). 
 
Sometimes a creature will have access to a power save. This is a 
special type of save that works exactly like armour saves and is 
rolled in addition to armour saves. Any failed armour saves may 
be rerolled if the creature has a power save. Nothing ever modifies 
a power save under any circumstances, and therefore power saves 
cannot be affected by something like the Rend weapon quality. 
Sometimes a power, spell, attack or other actions states that the 
damage caused ‘ignores all armour saves’ – this does not ignore a 
power save. A character cannot ever benefit from more than one 
power save per point of damage sustained – they simply roll their 
best power save if they have multiple. 
 
Damage and Death 
When a non-player character is reduced to 0 hit points they are 
killed. This is slightly different for player characters. When a player 
character is reduced to 0 hit points, they immediately suffer a critical 
hit, and the attacker should roll on the appropriate critical hit table. 
All further hits on a character with 0HP count as critical hits. 
 
Sometimes an effect, such as a critical hit, may cause a character to 
be dying. A dying character is unconscious and must either be 
stabilised or healed to prevent their death. To stabilise a dying 
character, they must successfully be the subject of a Healing (First 
Aid) test. 
 
When a character is dying the player creates their death pool. This 
is a number of d6s equal to their proficiency level in END. At the 
end of each round, the player should roll their death pool of d6s 
and remove any natural 1s. Repeat this at the end of each round, 
removing the natural 1s each time. When the death pool is totally 
depleted and no dice remain, the character dies. If a character has 0 
proficiency levels in END (Untrained) then they have a death pool 
consisting of a single d4. What a character’s death pool should be 
is shown in the table below: 
 
DEATH POOL TABLE 
END 
Death Pool 
Untrained 
1d4 
Trained 
1d6 
Journeyman 
2d6 
Expert 
3d6 
Master 
4d6 
Grandmaster 
5d6 
 
Hit Locations for Non-Attack Damage 
When a character is subject to an attack test, a hit-location roll is 
made to determine where the character is hit and therefore which 
armour save is made. There are many effects that cause damage 
which do not specify which location is hit, such as deity disfavour 
tables and magical mishap tables. In such situations, the damage 
is applied as a hit against the body. 
 
 


Game Mastery 
88 
 
Critical Hits 
If the attack roll is a natural 20 then the hit scored is ‘critical’. The 
hit location is then determined normally, and the target immediately 
makes their armour saves as usual. The target suffers double the 
usual damage and then the attacker rolls on the appropriate table 
below for the hit location. If the target was reduced to 0HP then the 
‘excess’ damage that would have been left over once they reached 
0HP (if any) is added as a bonus to the below d10 roll. 
 
CRITICAL HIT: LEG 
d10 + Excess 
Damage 
Result 
1 
Agonising Smash: The target gains 4 stunned 
conditions as their leg is smashed. 
2-3 
Crumpled Toes: The target’s toes are crushed. 
They suffer 2 stunned conditions and may not 
move next round. The target permanently loses 1 
toe. 
4-6 
Dislocated Leg: The target’s leg is ripped from its 
socket and must be set with a healing (surgery) 
test. Until it is healed the character starts each day 
with 2 drained conditions and their movement 
speed is halved. It takes 3d10 days to heal once 
set. 
7-8 
Deep Cut: A dangerous injury is inflicted on the 
target which gains 2 stunned conditions and 
1d4+1 bleeding conditions. 
9-10 
Vicious Amputation: The target’s leg is shorn 
clean off. The target gains 1d4+1 bleeding 
conditions and 4 stunned conditions. 
11+ 
Spray of Gore: The target’s leg is torn off and 
blood explodes out in every direction. The target 
dies.

Game Mastery 
89 
 
Critical Fumbles 
When making an attack test it is possible to critically fumble. 
This is a special type of critical failure. When a creature rolls a 
natural ‘1’ on an attack test, immediately roll 1d10 on the table 
appropriate to the user’s weapon below. Note that there are no 
critical fumbles for natural weapons such as claws and teeth.  
 
CRITICAL FUMBLE: ATHLETICS (THROWING) 
D10

1

wielder trips and lands on their own weapon causing the 
full attack damage to themselves on a random hit location 
and causing a bleeding condition

hard object, bending it out of shape. If the weapon is 
non-magical, it is ruined. 
3

themselves. If the weapon causes piercing or slashing 
damage, then they gain a bleeding condition. If it causes 
bludgeoning damage, then they gain 3 stunned 
conditions. 
4-7

mistake. Choose the nearest friendly creature to the 
intended target. The attack hits them on a random hit 
location causing normal damage. 
8

shouldn’t, damaging it. Until it is repaired for a nominal 
fee, it causes -1 damage. 
9 
Tripped Over: The wielder trips, falling prone. 
10 
Loose Grip: The weapon slips in the wielder’s hand. No 
other issue emerges.

Dramatic Actions 
Some creatures have ‘dramatic actions’ listed in their statblock. A 
dramatic action does not cost a creature its action for that round – 
instead it is taken after any other step in the round (even after the 
enemy’s steps). Most dramatic actions are reserved for the most 
powerful of monsters. 
 
Resistances and Vulnerabilities 
▶ Resistance: If a creature has resistance to a damage type, then 
any damage it takes from an attack is halved (rounded down), to a 
minimum of one. For example, if a creature with resistance to 
slashing damage is hit for 2 points of slashing from a longsword, 
then 1 point of damage is ignored automatically. The creature then 
rolls its armour saves against the remaining damage. 
▶ Vulnerability: If a creature has vulnerability to a damage type, 
then any damage it takes from an attack is doubled. For example, if 
a creature with vulnerability to slashing damage is hit for 2 points 
of slashing from a longsword, then it must make its armour saves 
against 4 points of damage. 
 
Saves 
Oftentimes a character is called upon to make a save. This is almost 
always to avoid the effects of something unpleasant, such as a spell 
or trap. This will be stated as a ‘save vs’ and then a stated attribute, 
such as a ‘save vs PER’. A save is simply a test taken for that core 
attribute.  
 
Cover 
Sometimes a creature is in cover, granting an extra save to reduce 
damage if an attack hits. This save is taken before any armour saves 
or power saves and is referred to as a cover save. A cover save is 
affected by anything that affects an armour save and similarly 
always succeeds on a natural 6 and fails on a natural 1. 
 
To benefit from a cover save, a creature must be at least 50% hidden 
from the would-be attacker. Similarly, in most circumstances a 
creature only gains the benefit of a cover save during the Missile 
Attacks Step. The best cover save a creature can hope for is a 4+.  
 
It is ultimately up to the game master as to what constitutes granting 
cover save, and some examples are given below: 
▶ 6+: hedges, bracken, etc. 
▶ 5+: ruins, debris, carriage doors, etc. 
▶ 4+: stone walls, castle battlements, etc. 
 
Surprise 
Sometimes one side in combat has the element of surprise over 
another. It is also possible that both sides blunder into each other 
without warning. When this is the case, the members of the side that 
might be surprised (which could be both) should save vs INT. On a 
failure, they are surprised and can make no actions during the first 
round of combat, including responsive actions such as dodging and 
parrying. 
 
 


Game Mastery 
90 
 
Flanking 
It is easier to hit a creature that is surrounded. A creature counts as 
being flanked if it is being attacked from two opposing sides. 
Melee attacks made by these creatures against the flanked target 
gain a +3 bonus to their melee attack tests and their attacks deal 
+1 damage. A creature cannot flank a target that is more than one 
size category larger than itself. An example of what flanking looks 
like is shown below: 
 
 
In this example the orcs are flanking the PC as they are attacking 
from two opposing sides. 
 
 
Again, here the orcs are flanking the PC as they are attacking from 
two opposing sides. 
 
 
Here, the orcs are not flanking because, while there are two of 
them, they are not on directly opposite sides of their target. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Double Flanking 
Additionally, if a creature is flanked by two pairs of creatures 
simultaneously it is counted as being double flanked. Melee attacks 
made by these creatures against the flanked target automatically 
succeed their melee attack tests and their attacks deal +1 damage. 
A creature cannot flank a target that is more than one size category 
larger than itself. An example of what double flanking looks like is 
shown below: 
 
 
Here is an example of a double flank, as the PC is being flanked by 
two pairs of opponents simultaneously. 
 
Dodging 
Sometimes a character may wish dodge clear of an incoming attack. 
This is often better than taking the damage of very high-damage 
attacks such as those from handguns and the blows of terrifying 
monsters. Dodging works as follows: 
 
▶ Once the attacker has made a successful attack test, the defender 
may attempt a dodge test. This can be done against both melee and 
ranged attack tests. A success means the blow is dodged. A 
character may only dodge once per round. 
▶ If the defender fails their dodge test, the damage they would have 
suffered from the attack is increased by 3. 
 
Parrying 
Sometimes a character may wish to use their weapon to parry a 
blow. A weapon must have the Parry trait to do this. Parrying works 
as follows: 
 
▶ Once the attacker has made a successful attack test, the defender 
may attempt a melee test. If successful, the defender takes no 
damage. This can only be done against melee attacks. A character 
may only parry once per round. 
▶ If the defender fails their melee test, the damage they would have 
suffered from the attack is increased by 3. 
▶ A creature may only attempt to parry those attacks coming from a 
creature that is the same size category as themselves or smaller. 
 
Blocking 
Sometimes a character may wish to use their shield to block a blow. 
A shield must have the Block trait to do this. Blocking works as 
follows: 
▶ Once the attacker has made a successful attack test, the defender 
may attempt an Armour test (based on the Armour skill, not an 
Armour save). If successful, the defender takes no damage. This can 
be done against both melee and ranged attack tests. A character 
may only block once per round. 
▶ If the defender fails their Armour test, the damage they would 
have suffered from the attack is increased by 3. 
 
 


Game Mastery 
91 
 
Conditions 
There are different conditions that the characters and other creatures 
can accrue because of various actions and injuries. They are 
explained below: 
▶ Bleeding: For every bleeding condition, a creature loses 1hp at 
the start of each round. Abilities that restore HP may also be used to 
remove bleeding conditions. In addition, any creature on 0 HP 
suffers a cumulative 10% chance of dying at the end of each round 
for each bleeding condition they have. The creature then saves vs 
END for each bleeding condition, removing one condition for each 
success. This is handled separately to the death pool (and perhaps 
in addition to it if the character is also dying. 
▶ Blinded: Creatures that are at a -9 to all tests that rely on sight. 
▶ Burning: A creature is on fire. For every burning condition, a 
creature loses 1hp at the start of each round. The creature then saves 
vs AGI for each burning condition, removing one condition for each 
success. 
▶ Drained: A creature takes a -1 to tests for each drained condition. 
A creature naturally loses two drained conditions at the end of an 
extended rest period. 
▶ Drunken: A creature takes a -3 penalty to all tests that require 
movement or sight. However, they gain a +3 bonus to all composure 
tests and saves vs PER. 
▶ Frightened: A creature takes a -3 to all tests while they have this 
condition. They may not move towards any creature or object that 
gave them the frightened condition. At the start of each round, they 
may make a hard (-3) Composure test to remove a frightened 
condition. 
▶ Poisoned: A creature is poisoned. For every poisoned condition, 
a creature loses 1hp at the start of each round. The creature then 
saves vs END for each poison condition, removing one condition 
for each success. 
▶ Slowed: A slowed creature’s movement is reduced by half.  
▶ Stunned: A creature takes a -1 to all tests. A creature naturally 
loses one stunned condition at the end of the next round. 
 
Cones, Lines, Radii and Sweeps 
Some attacks are not simply determined as one creature and one 
target but may instead hit a group of creatures. This often the case 
with dragon’s breath attacks and most prominently by wizards 
casting spells. The terms Mud, Blood & Rats uses are explained 
below: 
 
Cones 
Some attacks and abilities, such as a wizard’s spell, may affect a 
‘cone’ of creatures. The number stated is the length the cone extends 
from the source, and it ends when it reaches the width stated. For 
example, a 15’ cone would look like this, with the wizard as the 
point of origin, assuming one ‘square’ on the area below is 5’: 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Lines 
Lines affect all creatures along a single trajectory and the listed 
distance is the length of the line and is commonly used for spells 
like lightning bolt. For example, a 20’ line with the wizard as the 
point of origin, assuming one ‘square’ on the area below is 5’ would 
look like this: 
 
 
 
Radius 
A radius affects a rounded area and is often used for thrown 
explosives and other grenade-like effects. A radius the size of the 
area from the centre point to the edge. For example, a 5’ radius on 
a 5’ grid would look like this: 
 
 
 
Sweep 
A sweep is a line directly adjacent to the creature and is often used 
when determine wide, cleaving swipes and arcs. If a creature can hit 
multiple targets with one melee attack sweep, then they must make 
an attack test against each. Two examples of a 15’ swipe with a 
fighter as the point of origin is shown below on the 5’ scale grid: 
 
 
 
 


Game Mastery 
92 
 
Dual Wielding 
Sometimes a character may wish to use two weapons at once when 
fighting in melee. At least one of the weapons must have the 
Offhand weapon trait. This works as follows: 
▶ Attacks with the weapon in the main hand are made as normal. 
▶ Attacks with the off-hand weapon cost 1FP to use. 
▶ Attacks with the off-hand weapon are made at a -3 penalty. 
 
Suffocation and Drowning 
When a creature has its airflow cut off it is suffocating. 
 
When a creature is suffocating create a pool of d6s equal to the 
creature’s proficiency level in END. This is known as their 
suffocation pool. At the end of each round, the suffocating creature 
rolls their suffocation pool of d6s and remove any natural 1s. 
Repeat this at the end of each round, removing the natural 1s each 
time. When the suffocation pool is totally depleted, the creature 
immediately drops to 0 hit points, is unconscious and considered to 
be dying. 
 
If a character has 0 proficiency levels in END (Untrained) then they 
still have a suffocation pool consisting of a single d4. 
 
Called Shots 
Sometimes a character may wish to shoot a particular part of their 
opponent, such as going for a head shot to kill a zombie or a leg 
strike to stop their opponent from fleeing into the night. We call 
these sorts of attacks ‘called shots’. Called shots work as follows:  
 
The attacker declares they are making a called shot, stating which 
part of the target they are aiming for. They also state how much of 
a penalty to the attack test they are willing to take in order to make 
the shot hit where they intend which is then applied to the attack 
test (before it is rolled).  
 
If the attack hits with the states penalty, the character immediately 
rolls their hit location d12 and adds the penalty they took on the 
attack test as a bonus to the d12 roll. They may choose to hit the 
location they rolled on the d12, or any other value lower than what 
they rolled. 
 
If the attack test is successful but the character does not hit the 
stated called shot location, the target gains a +1 to their armour 
saves against this attack. 
Unarmed Attacks 
When a character makes an unarmed attack, the damage is 1 
bludgeoning damage plus their Melee damage modifier (determined 
by their STR). However, a creature succeeds all Armour saves 
against this damage on a natural 4, 5 or 6, not just the usual 6. 
 
Siege and Lacerate Qualities 
Occasionally, a weapon will have the siege quality. This means that 
the weapon can deal damage to structure points of vehicles and 
buildings as well as to normal hp. 
 
The attacks of creatures that are huge or larger automatically have 
the siege quality. 
 
Often weapons that have the siege quality also have the lacerate 
quality. A weapon with the lacerate quality multiplies its damage by 
5 against creatures with hit points (as opposed to buildings and 
vehicles with structure points)  
 
Combat Manoeuvres 
It is impossible for a game master to consider every single possible 
action a character may take in combat. A wide array of actions are 
not covered succinctly by the rules, such as pinning a foe to the wall 
with an arrow; disarming an opponent; or jumping on a foe’s back. 
We call all these actions combat manoeuvres. If a character (either 
a player character or non-player character) wishes to perform a 
combat manoeuvre, use the following steps: 
 
1. The attacker makes a Challenging (+0) test with the appropriate 
skill for the weapon (which is listed in the weapon entry) This is 
called the attack test. They must state what their intended action is, 
such as disarming their opponent, gouging their eye, tripping them 
up. The target of the attack decides whether they want to take the 
described effect of the combat manoeuvre, or not. 
 
2. The attacker makes their attack test. If it is successful, then the 
target suffers the effects of the combat manoeuvre if they accepted 
it before, or they take normal weapon damage otherwise. 
 
By allowing the target to decide whether to take damage from the 
attack or a describe combat manoeuvre effect, it naturally balances 
out the intended effects amongst players and creatures (both players 
and foes) tend to accept more non-lethal combat manoeuvres the 
closer they are to death, as to avoid the damage a normal attack 
causes. 
 
 


Game Mastery 
93 
 
Composure 
Composure is the ability to withstand frightening or disgusting 
experiences. It is often used by game masters to determine when an 
NPC creature breaks from combat and attempts to flee. Composure 
is also used to resist gaining frightened conditions in the presence 
of particularly fearsome opponents. 
 
Composure Tests 
Each creature will have a composure proficiency level listed in its 
statblock. A composure test is made just like any other sort of test. 
On a failure the creature gains a frightened condition and will 
attempt to surrender, flee, or parley with the other combatants. If 
the creature passes its composure test, it will continue fighting. 
 
When to Test Composure in Combat 
It is ultimately up to the game master as to when composure should 
be tested. However, here are a few principles to help guide your 
decision making. 
▶ 50% casualties: When the opponent’s side has suffered 50% 
casualties, the game master should roll a composure test. A failure 
indicates that the remaining combatants on that side should attempt 
to flee or surrender. 
▶ Leader Killed: When a leader on a creature’s side has been taken 
as a casualty, the game master should roll a composure test. A 
failure indicates that the remaining combatants on that side should 
attempt to flee or surrender. 
▶ Lone Survivor: When a creature that was fighting as part of an 
enlarged group is the only active combatant left, the game master 
should roll a composure test. A failure indicates that the remaining 
combatant on that side should attempt to flee or surrender. 
 
Creatures with Different Composure Ratings 
Sometimes creatures in groups will have different composure 
ratings, such as a group of goblins being led by an orc skarred one. 
In these cases, if the game master is called upon to make a 
composure test, then they should use the highest composure rating 
amongst all the combatants on one side. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Experience 
Throughout the game, characters will gain experience points and 
levelling up. The manner in which characters gain experience can 
vary depending on the campaign. Below are a few general principles 
to help guide you. Characters should generally gain XP for the 
following: 
 
Interesting Ideas 
The game master should award XP if a player comes up with a 
particularly smart solution to a problem that is present. Use the 
miscellaneous XP table to help you work this out. 
 
Reasons to Award Experience Points 
From locating the entrance to the ruins, to negotiating a difficult 
meeting with a noble, there are many encounters and events that are 
deserving of experience points. A couple of less well-defined reasons 
a game master may wish to reward experience points for are: 
▶ Defeating Monsters and Enemies: The characters will often find 
themselves facing monsters such as undead, goblinoids and other 
such creatures. Encounters where they defeat, kill, or otherwise 
overcome these creatures are worthy of experience point rewards. 
▶ Social Encounters: Encounters where characters have to 
negotiate, persuade or intimidate NPCs might offer experience if the 
game master feels that the encounter should be justly rewarded 
(simply paying the tollkeeper to get into a city should not generate 
XP rewards). 
▶ Investigation: Encounters where characters must investigate or 
otherwise solve some sort of puzzle may warrant an XP reward. 
▶ Exploration: The discovery of major locations such as dungeon 
sites and settlements may warrant an XP reward. 
▶ Quest or Goal Completion: Completing a major quest (or even a 
minor one), or part thereof should be rewarded with XP. 
▶ Good Roleplaying: A character whose player leans into their 
virtue or vices, actively playing out that character’s beliefs should be 
rewarded. A low experience point reward is given for leaning into a 
virtue, while an average one is awarded for learning into a vice. 
▶ Achieving an Ambition: A character should receive a high 
experience point reward for achieving their ambition, at which point 
a new ambition should be chosen. 
 
For each of these types of encounters, game masters may refer to 
the Miscellaneous Experience Rewards table below.

Game Mastery 
94 
 
Evasion 
Sometimes one party in a fight may wish to flee and the other may 
wish to pursue. In these cases, a game master may use to following 
rules to quickly determine if the fleeing party escapes or not. During 
evasive action, it is assumed both sides are running as quickly as 
they can.

If a creature or party attempts an evasion test and fails, then the 
pursue catch up and threaten to overrun the pursued. Each member 
of the pursued party gains a drained condition in such a situation. 
Reaction Rolls 
Reaction rolls are used to help a game master decide how NPCs and 
wild creatures respond to the presence of characters. For example, 
this could be whether a dragon tries to kill a character or parley with 
a party first. It could, however, also be how a sage responds to the 
characters’ requests for help. A reaction roll is not always required – 
sometimes the contents of an adventure or the characters’ previous 
actions will overrule the need for a reaction roll. To make a reaction 
roll, roll on the following table: 
 
REACTION ROLL TABLE 
2d6 
Result 
2 
Critical Failure: The creature or NPC is incensed by 
the characters. It will do everything it can to harm 
them, both immediately and in the future. 
3-4 
Hostile: The creature or NPC feels a strong enmity 
against the characters. If it is socially acceptable (and 
sometimes even if it isn’t) then it will attack.  
5 
Threatening: The creature or NPC is generally hostile 
to the characters. It will retest next round with a -5 
penalty unless the characters do something to change 
this. 
6-8 
Cautious: The creature is unsure how to respond. It 
may attack, help or flee, but its response should be 
based on the character’s immediate actions. Retest 
again next round. 
9 
Curious: The creature or NPC is cautiously curious 
about the characters. It will retest next round with a +5 
bonus unless the characters do something to 
significantly change this. 
10-11 
Friendly: The creature or NPC feels a strong sense of 
affability towards the characters. If it is socially 
acceptable then it will generally do what it can to help 
the characters. 
12 
Critical Success: The creature or NPC feels that the 
characters have entered its life at just the right time. 
Both immediately and in the future, it will do its best to 
aid the characters however it can. 
 
Modifiers to Reaction Rolls 
Whichever main character is interacting with a particular creature 
(and we must appreciate as game masters that this may change 
from round to round), then certain modifiers based on PER come 
into play. A character should gain a bonus to reaction roll equal to 
their proficiency level in PER. 
 


 
95 
 
Vehicles and Structures 
Vehicles and structures will play a significant role in many 
campaigns by game masters as they cover everything from dwarfen 
steam machines to northmen longships, from mud huts to castle 
walls.  
 
Structure Points 
Structure points (abbreviated as SP) replace hit points for vehicles 
and structures. A structure point is not harmed by regular damage – 
it is only harmed by attacks which have the siege special rule, 
ignoring the damage otherwise. 
 
Huge and Larger Creatures 
Creatures that are of huge size or large may automatically give any 
of their attacks the siege quality. 
 
Vehicle Statistics 
A vehicle entry contains a number of abbreviations that are defined 
below: 
▶SP: Structure Points. This represents the structural integrity of a 
vehicle. When it reaches 0, the vehicle is destroyed. 
▶AS: Armour Save. It tells you what the vehicle’s armour save is. If 
there is a second save listed, such as 5+/6+ then the second save is 
a power save. 
▶Move: How far the vehicle can move in the move step. 
▶ Drift: The minimum amount of movement a vehicle must go in a 
straight line before it can make a turn. 
▶ Manoeuvre: The maximum number of degrees a vehicle can turn 
before needing to move its drift value again. 
▶ Size: There are four different categories of size: medium, large, 
huge and gargantuan Medium vehicles take up 5’x5’ space. Large 
vehicles take up a 10’x10’ space. Huge vehicles take up 15’x15’ 
space. Gargantuan vehicles take up to or more than a 20’x20’ space. 
▶ Immune: If the vehicle is hit with this damage type, then the 
vehicle takes no damage. 
▶ Hardpoints: The is the number of hardpoints the vehicle has 
representing reinforced areas of the vehicle where weapons may be 
mounted. For each hardpoint, they vehicle may be outfitted with a 
piece of siege equipment such as a cannon or scorpion. 
▶ Abilities: Lists other abilities that a vehicle may have. 
 
Combat Involving Vehicles 
Combat involving vehicles works just like ordinary combat with a 
few caveats. 
 
Vehicular Combat Scale 
It is quite possible that a game master would like to have scenarios 
where there are many vehicles involved, and the primary form of 
combat is between these vehicles. This may be the case in situations 
where player characters are part of a pirate crew on the high seas, 
for instance, and battling other privateers and pirate hunters forms 
a basis for the campaign. 
 
For combat in such situations, combat takes place on a hexagonal 
map with each hex equalling 10’. This matches the drift and 
manoeuvre values most closely. This is a deep abstraction, and on 
a combat map each vehicle should only take up 1 hex.  
 
Vehicle Damage 
When a vehicle suffers unsaved damage from an attack with the 
siege special rule, add all the unsaved damage into one pool and 
consult the table below for what kind of impact has been made: 
 
VEHICLE HIT TABLE 
Damage 
Result 
1-3 
1 single impact 
4-6 
2 single impacts 
7-9 
1 double impact 
10-12 
2 double impacts 
13-15 
1 triple impact 
16+ 
2 triple impacts 
 
Once the types of impacts have been made, roll 1d12 on the table 
below for each impact. You roll once for each impact (regardless of 
whether it is single, double, or triple). A double impact counts as 
two impacts to the listed location, and a triple impact counts as 
three. 
 
VEHICLE HIT TABLE 
1d12 
Impact Location 
1 
Structure 
2 
Crew 
3 
Structure 
4 
Armour 
5 
Structure 
6 
Power Source 
7 
Structure 
8 
Locomotion 
9 
Hardpoint 
10 
Locomotion 
11 
Hardpoint 
12 
Attacker’s Choice 
Armour 
The vehicle’s armour is reduced by 1. If it becomes 6+, then all 
future impacts on this location are against the structure. 
 
Crew 
This means a member of the crew has been hit. Determine randomly 
who has been hit. For each level of impact (single, double, or triple) 
the crew member suffers 5 points of damage of the type originally 
used to attack the vehicle. If there are no crew, then all impacts on 
this location are against the structure. 
 
Locomotion 
The manner in which the vehicle moves has been hit.  
▶First Impact: The vehicle’s speed is reduced by 50%. 
▶Second Impact: The vehicle’s speed is reduced by 75%. 
▶Third Impact: The vehicle’s speed is reduced by 100%. 
▶Further Impacts: Count these as being against the structure. 
 
Power Source 
The mode of powering the vehicle has been hit. 
▶First Impact: The vehicle’s speed is reduced by 25% 
▶Second Impact: All creatures within the vehicle make tests with 
a -3 penalty so long as the vehicle is in motion. 
▶Third Impact: The vehicle’s power supply is destroyed. The 
vehicle immediately suffers one additional single impact. 
▶Further Impacts: Count these as being against the structure. 
 
Structure 
Reduce the vehicle’s SP by 1-3, depending on the impact type. 
▶ Single Impact: Reduce the vehicle or structure’s SP by 1. 
▶ Double Impact: Reduce the vehicle or structure’s SP by 2. 
▶ Triple Impact: Reduce the vehicle or structure’s SP by 3. 
 
Hardpoint 
If there are multiple hardpoints on the vehicle, determine randomly 
which is impacted. Hits against vehicles without hardpoints are 
made against the structure. 
▶First Impact: Attacks by the weapons on this hardpoint suffer a -
3 penalty to their attack tests. 
▶Second Impact: Attacks by the weapon on this hardpoint suffer a 
-6 penalty to their attack tests. 
▶Third Impact: The weapon on this hardpoint is destroyed. 
▶Further Impacts: Count these as being against the structure. 
 
Weapon Batteries 
Some vehicles may have several weapons of the same type, such as 
a galley having 22 cannons. To make these weapons easier to run, a 
game master may merge multiple weapons into a single ‘battery’. By 
doing this, the battery multiplies its damage by the number of 
individual weapons in the battery. The number of crew required to 
operate the battery should also be multiplied by the number of 
individual weapons. A single attack test is made to fire a battery. 
 
 
 


 
96 
 
Boarding Actions 
If a boarding action between two vehicles such as ships occurs, roll 
1d100 for each side. Add the number of attackers and defenders 
(usually the crew) as bonuses to their respective rolls and add the 
level of any character’s present as a bonus too. The highest number 
wins.  
 
A defender pushes the enemy ship off whereas an attacker gains 
control of much (if not all) of the ship they have boarded. This is 
very abstract but can be useful if game masters need a resolution to 
this sort of activity quickly. 
 
If a more thorough outcome is needed, use the Battle Engine or War 
Engine rules and calculate both crews as the relevant forces. 
 
Structures 
Fighting and damaging structures works exactly like fighting and 
damaging vehicles, only count every hit as against the structure. 
 
The following gives the amount of structure points and armour 
typical of several structures.

2+ 
Tower, Wooden, Square 
10 
4+ 
Tower, Wooden, Circular

3+

6 
3+ 
Gateway, Portcullis 
8 
2+ 
 
Vehicles 
Below are some vehicles that may be present in a campaign of Mud, 
Blood & Rats. 
 
Barge, Large – Huge Vehicle 
Large mercantile guilds often own several large barges for moving 
cargo around the known world. 
SP: 16  AS: 5+  Move: sail 40’/4mph  Drift: 10’  Manoeuvre: 120° 
Armour: 
▶ The vehicle has reinforced wooden boards, giving a 5+ armour 
save. 
Crew: 2 + 6 passengers 
▶ 4 crew members are required to move and drive the barge. The 
skill to do this is Drive (Sailing). 
▶ 12 passenger slots are available. 
Locomotion:  
▶ The ship is moved by its sail. 
 
Power Source:  
▶ The ship’s power source is its hull. 
 
Hardpoints: 2 
Abilities:  
▶ Flammable: The barge is particularly at risk to fire. Any fire 
damage the barge suffers counts as having the siege quality. 
▶ Cargo: The barge may carry 140 tons of cargo. 
 
 
 
Barge, Small – Huge Vehicle 
Many folk of the world eke out their lives on narrowboats and other 
small sailing vessels, living on canals and rivers. 
SP: 10  AS: 5+  Move: sail 40’/4mph  Drift: 10’  Manoeuvre: 120° 
Armour: 
▶ The vehicle has reinforced wooden boards, giving a 5+ armour 
save. 
Crew: 2 + 6 passengers 
▶ 1 crew members are required to move and drive the barge. The 
skill to do this is Drive (Sailing). 
▶ 8 passenger slots are available. 
Locomotion:  
▶ The ship is moved by its sail. 
 
Power Source:  
▶ The ship’s power source is its hull. 
 
Hardpoints: 1 
Abilities:  
▶ Flammable: The barge is particularly at risk to fire. Any fire 
damage the barge suffers counts as having the siege quality. 
▶ Cargo: The barge may carry 80 tons of cargo. 
 
Carriage – Huge Vehicle 
Carriages are carts with enclosed roofs to protect passengers from the 
elements of the world. 
SP: 5  AS: 5+  Move: 30’/3mph Drift: 10’  Manoeuvre: 120° 
Armour: 
▶ The vehicle has reinforced wooden boards, giving a 5+ armour 
save. 
Crew: 1 + 6 Passengers 
▶ 1 crew member is required to move and drive the vehicle. The 
skill to do this is Drive (Cart). 
▶ 4 passenger slots are available. 
Locomotion:  
▶ The vehicle’s locomotion is 4 wooden wheels. 
 
Power Source:  
▶ The vehicle must be pulled by at least 1 beast of burden. If the 
impact location is the power source, ignore the normal damage. 
Instead deal damage to one of the creatures’ pulling the carriage. 
▶ If at least four large creatures are pulling the carriage, increase its 
Move to 40’ (providing the animals can actually move at least this 
quickly usually). 
Hardpoints: 0 
 
Cart – Huge Vehicle 
Common throughout the known world, the simple horse and cart is 
a useful vehicle when attempting long voyages. 
SP: 3  AS: 6+  Move: 30’/3mph Drift: 10’  Manoeuvre: 120° 
Armour: 
▶ The vehicle has no armour. 
Crew: 1 + 6 Passengers 
▶ 1 crew member is required to move and drive the vehicle. The 
skill to do this is Drive (Cart). 
▶ 6 passenger slots are available. 
Locomotion:  
▶ The vehicle’s locomotion is 4 wooden wheels. 
 
Power Source:  
▶ The vehicle must be pulled by at least 1 beast of burden. If the 
impact location is the power source, ignore the normal damage. 
Instead deal damage to one of the creatures’ pulling the cart. 
▶ If at least four large creatures are pulling the cart, increase its 
Move to 40’ (providing the animals can actually move at least this 
quickly usually). 
Hardpoints: 0 
Abilities:  
▶ Open Topped: Attacks against this vehicle may be made against 
the crew and passengers instead of the vehicle.  
▶ Cargo: The barge may carry 10 tons of cargo.

99 
 
Encounters
Encounters are the bread and butter of adventure. This section 
contains rules and procedures for generating encounters as the 
characters travel the game world, both overland and within the 
grotty streets of the larger cities. 
 
Encounters: What’s the Point? 
In more traditional fantasy games, there is often the expectation of 
procedural content and play emerging from ‘random encounters’. 
However, within the grubby fantasy genre which generally leans 
more towards investigation, mystery, and politicking, these 
expectations are not the same. These assumptions about the genre 
may encourage the game master to avoid random monsters on the 
road. Having acknowledged these differences, the following 
encounters have been carefully considered to reinforce the 
conventions of this specific genre and are constructed to be light 
enough on detail that a game master can use them in any way they 
see fit. It is suggested that you use these to help inspire any such 
moments that you believe will add tension or texture to your 
campaigns of grubby, grimy adventure. 
 
Wilderness Encounters 
Most characters in a game of Mud, Blood & Rats will spend time 
walking from location to location, often through areas of unexplored 
wilderness. 
 
The Wilderness in Grubby Fantasy 
The wilderness in a grubby fantasy world is rife with danger. It’s 
crisscrossed with half-maintained roads and dotted with the 
occasional village, which may or may not be the centre of a mad cult 
dedicated to a pantheon of dark gods.  
 
The woods are of particular significance to such a setting, and rightly 
so. They represent the unseen enemy – evil creatures that scheme 
and plot the downfall of humanity. They are thick with mutants and 
witches. For this reason, a distinction is made between woodland 
and deep woods in the encounter rules. Woodland is any wooded 
area relatively close to a settlement, fortification or any other such 
haven. Deep woods are those areas of forest far from civilisation. In 
such places, the corruption grows openly, and darkness is king. A 
good rule of thumb is that any wooded area within six miles of a 
settlement is woodland whereas any such place more than six miles 
from a settlement is the deep woods. 
 
Movement in the Wilderness 
Every character has a movement rate per day. The slowest member 
of a party determines the overall speed of the party, unless the 
members of the group are happy to leave one or more of their slower 
companions behind.  
 
Some terrain will affect the movement speed of the party, as 
determined by the terrain types below: 
▶ Woodland, Deep Woods: 75% speed/Hard (-3) Pathfinding to 
avoid getting lost each day. 
▶ Mountain, Swamp: 50% speed/Very Hard (-6) Pathfinding to 
avoid getting lost each day. 
▶ Plain/Scrub: 100% speed/Easy (+3) Pathfinding to avoid getting 
lost each day. 
▶ Road: 150% speed/no Pathfinding test required to avoid getting 
lost. 
 
Wilderness Encounter Die 
Each day that a party is exploring the wilderness, roll two dice to 
see if they get an encounter during the day, or at night. If the dice 
comes up as a ‘1’ then they have an encounter. The dice rolled for 
this are determined by the current terrain, as seen below: 
▶ Mountain: Daytime d8; Nighttime d12. 
▶ Woodland, Deep Woods, Plain, Scrub: Daytime d6; Nighttime 
d12. 
▶ Swamp: Daytime d6; Nighttime d8. 
▶ Settled Terrain*: Daytime d6; Nighttime d10. 
▶ Road: Use the dice type for the terrain through which the road 
runs. 
 
*Any terrain within 6 miles of a large town, city or fortification that 
has the power to exert its influence over the region through the use 
of patrols and other measures. 
 
Wilderness Encounter Distance 
If an encounter occurs, then the game master rolls 3d6 and multiplies 
the result by ten. This is the number of feet away that the 
approaching creature(s) is when someone in the party detects it. For 
nighttime encounters, only multiply the number by 5, unless 
appropriate watches have been set. 
 
 
 
Wilderness Encounter Tables 
To help a game master adjudicate what creature is met when an encounter is generated, roll on the table below, appropriate for the party’s current 
terrain type.

Waterborne Encounters 
It is quite possible that the player characters will find themselves 
moving along the various waterways of the grim and grubby worlds 
of Mud, Blood & Rats. Indeed, it is that case that many developed 
nations moving into the direction of modernity have developed 
cultural histories around rivers and the increasing reliance of 
industry on canals means that characters are never far from water. 
 
In most situations, it is safe for characters to travel by water and they 
may wish to pay for passage on a barge or other such vehicle. 
 
Movement on Water 
The use of marine vehicles like ships and boats is not covered here, 
but in the chapter detailing vehicles and structures. The movement 
speed of a particular sailing vessel will be largely down to factors 
such as size, adequate crew and favourable currents and winds. 
 
Waterborne Encounter Die 
The game master should check to see if a party has an encounter 
when adventuring on or near water, as determined by the 
information below. Roll the dice at the appropriate time. If the result 
is a ‘1’ then an encounter occurs: 
▶ Lake, River, Canal: Daytime 86; Nighttime d12. 
 
 
 
 
Waterborne Encounter Tables 
The tables below are used to help a game master adjudicate what 
creatures are encountered when a party traverses the rivers, canals 
and lakes of the known world. 
 
Waterborne Encounter Distance 
If an encounter occurs, then the game master rolls 3d6 and multiplies 
the result by ten. This is the number of yards away that the 
approaching creature(s) is when someone in the party detects it. For 
nighttime encounters, only multiply the number by 5, unless 
appropriate watches have been set.
