Travel Event Descriptions 
Below are the event descriptions. All of these are suggestions, and 
the game master may wish to flesh out the description with greater 
roleplaying detail.  
 
Ancient Cairns 
Someone in the party notices a group of burial cairns nearby. 
These consist of stone rings, barrows and other ancient tombs. 
These may simply add colour to the adventure, but they may also 
prove interesting if the players explore them. If players decide to 
explore them, you may wish to use the following ideas: 
 
The burial of members of an ancient and forgotten race. 
 
Thick with undead skeletons and zombies. 
 
Connecting to various tunnels and levels. 
 
Haunted by ghosts. 
 
Aura of Madness 
There is some sort of celestial event – perhaps the planets align, or 
a comet made of madness passes the night sky. Whatever the 
event, each character should save vs PER or gain a Grime point.  
 
Blizzard 
Driving snow comes down heavily on the party. If the party insists 
on pressing on through the rain or doesn’t have adequate 
protection from the weather while they rest, they will awake the 
next day with 2 drained conditions, either way each member of 
the party suffers 2 cold damage that ignores armour saves unless 
they wait out the blizzard next a suitably sized fire. 
 


Encounters 
105 
 
Boat Fire 
The vessel that the party are travelling on catches fire 
unexpectedly. It is up to the game master as to how to handle this, 
but the following dramatic test is provided to help you adjudicate 
this event, with example skills and their use: 
 
5 successful tests before 3 failures. 
 
Example Tests 
▶ STR: A character may test STR to pail water onto the fire. 
▶ END: A character may test END to hold their breath in the smoke 
and flames. 
▶ Drive (Sailing): A character may test Drive (Sailing) to maintain 
control of the boat. 
▶ Composure: A character may test Composure to keep their cool 
and direct their companions against the blaze. 
▶ Perception:  A character may test Perception to locate an easier 
way to put the fire out. 
▶ Melee (Any):  A character may test Melee to cut ropes in order to 
release a sail or other flammable objects from the boat. 
 
Example Consequences of Failure 
▶ The boat sinks. 
▶ A character catches fire. 
 
Broken Road Sign 
The characters come to a fork in the road but the signage is 
unclear, broken or vandalised in such a way that it cannot be relied 
upon. One character should test Pathfinding. On a success, they 
intuit which way to go but on a failure the party becomes lost. 
 
Charcoal Burners 
Beehive-shaped kilns and black mounds are tended to by men 
whose faces and hands are covered in sooty grime. The party may 
speak with the charcoal burners who, because of their profession, 
seem to know everyone. Any Gossip tests with them are made as 
very easy (+6). 
 
Circling Wolves 
A group of 1d6+6 wolves begins tailing the party. Do not complete 
a reaction roll: for whatever reason this group of wolves is 
starving and desperate. They will attempt to attack the party. 
Coaching House 
The party comes to one of the many coaching houses that dot the 
many roads and rivers of the civilised world. This place may offer a 
warm bed and a hot meal to those who can pay. A game master 
may wish to roll on the following table to determine the relative 
quality of the coaching house. If you wish to use the Tavern system 
for this coaching house, use the encounters for villages. 
 
COACHING HOUSE TABLE 
d6 
Coaching House Quality 
1 
Terrible 
2 
Basic 
3-5 
Moderate 
6 
Lavish 
 
Damaged Bridge 
The road leads across a bridge that has been damaged in some 
way, perhaps by accident, weather, wear and tear or active 
maliciousness. It is up to the game master as to how to handle this, 
but the following dramatic test is provided to help you adjudicate 
this event, with example skills and their use: 
 
5 successful tests before 3 failures. 
 
Example Tests 
▶ AGI: A character may test AGI to leap across the gap on the 
bridge. 
▶ Pathfinding: A character may test Pathfinding to locate another 
bridge. 
▶ Perception:  A character may test Perception to determine the 
way in which the bridge was damaged. 
▶ Survival:  A character may test Survival to locate an area where 
the river or gorge may be forded. 
▶ Cast Spell (Fabricate):  The game master may wish to give the 
party an automatic success if a wizard character casts Fabricate to 
repair part of the bridge (and maybe a failure if the spell fails to 
cast). 
 
Example Consequences of Failure 
▶ A character falls into the gap on the bridge. 
▶ One of the party’s animals is spooked. 
▶ Monsters such as goblinoids or forestborn attack. 
 
 


Encounters 
106 
 
Dead Courier 
The party come across a dead courier or other some such 
messenger. The courier should be carrying a news article or 
message that is either useful or interesting to the party (or both). 
Relevant tests such as Healing (Any) and Perception may reveal 
the how the courier died. 
 
 
 
Driving Rain 
Endless heavy rain assaults the party. If the party insists on 
pressing on through the rain or doesn’t have adequate protection 
from the weather while they rest, they will awake the next day 
with 2 drained conditions. 
 
Dwarf Berserkers 
The party meet 1d3 dwarf berserkers while travelling. The game 
master should roll appropriate reaction rolls for the dwarf 
berserkers. There is all manner of ways that the dwarf berserkers 
may interact with the characters. Ideas include: 
 
The dwarfs are seeking a great monster to test their 
mettle. 
 
They are travelling to muster for an army. 
 
They are searching for some ancient piece of dwarf lore 
or missing magic item. 
 
They are investigating the same thing the characters are. 
 
Endless Roads 
The game master should immediately re-roll the encounter on the 
table for the terrain through which the road is moving (for the 
appropriate time of day). 
 
Fallen Trees 
One or more trees has fallen, blocking or damaging the trail. It is 
up to the game master as to how to handle this, but the following 
dramatic test is provided to help you adjudicate this event, with 
example skills and their use: 
 
3 successful tests before 2 failures. 
 
Example Tests 
▶ STR: A character may test STR to move the trees out of the way. 
▶ Lore (Nature):  A character may test Lore (Nature) to determine 
what caused the trees to fall in the first place. 
▶ Pathfinding: A character may test Pathfinding to locate a safe 
way through the area. 
▶ Perception:  A character may test Perception to determine if any 
other trees are likely to fall. 
 
Example Consequences of Failure 
▶ Another tree falls, perhaps injuring someone. 
▶ One of the party’s animals is spooked. 
▶ Monsters such as goblinoids or forestborn attack. 
 
Fire 
Due to some unforeseen disaster a fire spreads throughout the 
camp or place where the party are resting. It is up to the game 
master to produce details of this scene, but it is likely that at least 
some of the party’s equipment will be destroyed. 
 
Flooded Trail 
The track, trail or road that the characters are walking down has 
become totally flooded perhaps due to heavy rain, a river that has 
burst its banks or other such inconvenience. It is up to the game 
master as to how to handle this, but the following dramatic test is 
provided to help you adjudicate this event, with example skills and 
their use: 
 
5 successful tests before 3 failures. 
 
Example Tests 
▶ STR: A character may test STR to push the party’s cart, wagon or 
other vehicle through the mud (if they have one). 
▶ AGI: A character may test AGI to maintain their footing in the 
mud. 
▶ Pathfinding: A character may test Pathfinding to find a route 
through the muddy area. 
▶ Animal Handling (-3):  A character may test Animal Handling 
to help any animals such as horses that the party has through the 
muddy region. 
▶ Perception:  A character may test Perception to locate higher 
ground or roadways made of stone rather than earth. 
▶ Cast Spell (Protection from Rain):  The game master may wish 
to give the party an automatic success if a wizard character casts 
Protection from Rain (and maybe a failure if the spell fails to cast). 
 
Example Consequences of Failure 
▶ A character falls in the mud. 
▶ One of the party’s animals is spooked. 
▶ The party lose the trail and become lost. 
▶ Monsters such as goblinoids or forestborn attack. 
 
Highwaymen 
The party are stopped by a group of 1d6+3 highwaymen. The 
game master should roll appropriate reaction rolls for the 
minstrel. The highwaymen intend to rob the characters and would 
prefer not to fight. If the party looks poorly dressed and each have 
a Status of Trained or Untrained they may be able to convince 
the highwaymen that they are too poor.  
 
Hungry Ogres 
The party meet 1d4 ogre brutes while travelling. The game master 
should roll appropriate reaction rolls for the ogres. There is all 
manner of ways that the ogres may interact with the characters. 
Ideas include: 
 
They are hungry, and will offer the party safe passage (or 
payment) to be able to eat part of one member of the 
party, such as an arm or leg. 
 
They attempt to make the characters into their next meal 
 
They are hunting something and want the characters 
help. 
 
They are looking for work as mercenaries. 
 
Hunters 
The party meet a group of 1d6 hunters while travelling. The 
hunters are out catching game and setting traps. The game master 
should roll appropriate reaction rolls for the hunters. There is all 
manner of ways that the hunters may interact with the characters. 
Ideas include: 
 
They want help tracking a particularly difficult or 
monstrous game animal (see the Monstrous Fauna 
event). 
 
They are willing to sell the party rations, hides or other 
such equipment. 
 
They can tell the party the latest gossip from the nearby 
towns. 
 


Encounters 
107 
 
Illness 
Someone in the party, determined randomly, contracts an illness. 
Roll on the table to determine was illness they have contracted: 
ILLNESS TABLE 
D4 
Disease 
1 
The Green Flu 
2 
The Dripping Anvil 
3-4 
The Stinging Trots 
 
See the Sickness and Disease descriptions on page 191 for 
information on how to adjudicate these illnesses. 
 
Isolated Settlement 
The party find a settlement, perhaps a farm or even small village 
that is largely isolated from the outside world. It is up to you as the 
game master to decide the details of this place, but some ideas may 
include. 
 
It is a simple logging camp or farm that occasionally 
sends out members to sell their goods. 
 
The people here all worship evil and proscribed gods. 
 
The people live with mutants or forestborn. 
 
Knights 
The party meet 1d6 knights while travelling. The game master 
should roll appropriate reaction rolls for the knights. There is all 
manner of ways that the knights may interact with the characters. 
Ideas include: 
 
The knights are on a quest, hunting a great monster. 
 
They are travelling to muster for an army. 
 
They are on a secret mission for their lord. 
 
They are investigating the same thing the characters are. 
 
Lightning 
A black and terrifying storm rolls in. Lightning strikes the ground 
nearby. Unless the characters take immediate action to shield 
themselves, one of them is struck by lightning. Roll 1d20 for each 
character in the party – the lowest score is the person who is hit by 
lightning. Characters wearing metal armour on the body roll twice 
and take the lowest. If a character is hit by lightning, they take 16 
lightning damage, rend (2) on the head location. A save vs AGI 
halves this damage. 
 
Lost 
The trail is confusing and the party are on the verge of losing 
themselves. One character in the party must succeed a hard (-3) 
Pathfinding test or the party becomes lost. 
 
Low Bridge 
The ship approaches a bridge or other similarly low structure. Roll 
1d20 for each character in the party – the character with the lowest 
score has their head up at the wrong time and they take a nasty 
bang as their head connects with the bridge, suffering 5 
bludgeoning damage. If this reduces them to 0HP then they are 
also knocked overboard. 
 
Lurid Chanting 
The characters can hear distant chanting, perhaps it is from a 
settlement of mutants or a gathering or forestborn. Either way, it is 
deeply unsettling. Each character should save vs PER or awake the 
next morning with 2 drained conditions and a Grime point.  
 
Man Overboard! 
A character slips on the deck or trips over a coil of rope causing 
themselves to fall overboard. Roll 1d20 for each character in the 
party – the lowest score is the person who falls overboard. This 
might not be such an issue, so long as the character has at least 
trained in Athletics (Swim), otherwise the issue may be more 
serious. After determining who has gone overboard, immediately 
roll 1d6. On a 1, a giant pike also attacks.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Massacre 
A terrible massacre has occurred against another group of 
travellers. The game master is encouraged to describe how the 
wagons or other vehicles have been wrecked and burned and how 
bodies are perhaps strewn carelessly about. A game master can roll 
on the table below to determine what perpetrated the massacre. 
Roll 1d8 for each character. The character with the lowest score 
finds someone they know amongst the dead.  After appropriate 
tests such as Perception and Lore the party may be able to 
discover what sort of creatures perpetrated the massacre. If the 
grieving character liked the person who was killed, they may 
immediately gain the Hatred Talent against the type of creature 
that perpetrated the massacre.  
 
MASSACRE TABLE 
d6 
Perpetrator 
1 
Goblinoids 
2-3 
Criminals 
4 
Mutants 
5 
Trolls 
6 
Forestborn 
 
 
Minstrel 
The party meet a minstrel while travelling. The game master should 
roll appropriate reaction rolls for the minstrel. The minstrel may 
accompany the party. They love to play their instrument (which 
may be anything from a flute, hurdy-gurdy or even bagpipes). 
Capricious game masters may wish to have their love of playing to 
extend to all times of day and even the dead of night.  
 
 


Encounters 
108 
 
Monstrous Fauna 
This far from civilisation even the natural world can be subject to 
the corrupting effects of the darkness. The party encounter an 
animal that is obviously mutated or monstrous in some way. This 
animal has twice the hit points of its usual form, is worth twice the 
amount of XP, has a power save of a 6+ on all locations and causes 
one point of grime to anyone it manages to inflict damage on. 
Additionally, it inflicts +3 damage with each of its attacks. Roll on 
the table before for the type of creature: 
 
MONSTROUS FAUNA TABLE 
d6 
Perpetrator 
1-2 
Boar 
3 
Bear, Black 
4 
Bear, Brown 
5-6 
Wolverine 
 
Pedlar 
A strange individual wearing coloured rags and carrying several 
wicker baskets approaches the party. The pedlar has any common 
item for sale at double the price, as well as the following items: 
▶ Lucky Charm: 8d. This charm may be a piece of magic heather, 
a lucky rabbit’s foot or other such charm. A character wearing this 
may re-roll any test, the charm then crumbles. One use. 
▶ Holy Pendant: 1gp. The pedlar is selling something pertaining 
to be a holy relic of power. Its presence will allow the wearer to 
automatically ignore the damage of one successful enemy attack on 
them, then the pendent crumbles to dust. One use. 
 
Poisoned Earth 
Due to the effects of malignant sorcery, extreme industrialism, or a 
site of a great catastrophe, this area is corrupted. The exact details 
are left up to the game master, but anyone eating or drinking 
things foraged or hunted in this place should immediately gain a 
grime point. 
 
Road Patrol 
The party meet a 1d6+1 road patrolmen while travelling. The 
game master should roll appropriate reaction rolls for the 
patrolmen. There is all manner of ways that the patrolmen may 
interact with the characters. Ideas include: 
 
The patrolmen are searching for a particular 
highwayman. Perhaps they believe one of the characters 
is who they’re after. 
 
They warn the characters that the area is thick with 
goblinoids, mutants or other such creatures (game 
masters may wish to have these creatures attack the party 
later). 
 
They patrolmen are hunting down a particular band of 
creatures right now. 
 
They are investigating smuggling. They may wish to 
check the party’s vehicle (if they have one). 
 
Rockslide 
There is a sudden trickle of stones that quickly give way to a 
dangerous landslide It is up to the game master as to how to 
handle this, but the following dramatic test is provided to help 
you adjudicate this event, with example skills and their use: 
 
5 successful tests before 3 failures. 
 
Example Tests 
▶ AGI: A character may test AGI to evade incoming falling stones 
▶ Pathfinding: A character may test Pathfinding to locate a suitable 
route around the landslide. 
▶ Perception:  A character may test Perception to find a place to 
hide while the landslide is happening 
▶ STR:  A character may test STR to drag their companions from the 
path of the landslide. 
▶ Cast Spell (Cloak of the Eight Winds):  The game master may 
wish to give the party an automatic success if a wizard character 
casts Cloak of the Eight Winds to protect themselves from the falling 
rocks (and maybe a failure if the spell fails to cast). 
 
 
 
 
 
Spoiled Rations 
Someone realises their rations have gotten wet and spoiled. Roll 
1d20 for each character in the party – the lowest score is the 
person whose rations are destroyed. All their carried food must be 
discarded. 
 
Stuck in the Mud 
The trail the party are following through the swamp becomes 
impossible to follow and soon enough they are wading through the 
muck.  
 
5 successful tests before 3 failures. 
 
Example Tests 
▶ END: A character may test END to press on through the mire. 
▶ Pathfinding: A character may test Pathfinding to help locate a 
trail. 
▶ STR:  A character may test STR to carry a companion through the 
swamp. 
▶ Survival: A character may test Survival to ignore the strange witch 
lights that try to lead them astray. 
▶ Lore (Nature):  A character may test Lore (Nature) to recall useful 
knowledge about this swamp or the ways in which creatures pass 
through it. 
 
Example Consequences of Failure 
▶ A character loses their boots or other piece of equipment in the 
mud. 
▶ One of the party’s animals is spooked. 
▶ Monsters such as goblinoids or forestborn attack. 
 
Tarn of Power 
The party come across a forlorn tarn. Perhaps due to the influence 
of weird magic or perhaps simply the spirits of such a remote 
place. There are many ways that the tarn may be interacted with. 
 
Members of the party see a vision of their future in the 
tarn. 
 
Drinking from tarn causes the character doing so to gain 
a grime point. 
 
It is haunted by a benevolent or malevolent water spirit 
that exacts some toll from the characters. 
 
Tollkeeper 
A small man emerges from a stone building at the side of the road 
or waterway bearing a lantern and a holding out a coin bag. The 
party must pay the tollkeeper to pass this way. The cost is up to 
game master, but a rough estimate is about 1d4 shillings per 
person. If the toll is not paid, they may not pass this way and must 
find another way round. 
 
Travelling Dwarfs 
The party meet a group of travelling dwarf merchants. The dwarfs 
are willing to sell the following items at list cost. 
▶ Dwarf Ale 
▶ Battle Axes 
▶ Handgun, (Crank) 
▶ Dwarf Tunnel Gun 
 
Unsure Footing 
One character (determined at random) slips or twists their ankle. 
The ankle can be cured with a successful Healing (Surgery) test 
and a day’s rest. Until healed the character has a permanent 
drained condition and moves at half speed. 
 
Wagon Train 
A train of wagons is heading in the same direction as the party. At 
the cost of 6d per day, they may ride the wagons. While the party 
are with the wagons, they increase the wilderness encounter die 
type by one (so d6s become d8, d12s become d20s, etc) reducing 
the chance of an encounter overall. 
 
 


Encounters 
109 
 
Warrior Priest 
The party meet a warrior priest while travelling. The game master 
should roll appropriate reaction rolls for the warrior priest. There 
is all manner of ways that the warrior priest may interact with the 
characters. Ideas include: 
 
They are travelling to reconsecrate ground overrun by 
monsters. 
 
They are travelling to muster for an army. 
 
They are travelling to deal with an outbreak of undead or 
mutation. 
 
They are investigating the same thing the characters are. 
 
Wayshrine 
The party come across a mouldering shrine to a deity. The deity 
may be one commonly worshipped in the land or it may be an 
ancient deity. The game master may wish to allow their player 
characters to worship here for a minor boon. 
 
Wicked Dolmens 
A series of eerie standing stones have been erected here. Their 
exact nature is left up to the game master, but some ideas may 
include: 
 
They are a magical nexus left by an ancient and evil race. 
Magic here is more potent but also provokes grime 
points. 
 
They’re sacred to wood-dwelling monsters such as 
dryads or forestborn. 
 
It is the entrance to a site of ancient elven power. 
 
Witch 
The party meet a witch (or warlock) while travelling. They perhaps 
see them travelling or notice their abode. The game master should 
roll appropriate reaction rolls for the witch. There is all manner of 
ways that the witch may interact with the characters. Ideas include: 
 
They want something from the party, such as a lock of 
hair or fingernail. 
 
They may sell the party poisons or healing poultices. 
 
They tell the party their future. 
Witch Lights 
Strange flickering lights are seen within the swamp. These are 
malevolent spirits trying to lure travellers to their doom. The party 
should save vs PER or follow them or stop walking, becoming 
fascinated by them. If more than half the party fail this save, the 
game master should roll for another travel encounter, but assume 
the d6 roll was a 6, meaning a d20 on the bestiary table as 
monsters rush in. If this encounter happens at night, then it only 
affects the characters who are on watch. 
 
Witch Hunter 
The party meet a witch hunter while travelling. The game master 
should roll appropriate reaction rolls for the witch hunter. There 
is all manner of ways that the witch hunter may interact with the 
characters. Ideas include: 
 
They want something from the party, such as 
information. 
 
They have come to arrest spellcasters in the party. 
 
They are hunting a dangerous witch or mutant nearby. 
 
Zealots 
The party meet 1d6+6 zealots while travelling. The game master 
should roll appropriate reaction rolls for the zealots. There is all 
manner of ways that the zealots may interact with the characters. 
Ideas include: 
 
The zealots believe the end times are here and that the 
characters should immediately throw away all of their 
earthly goods. 
 
They are travelling to muster for an army, hoping to find 
peace in battle. 
 
They are obsessed with some religious development, 
diktat or person and they want the party to be too. 
 


Encounters 
110 
 
Settlement Encounters 
Settlements in a game of Mud, Blood & Rats are crammed to the brim 
with preening nobles, disguised mutants, murderous alley bashers 
and other such losers, freaks and delinquent wasters. While game 
masters are free to run settlements as they wish, the following 
subsystem is offered as an option to structure this part of the game 
as urban mystery and intrigue is often a central element of grubby 
fantasy. 
 
The Settlement Turn 
A settlement turn is broken into the following steps each turn, which 
represents a day of exploration and intrigue in a settlement: 
 
Settlement Steps Each Round 
1. Form Parties 
2. Choose Settlement Activity 
3. Roll for Settlement Event 
 
1. Form Parties 
Sometimes it is much more useful to have the party split up and 
complete different activities in the settlement. Similarly, it may be 
that some characters need to rest and recuperate after significant 
peril. In this step players should decide which character will be 
spending the day with which other characters. All characters in each 
party complete the same activity and experience the same settlement 
encounter (if there is one).  
 
 
2. Choose Settlement Activity 
Each party chooses what activity they will complete for the day and 
then engages in that activity. Possible activities are detailed below: 
Carouse: 
The party wanders about the town squares, taverns and other 
similar places gathering gossip, gambling and drinking. While it’s 
almost certain that they will gather information, it’s less clear that it 
will help them with their current goals and interests. 
Investigate: 
The party completes some element of investigation that they have 
prepared, such as speaking with officials, examining a particular 
place or securing significant information. 
Shopping: 
The party visits various vendors and traders, buying and selling as 
they please. This may include the acquisition and sale of cargo that 
the party may have obtained. 
Craft 
The party spends the day crafting objects that they have the skills 
and talents for. 
Visit Patron 
Certain party members may have a patron: apprentice wizards and 
engineers may have masters that they are studying under while 
squires may wish to speak with their knightly orders. 
 
3. Roll for Settlement Event 
Each day that a party is out and about in a settlement, roll a 1d6 to 
see if they get an event. If the die comes up as a ‘1’ then they have 
an event that day. Descriptions for each event can be found on the 
following pages.

Encounters 
111 
 
Settlement Event Descriptions 
Below are the settlement event descriptions. All of these are 
suggestions, and the game master may wish to flesh out the 
description with greater roleplaying detail.  
 
Accident 
The party is caught up in some physical accident to which they 
must respond. You may wish to use the following ideas: 
 
A barrel slips off a wagon and must either be dodged or 
caught by a party member. 
 
A horse or other beast of burden is out of control and 
needs to be stopped. 
 
A manhole cover is left open, and a party member 
(almost) falls down it into the sewers. 
 
Alley Scum 
The party is set upon by 1d6+3 thugs. They intend to mug the 
party but don’t let that stop you from murdering a couple of 
characters if they put up too much of a fight. 
 
Beggars 
The party is accosted by a throng of ragged beggars. There are 2d6 
beggars, each of which demands a pfennig from the characters. If 
any of the characters are of Expert Status or higher (and show it) 
then the beggars raise their demand to 6d each. Use appropriate 
reaction rolls for the beggars, as they may become violent if 
denied. 
 
 
Betrothal 
One individual in the party, determined at random, is suddenly 
surrounded by 1d6+6 peasants. All these peasants are quite angry 
and believe that the character has agreed to marry their brother, 
sister or other relative (maybe one of their farm animals or ancient 
aunties if you’re feeling particularly capricious). 
 
Coin Clippers 
One party member (determined at random) realises that their 
carried coinage has been clipped or shaved, reducing its value. The 
affected character’s carried wealth is now worth 25% less than what 
it was.  
 
Cultists 
Settlements are breeding grounds for wicked cults and murderous 
religions. One member of the party, determined at random, must 
test Perception. On a success, they notice a series of figures 
moving in an otherwise abandoned or empty building. 
Investigation may allow them to realise that they have stumbled on 
a cult dedicated to the worship of demons. Failure may mean that 
the cult goes unnoticed, but a game master may wish to have the 
cultists attack the party. If this is the case, 1d6+4 cultists attack 
(use the statblock for zealots). 
Debt 
One party member, determined at random, is served with notice 
that a previous debt has caught up with them. A game master may 
have this encounter play out in several ways, but some ideas may 
include: 
 
When they return to their lodgings, a character finds a 
letter has arrived which details the debt and when it 
must be repaid. 
 
A debt collector and their thugs stop the party in the 
street. 
 
The debt is actually for somebody else who has the same 
(or similar) name as the character. 
 
The initial amount borrowed was nominal, but the 
interest has been astronomical – the player owes 
somewhere in the region of 1d10 gold pieces. 
 
Duel 
A noble challenges one member of the party to a duel. The duel is 
likely to take place the following day at dawn using pistols. A 
character cannot reasonably deny the challenge without being run 
out of the settlement. A game master may have this encounter play 
out in several ways, but some ideas may include: 
 
The character accidentally bumped the noble into the 
mud for which they are insulted. 
 
The noble has been wronged in the past by the actions 
of the party. 
 
The noble is acting out of spite or for the party’s enemies 
and wants to kill the character in a legal fashion. 
 
Dwarf Berserkers 
The party meet 1d3 dwarf berserkers while in the settlement. The 
game master should roll appropriate reaction rolls for the dwarf 
berserkers. There is all manner of ways that the dwarf berserkers 
may interact with the characters. Ideas include: 
 
The dwarfs are seeking a great monster to test their 
mettle. 
 
They are travelling to muster for an army. 
 
They are searching for some ancient piece of dwarf lore 
or missing magic item. 
 
They are investigating the same thing the characters are. 
 
Dwarf Engineering Machine 
A nearby piece of dwarfen machinery is out of control. You may 
wish to use the following ideas: 
 
A steam carriage careens down the street towards the 
party. 
 
A nearby digging or lifting machine suddenly spins out 
of control. 
 
A pump, vessel or other piece of machinery suddenly 
explodes. 
 
False Accusation 
1d6+6 guards bar the party’s way. One party member, determined 
at random, is accused of a crime that they (probably) did not 
commit. Roll 1d6 on the following table to see what crime they 
have been accused of.  
 
FALSE ACCUSATION TABLE 
d6 
Crime 
1 
Murder 
2 
Heresy 
3 
Robbery 
4 
Assault 
5 
Fraud 
6 
Toll Evasion 
 
A game master may have this encounter play out in a number of 
ways, but some ideas may include: 
 
The arresting guards know of the character’s innocence 
but are determined to exhort them for money. 
 
The character looks uncannily like someone who has 
actually committed a crime. 
 
The accusation has come from one of the character’s 
enemies who are trying to false imprison them (or 
worse). 
 


Encounters 
112 
 
Fanatics of Doom 
The party cross paths with a band of fanatics. There is 1d6+4 of 
them. These fanatics rant about the end of the world, whip 
themselves, burn themselves and generally rant incoherently. The 
game master should make reaction rolls for the fanatics. A high 
roll means that the fanatics merely rant, telling the characters to 
throw away their worldly possessions as the end of days surely 
approaches. A low roll means that the same happens, but they will 
attempt to enforce this view on the players, by violence if 
necessary.  
 
Fire 
A fire breaks out in the settlement, and the party become involved. 
A game master may have this encounter play out in several ways, 
but some ideas may include: 
 
The fire was started by the party’s enemies, and they are 
going to frame the characters. 
 
A dramatic accident has meant that fire is quickly 
spreading from building to building and dwarfs are 
rushing to demolition the wooden, human made 
dwellings to make a fire break. 
 
Someone is clearly trapped in a flaming building nearby 
and the party could do something to save them. 
 
Fooled 
One person who has purchased something today, determined at 
random, realises the item they have bought is worthless: weapons 
fall to pieces, food is spoiled, horses are dead, or some other such 
nonsense. Use this event to irritate the players – if they (perhaps 
falsely) accuse the vendor of conning them, even better. 
 
Funeral Procession 
The characters see a procession of priests and mourners on their 
way to a funeral. Perhaps some of the people are carrying a coffin 
(or coffins). A character may bow their head and make a very hard 
(-6) Pray test to see if their own patron deities are listening. On a 
success, the character gains +2FP to their maximum for 2 days. 
 
 
Gambling 
The characters have the opportunity to engage in a bit of 
gambling. A game master may have this encounter play out in 
several ways, but some ideas may include: 
 
The party come across a gambling house where they can 
engage in dice and card games. 
 
The party come across a cockfight, badger bait horse 
race or other grisly blood sport that can be betted on. 
 
The party come across a fight, perhaps organised or 
perhaps impromptu. Either way, someone is offering 
odds on the winner. 
 
Hard Day’s Work 
Some or all of the party have the opportunity to engage in their 
profession. Those affected party members have the opportunity to 
gain their earnings as based on their Status.  
 
Hot Bath 
The party come across the opportunity to have a proper clean. A 
game master may have this encounter play out in several ways, but 
some ideas may include: 
 
A barber surgeon has a stool in the street and is offering 
a hair cut and shave.  
 
The party come across a formal bathhouse where they 
can pay (significantly) to have a team of beauticians 
scrub them down, exfoliate their skin, trim their beards, 
etc. 
 
An old crone has had her rather burly sons haul a couple 
of tin tubs out into the street and has filled them with 
river water. She’s currently heating the water and 
offering passersby a quick dip.  
 
 


Encounters 
113 
 
Illness 
Someone in the party, determined randomly, contracts an illness. 
Roll on the table to determine what illness they have contracted: 
ILLNESS TABLE 
D4 
Disease 
1 
The Green Flu 
2 
The Dripping Anvil 
3-4 
The Stinging Trots 
 
See the Sickness and Disease descriptions on page 191 for 
information on how to adjudicate these illnesses. 
 
Lepers 
The streets clear and the sound of mournful bells ring out as a 
group of 1d6+6 lepers (use the statblock for beggars) shuffles into 
view. Each leper demands three pfennigs from the characters. Each 
character must succeed an immediate hard (-3) save vs END or 
gain 1d3 grime points. 
 
Militia Training 
The party come across a group of local militia men or members of 
the watch engaging in training. A game master may have this 
encounter play out in several ways, but some ideas may include: 
 
A grisly old captain of the guard wants the characters to 
show his new recruits how to fight by sparring with 
them. 
 
A mercenary captain his cleaning and retooling his 
company’s handguns, perhaps offering to sell some. 
 
A watch man is explaining to his new partner how crime 
and poverty works in this area of town. 
 
Investment 
The characters come across the opportunity to invest in a business. 
A game master may have this encounter play out in several ways, 
but some ideas may include: 
 
The party meet a street hawker who wants them to invest 
in his river barge used to fish for eels. 
 
The party meet someone while having lunch. They want 
them to invest in their key cutting and shoe repair 
business. 
 
The party are approached by a group of dwarfs who are 
prospecting for gold, coal, iron or other mineable 
resource. 
 
Mistaken Identity 
Someone in the party, determined at random, is mistaken for 
someone else. A game master may have this encounter play out in 
several ways, but some ideas may include: 
 
The character is assumed to have committed a crime (see 
the False Accusation event). 
 
Someone believes the character is someone else who has 
just inherited an enormous wealth (perhaps they are 
ushered somewhere for a will-reading). 
 
An old crone is convinced a character is their long-lost 
child. Perhaps their child died years ago, is off fighting in 
a distant war, or become a mutant as a teenager and fled 
into the woods. 
 
 
 
Ogre Mercenaries 
The party meet 1d4 ogre brutes during the day. The game master 
should roll appropriate reaction rolls for the ogres. There is all 
manner of ways that the ogres may interact with the characters. 
Ideas include: 
 
They are hungry, and will attempt to mug the party for 
their food. 
 
They attempt to make the characters into their next meal 
 
They looking for work as fighters and will fight for the 
party if the pay is good enough. 
 
They have taken to eating vagrants. 
 
Old Friend 
One individual in the party, determined at random, suddenly meets 
an old friend, acquaintance or family member. This can be used by 
game masters to draw extra NPCs into the action or merely add 
colour to the character’s own presence in the city. 
Pedlar 
A strange individual wearing coloured rags and carrying several 
wicker baskets approaches the party. The pedlar has any common 
item for sale at double the price, as well as the following items: 
▶ Lucky Charm: 8d. This charm may be a piece of magic heather, 
a lucky rabbit’s foot or other such charm. A character wearing this 
may re-roll any test, the charm then crumbles. One use. 
▶ Holy Pendant: 1gp. The pedlar is selling something pertaining 
to be a holy relic of power. Its presence will allow the wearer to 
automatically ignore the damage of one successful enemy attack on 
them, then the pendent crumbles to dust. One use. 
 
Pickpocket 
One individual in the party, determined at random, is 
pickpocketed. The thief takes one slot (determined at random) that 
the character is using to carry coins. The character loses all the 
coins in the slot. An immediate save vs INT allows them to realise 
have been robbed. 
 
 


Encounters 
114 
 
Political Agitation 
A political agitator stands in the streets shouting about how the 
upper classes are parasitic on the peasant classes. The theme of 
their rhetoric may be one of the following: 
 
The upper class need to be removed, perhaps by force. 
 
The monarchical system used by kings and queens is 
unfair – the people should get a vote. 
 
Society would be better off if everyone lived in worker-
communes. 
 
The church is a joke and needs serious (if not total) 
reform. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
‘That’s the fing ‘bout nobles – 
no manners, don’t care about 
anyone but themselves. That’s 
why I reckon we rise up, stove 
their ‘eads in and set up shop in 
their shiny palaces. That’d make 
the world fairer for sure.’ 
 
 
 
Gunter Mannjaeger, agitator-leader 
of the League of Benevolent 
Uprisings. 
 
 
 
 
 
Pompous Nobility 
The characters come across 1d3 nobles. The game master should 
use appropriate reaction rolls for the nobles, but the encounter 
may play out in several ways, such as: 
 
A character (or characters) is shoved into the mud to 
make way for a palanquin or carriage. 
 
The character accidentally barges into a noble or pushes 
them into the mud. 
 
The nobles are with 2d6 guards and decide to harass or 
maybe even mug the characters. 
 
Preacher 
The party come across a street preacher who is delivering a sermon 
on some aspect of his/her faith. The game master should use 
appropriate reaction rolls for the priest, but the encounter may 
play out in several ways, such as: 
 
They believe everyone should dispose of their worldly 
goods, including their clothing, starting now. 
 
The preacher believes they have had a vision of the 
party’s future (perhaps they have) – the game master 
may call for appropriate pray tests to convey the vision. 
 
The preacher is sincere and wants a member of the party 
to join the clergy (an easy opportunity to allow someone 
to become a friar/nun or even an ordained priest). 
 
Priests of Stone 
The characters see a procession of high priests of the dwarf 
religion. They are carrying great carved runestones and stone 
sarcophagi. A character may bow their head and test Pray to see if 
these dwarf gods are listening. The test is hard (-3) for non-dwarf 
characters. On a success, the character doubles their natural 
healing rate for a week.  
 
 
 
 
Reward 
The party come across a bounty board or other such opportunity 
for reward in a dangerous line of work. There are many 
possibilities as to what they may see, but some suggestions are: 
 
Goblins are infesting the sewers. The watch is offering 
money for each goblin ear turned in. 
 
There is a wanted murderer/witch/heretic/mutant on the 
loose and there is a significant bounty on their heads. 
 
The woods nearby are thick with forestborn. There is a 
bounty on their heads. 
 
Riot 
A riot breaks out in the settlement. Perhaps it is as a result of 
political agitation, difficulty securing food or some other pressure 
on the population. Either way, looting and violence are quickly 
spreading. The party may get caught up in the chaos, using it as a 
cover to complete some other goal or generally letting off steam by 
robbing places and getting into fisticuffs with the guard. 
 
Runaway 
The party come across a person or creature that is attempting to 
run away from their current life. A game master may have this 
encounter play out in several ways, but some ideas may include: 
 
The woman who is betrothed to someone she does not 
love. The man is in tow with a group of his angry family 
members. 
 
A dog, roundly mistreated by its owner has rushed to a 
member of the party for help. 
 
1d6 x 10 cows have escaped their paddock and are on 
the loose. 
 
Session 
Members of the party decide to have an alcoholic beverage with a 
meal, perhaps elevenses or lunch. Each member of the party must 
test Consume Alcohol. On a failure, they decide to have a few 
more drinks and the day quickly descends into a session of heavy 
drinking. The game master may wish to use the Taverns and 
Carousing section of this book to help adjudicate the rest of the 
day. 
 
Temple Donation 
The party come across a temple or shrine to their respective gods 
and goddesses. Filled with an urge to show their piety, each 
character must test Composure or go and donate 1d4 shillings to 
their temple. If they do donate, allow them to test Pray. On a 
failure, their gods aren’t listening, but on a success, they gain 250 x 
their level experience points. They can only gain this benefit once 
per week. 
 
Watch Press Gang 
The party are approached by 1d6+6 friendly guards. Unbeknownst 
to the party, the settlement’s watch is low on manpower and 
therefore these guards are going to try and press them into service 
for 1d4 weeks.  
 
Witch Burning 
A group of witch hunters, guards or other concerned citizens have 
formed a ring and are preparing to burn a witch or a mutant. It 
may be that the person is clearly innocent (or clearly guilty). Either 
way, the party may wish to wait and watch the proceedings unfold. 
 


 
115 
 
Taverns and Carousing
Within a campaign of grubby, grimy fantasy characters are more 
often than not down-and-out losers, vagrants and general weirdos. 
For this reason, it is more than likely that characters will spend time 
in a tavern, burning away their last few coppers on cheap booze and 
other seedier activities. To support this aspect of the genre, the 
following rules are provided to help game masters adjudicate 
moments of play in which characters decide to go carousing whether 
that’s spending the evening in a single tavern or having a crazy night 
out on the town. 
 
Drunk Limit 
Every character has a drunk limit listed on their sheet which is an 
abstract measure of how well they resist the effects of alcohol. 
 
To calculate a character’s drunk limit, use the following 
information. All characters have a base drunk limit of 10 plus any 
additional modifiers from below. 
 
DRUNK LIMIT MODIFIERS 
Descriptor 
Drunk Limit Modifier 
Trained or Journeyman in END 
+1 
Expert or Master in END 
+2 
Grandmaster in END 
+3 
Trained in Consume Alcohol 
+1 
Journeyman in Consume Alcohol 
+2 
Expert in Consume Alcohol 
+3 
Master in Consume Alcohol 
+4 
Grandmaster in Consume Alcohol 
5 
 
 
The Tavern System 
When the characters enter a tavern for a period of extended 
carousing, the game master should follow the below procedure for 
a tavern round, which consists of 30 minute periods. 
1. Buy a Round 
 
a. Purchase food or drink 
 
b. Consume the drink 
 
c. Roll Past Your Limit Checks 
2. Roll for Tavern Encounters 
3. Turn in for the Night 
 
1. Buy a Round 
1a. Purchase food or drink 
Everyone may purchase a food and/or drink item now from the 
tavern menu. All food and drink have a drink value which for 
most items varies slightly each time the character consumes it. 
Some food will even lower the drink value, preventing someone 
from getting too drunk. Each time a character imbibes a drink or 
eats some food, note down its drink value and add it to their 
consumed alcohol for that session of carousing. This will be used 
to determine whether they are past their limit or not. 
 
FOOD AND DRINK TABLE 
Food/Drink 
Cost 
Drink Value 
Ale, Flagon 
3d 
+1d3 
Dwarf Ale, Flagon 
5d 
+2d6* 
Wine (Common), Gill 
6d 
+1d4 
Wine (Fine), Gill 
2s 
+1d4 
Wine (Elven), Gill 
3s 
+1d4 
Spirits (Vodka, Schnapps, etc), Dram 
1s 
+1d4 
Meal (Basic) 
6d 
-1d4 
Meal (Moderate) 
1s 
-1d4 
Meal (Lavish) 
10s 
-1d3 
*This is only 1d3 for dwarf characters. 
 
1b. Consume the drink 
The character consumes the drink or eats the food, adding or 
subtracting the drink value from their currently consumed alcohol. 
A character who does not wish to drink, simply ignores this step 
and can either go straight to step 2 or 3. 
 
1c. Past Your Limit Checks 
A character can consume as much alcohol as they like, but if they 
ever have a total higher than their drink limit then they must test 
Consume Alcohol. On a success, they are fine but, on a failure, 
they are considered past their limit and must roll 1d8 on the table 
below, adding how much over their drunk limit they are: 
 
PAST YOUR LIMIT 
1d8 
Result 
1 
You immediately fall asleep, awake after an extended 
rest with 1d4 drained conditions. 
2 
You immediately buy 1d10 nearby patrons a drink. 
3 
Collapse onto the floor. You fall over, suffering 2 
bludgeoning damage. 
4 
You become angry and lash out at random nearby 
person for 1 turn, attempting to hurt them. 
5 
You vomit over yourself. You will awake after an 
extended rest with 1d4 drained conditions. 
6 
You are going to be quite badly hungover.  
You will awake after an extended rest with 1d4+1 
drained conditions. 
7 
You are going to be exceptionally hungover. You will 
awake after an extended rest with 2d4 drained 
conditions and find 1d20% of your carried wealth 
missing. 
8+ 
Oh dear. The character engages in a series of escapades. 
Every player in the group may add a detail to the story 
as to what this character gets up to while drunk for the 
rest of the night. The character awakes with 3d4 
drained conditions and loses 1d100% of their carried 
wealth. 
 
3. Roll for Tavern Encounters 
As the evening draws on, the characters may have a tavern 
encounter. Each character taking part in the carousal must roll a 
d4, with a ‘1’ indicating that they have a tavern encounter. The 
encounter are generated by 1d10 with a bonus equal to the total 
consumed drink value for that carousing session. The table of 
encounter may be modified as a game master sees fit for their 
current campaign world. 
 
4. Turn in for the Night 
The character bows out the evenings revelries, returns to their 
lodgings and goes to sleep. Other characters go back to the start of 
the Tavern round.

Tavern Event Descriptions 
Below are the event descriptions. All of these are suggestions, and 
the game master may wish to flesh out the description with greater 
roleplaying detail.  
 
A Quiet Drink 
The character sits by themselves and absorbs the atmosphere. They 
gain +1HP in natural healing at the end of the next extended rest. 
 
 
 
 
 
Animal Trainer 
Either inside the tavern, or directly outside, a travelling animal 
trainer has set up a ring. Roll on the table below to see what 
animals have been brought: 
 
ANIMAL TRAINER TABLE 
D6 
Result 
1 
The animal trainer has brought a brown bear which 
characters may offer to fight for 5 rounds for a hefty 
reward using only a spear given to them by the animal 
trainer. 
3 
The animal trainer has brought a wolverine which 
characters may offer to fight for 5 rounds for a hefty 
reward using only a spear given to them by the animal 
trainer. 
4 
The animal trainer has brought a pair of large dogs which 
characters may offer to fight for 5 rounds for a hefty 
reward using only a dagger given to them by the animal 
trainer. 
5 
The animal trainer has brought a pair of fighting 
cockerels. The characters may bet on the outcome of the 
fight. 
6 
The animal trainer has brought a badger that will be 
baited by a series of dogs. The characters may bet on the 
outcome of the fight. 
 
Arm Wrestling 
Someone in the pub begins to organise an arm-wrestling 
competition. Any character may enter, and any character may bet 
on any other character, including themselves. In addition, 1d4 
other patrons also join the competition. Other patrons have a STR 
of Journeyman. Everyone betting can bet up to 10 shillings, with 
winnings equal to their original bet x the number of competitors. 
To run the competition, use the following rules: 
 
Each player is paired with another competitor. One of the players 
rolls an opposed STR test with the stronger character being the 
‘attacker’. If the competitors are of the same STR then randomly 
determine who should be the ‘attacker’. 
 
 
 


Taverns and Carousing 
117 
 
Bloke Down the Pub 
The character is offered a dubious product by a shady man in the 
tavern. The character must save vs PER to avoid buying the 
useless junk. On a success they shuffle off, on a failure they 
purchase the item that costs 1d10 shillings. On a critical failure, 
they purchase 1d6 copies of the item. The next morning, they 
realise it is worthless. 
 
Brooding Berserkers 
1d4 dwarf berserkers are sat supping their ale. They generally 
want to be left alone but a character of sufficient strength (usually 
at least Expert) may manage to get their attention. The berserkers 
may offer to have an Arm Wrestling competition with the character. 
Such NPCs are useful contacts and, as well-travelled adventurers, 
may be able to offer advice to the character. 
 
Bully Boy 
The character becomes the subject of ridicule from a bully boy (use 
statistics for a thug). The only way to stop the harassment 
peacefully is to Turn in for the Evening. 
 
Drinking Contest 
Any character may enter, and any character may bet on any other 
character, including themselves. In addition, 1d4 other patrons also 
join the competition. The other patrons have a Consume Alcohol 
skill of Journeyman. Everyone betting can bet up to 10 shillings, 
with winnings equal to their original bet x the number of 
competitors. To run the competition, use the following rules: 
 
Each player tests Consume alcohol simultaneously. If someone 
fails the Consume alcohol test, then they are eliminated from the 
competition. This is repeated until there is only one competitor left, 
who is the winner, claiming the prize money. Everyone who takes 
part adds 1 to their total consumed drink value for each round they 
were in the competition. 
 
Dwarf Drinking Contest 
Any character may enter, and any character may bet on any other 
character, including themselves. In addition, 1d4 other patrons also 
join the competition. The other patrons have a Consume Alcohol 
skill of Master. Everyone betting can bet up to 10 gold pieces, with 
winnings equal to their original bet x the number of competitors. 
To run the competition, use the following rules: 
 
Each player tests Consume alcohol simultaneously. If someone 
fails the Consume alcohol test then they are eliminated from the 
competition. This is repeated until there is only one competitor left, 
who is the winner, claiming the prize money. Everyone who takes 
part adds 1 to their total consumed drink value for each round they 
were in the competition. 
 
Dwarf Engineer 
A dwarf engineer enters the tavern. It is clear what their profession 
from the sheer volume of brass piping, buckles and strange 
firearms about their person. Characters engaging with the engineer 
using Gossip may gain the following insights: 
 
The engineer may discuss their recent projects. The 
character may gain +1 Craft (Machinery). 
 
The engineer may show off one of their whirring 
clockwork guns. The character may gain +1 Ranged 
(Mechanical) 
 
They may be willing to sell a character one of their guns 
(choose from the Ranged (Mechanical) table on p.96). 
 
Dwarf Prince 
A member of the dwarf royal family enters the tavern. It is likely 
that the prince is in disguise (though this may be poor). Characters 
may attempt Status or Gossip tests to engage the prince in 
conversation. This person would be a powerful ally for the 
character. 
 
 


Taverns and Carousing 
118 
 
Dwarf Shatranj 
A dwarf in the tavern offers to play a friendly game of Shatranj 
with the character. If the character decides to play, they sit down 
and have a quiet conversation. Roll 1d6 on the following table: 
 
DWARF SHATRANJ TABLE 
D6 
Result 
1 
The character is thrashed by the dwarf and offers to buy 
the next round for the dwarf as a forfeit. This costs 6d. 
2-5 
The character gives the dwarf a run for his money. They 
have a good talk. 
6 
The character thrashes the dwarf and he offers to buy 
the character the next round as a forfeit. 
 
Dwarf Singing 
Several dwarfs in the tavern begin a mournful dirge of ages lost 
and gone. Any character may join in the singing, testing Perform 
(Singing) if they do so. On a success their singing should impress 
the dwarfs, and they will offer to pay for the character’s drinks for 
the rest of the evening. On a failure, they are insulted by the 
terrible singing. The character must immediately leave the tavern or 
Turn in for the Night out of shame.  
 
Dwile Flonking 
The character gets caught up in a game of ‘dwile flonking’, a 
favourite game of peasants and halflings. They are currently on and 
must throw the wet rag at one of the dancers. Test Ranged (any). 
On a success the character hits someone. Uproarious laughter 
issues from everyone. The players offer to buy the character their 
next drink. On a failure, nothing happens. 
 
Game of Cards 
Any character may enter, and any character may bet on any other 
character, including themselves. In addition, 1d4 other patrons also 
join the competition. The other patrons have a Gambling of 
Trained. Everyone betting can bet up to 10 shillings, with 
winnings equal to their original bet x the number of competitors. 
To run the competition, use the following rules: 
 
Each character must test Gambling simultaneously. If someone 
fails, then they are eliminated from the competition. This is 
repeated until there is only one competitor left. 
 
Inebriated Aristocrat 
The character tests Gossip. On a success they engage the drunken 
aristocrat in conversation. The aristocrat buys the next drink, no 
matter the cost. If the character critically succeeded the Gossip 
test then the aristocrat gifts them a valuable token such as a ring, 
brooch or other favour (worth 1d6gp). On a failure, the character is 
ignored or maybe even mocked. 
 
Interesting Substance 
A fellow reveller offers the character the opportunity to partake in 
a substance that they do not recognise. If the character agrees, then 
they should roll 1d4 on the table below to see the effects of the 
substance. 
 
INTERESTING SUBSTANCE TABLE 
D4 
Result 
1 
Something goes horribly wrong. The character is sick. 
Their total consumed alcohol reduces by 2d4 and they 
must save vs END. On a failure they did not make it to 
the privies and instead did it on the alehouse floor. The 
landlord is likely to charge the character around 2d6 
pfennigs to clean up the mess. 
2 
The character has a great time – the character’s awakes 
with +6FP after the next extended rest (even if this takes 
them above their normal maximum). 
3 
The character realises they are an incredibly charming 
individual. For 1 day they are considered grandmaster in 
Charm, Deception, and Persuasion.  
4 
Wow. Everything aligns for the character – they gain an 
average miscellaneous experience point reward 
appropriate to their level. 
 
 
 
Knight Errant 
Members of a prestigious chivalric order enter the tavern. The 
knight(s) may be engaged in conversation using Status or Gossip 
tests. If a character particularly ingratiates themselves with the 
knights, then they may offer to show the character a thing or two 
about riding and fighting on horseback (culminating in the 
character receiving the Equestrian or Chevalier talent. 
 
Mercenary Company 
Members of a wandering mercenary company enter the tavern. The 
characters do not need to interact with them, but it’s clear that the 
mercenaries are between jobs and may be hired as followers. 
 
Roll 1d6 to see which mercenaries have wandered into the tavern. 
The listed hire cost is per week and may be open to negotiation. 
 
MERCENARY COMPANY TABLE 
D6 
Result 
Hire Cost (each) 
1-2 
1d6 Guards 
4s 
3 
1d6 Halfling Rangers 
4s 
4 
1d6 Dwarf Warriors 
8s 
5 
1d3 Ogre Brutes 
2gp 
6 
1d3 Dwarf Berserkers 
5gp 
 
Old Friend 
The character meets an old friend or someone else from their past. 
The game master can use this encounter to work details of the 
character’s backstory into the unfolding plot. 
 
Other News 
The character overhears something relevant to the party’s current 
task or quest. The game master may wish to use Gossip tests to 
further add colour to this situation. 
 
Pickpocket 
The character has their pocket picked. They must save vs INT; on 
a success they notice who it was. On a failure, the pickpocket gets 
away unnoticed with 1d10 shillings. 
 
  


Taverns and Carousing 
119 
Professional Tattoo Artist

character must save vs PER or get a tattoo. Roll on the location 
chart and then on the chart for what it says:

Lover 
Pursuing Romance 
A young man or woman has taken a romantic interest in the 
character. The young man or woman will do their utmost best to 
ingratiate themselves with the character, doing their best to get 
their romantic attention, at least for this evening. The next day, the 
game master may wish to run the Betrothal settlement event if the 
character was unable to avoid the advances of this young man or 
woman. 
Sick 
The character has drunk too much and is sick. Their total 
consumed alcohol reduces by 2d4 and they must save vs END. 
On a failure they did not make it to the privies and instead did it 
on the alehouse floor. The landlord is likely to charge the character 
around 2d6 pfennigs to clean up the mess. 
Spilled Dwarf Pint 
The character knocks over a dwarf’s pint. The character must make 
a very hard (-6) charm or persuasion test. On a failure the 
individual becomes angry. The game master may which to adjust 
this based on the character’s response – if they are sorry or offer to 
purchase a new drink, they may wish to change a failure to a 
success. 
Spilled Pint 
The character knocks over someone’s pint. The character must test 
charm or persuasion. On a failure the individual becomes angry. 
The game master may which to adjust this based on the character’s 
response – if they are sorry or offer to purchase a new drink, they 
may wish to change a failure to a success. 
The Graf Himself 
The ruling lord of this village enters the tavern. It is likely that the 
lord is in disguise (though this may be poor). Characters may 
attempt Status or Gossip tests to engage the lord in conversation. 
This person would be a powerful ally for the character. 
The Graf’s Men 
2d6 soldiers of a local noble’s household enter the pub. Make a 
reaction roll for the character, adding +1 if the character is of 10th 
level or higher. Use the below, modified reaction roll table. 
THE GRAF’S MEN REACTION ROLL TABLE 
2d6 
Result 
2 
Critical Failure: The soldiers take a dislike to the 
characters(s) being not just in their pub but their town 
generally. They quickly start a fight with the intention 
of running them out of town. 
3-5
Hostile and Threatening: The soldiers take a strong 
dislike to the characters being in their tavern. They 
knock over drinks and generally try to start a fight. 
6-8
Cautious and Curious: The soldiers visibly notice the 
character(s) presence but generally leave them alone. 
10-11
Friendly: The soldiers are affable and friendly, perhaps 
offering to buy the character(s) a drink and make 
pleasant enquiries about their business. 
12 
Critical Success: The soldiers take a real liking to the 
character(s), perhaps they recognise them from 
elsewhere. They pay for their drinks for the rest of the 
evening and generally join the groups of revellers 
together. 


Taverns and Carousing 
120 
 
 
The Watch 
1d6+6 guards enter the tavern due to the raucous behaviour of the 
revellers (or some other real or imagined crime). Each character in 
the tavern must save vs PER. On a failure the watch takes 
unkindly to the character and arrests them on a charge of 
troublemaking, probably throwing them in the settlement’s strong 
house for the night. On a success, they recognise the character, 
apologise, but insist the establishment must close for the evening. 
The character must Turn in for the Night.  
 
Trappers 
A group of trappers enter the tavern after having spent time in the 
wilderness. With successful Gossip and Charm tests a character 
can engage the trappers, who are likely to want to interact in the 
following ways: 
 
Sharing stories of their greatest hunt – this may be a 
magical creature. 
 
Show the character how they once escaped a 
bear/wolverine by covering themselves in lard and 
slipping from the animals grasp (maybe they teach the 
character how to do this!). 
 
How to catch mink or using snares that don’t damage the 
pelt. 
 
How to train dogs to hunt particular animals such as 
beavers or badgers. 
Travelling Merchant 
A travelling merchant enters the tavern. The details of this person 
are up to the game master but characters using gossip to engage 
them will quickly realise that they’re looking for someone to invest 
in their business. Generally the merchant is looking for someone to 
invest 3d6 gold pieces in their business. If a character agrees to do 
so then they may check on their investment whenever they arrive 
in a large town or city that has financial institutions such as guilds 
or banks. A character may check on their investment once per 
week as a maximum, rolling 1d12 on the following table: 
 
TRAVELLING MERCHANT TABLE 
D12 
Result 
1 
The company has gone bust, and the character no 
longer has an investment. 
2-10 
The character claims 4d10 shillings. 
11-12 
Business is booming, the character claims 1d6gp 
 
University Students 
The character crosses paths with a very drunk student. Roll 1d4 
with on the following table to see what event unfolds with them, 
then consult the relevant encounter. 
 
UNIVERSITY STUDENTS TABLE 
D4 
Result 
1 
Spilled Pint 
2 
Drinking Contest 
3 
Bully Boy 
4 
Inebriated Noble 
 
Wandering Wizard 
A powerful wizard enters the tavern. Characters may attempt 
Status or Gossip tests to engage the wizard in conversation. This 
person would be a powerful ally for the character. 
 
You’ve Hand Enough 
In the establishment where the character is drinking the bar staff 
refuse to sell them any more booze tonight on account of their 
obvious drunkenness. The character does not need to Turn in for 
the Night but cannot purchase any more drinks here tonight. 
 
 


Mass Combat 
121 
 
Mass Combat
In many games it will be necessary at some time to adjudicate the 
combat of dozens, hundreds or even millions of individuals. JEM 
provides three possible systems for this type of combat that game 
masters can utilise. 
 
The Skirmish Engine 
The skirmish engine is the simplest form of mass combat offered 
by Mud, Blood & Rats. It is meant to be used when the party will 
be engaged in a large combat with up to around 20-40 combatants 
and you wish to ‘play it out’ perhaps on a battle map or other 
gridded surface. Combats of any greater size should really be 
played using the Battle Engine or War Engine. 
 
The primary goal of the Skirmish Engine is to simplify combat in 
order to make it faster. This naturally means that much of the 
subtlety usually meant for the normal combat system is removed 
for the sake of speed. 
 
A combat using the Skirmish Engine plays out exactly like a normal 
combat does, with the following rules changes: 
▶ Hit Points and Damage: A creature’s hit points and the damage 
it causes are all divided by four and rounded up. This means a 
creature with 1-4 hit points has 1 hit point while a creature that has 
5-8 hit points now has 2 hit points.  
▶ Armour Saves: A creature’s armour save for its body becomes 
its only armour save and hit locations are not rolled for in combat – 
they all hit the body. Critical hits double the damage and nothing 
else. 
▶ Block/Dodge/Parry: No one may perform blocks, dodges, or 
parries, except the player characters and it costs them 1FP to do 
so.  
▶ Test and Save Resolution: All tests and saves are handled with 
a d6, instead of a d20. The target numbers for proficiency level are 
listed below: U: (6+); T: (5+); J: (4+); E: (3+); M: (2+); G: (1+). 
These target numbers are unmodified by the level of the creature. 
A natural 1 is still a critical failure and a natural 6 is still a critical 
success.  
▶ Modifiers to Tests: Certain talents and abilities may modify 
tests. The bonus or penalty that they usually give to a d20 is 
divided by 3, rounded up (so a +3 bonus becomes a +1) for 
example). Player characters burning FP must now spend 3 FP to 
gain per +1 bonus. 
▶ Opposed Tests: If you must adjudicate opposed tests in this 
system, use the following table. A character suffers a -1 penalty for 
each size category smaller it is than its opponent when making 
opposed STR, END and AGI tests. Opposingly, it gains a +1 bonus 
for each size category larger it is than its opponent when making 
opposed STR, END and AGI tests. 
 
SKIRMISH ENGINE OPPOSED TESTS 
 
 
ATTACKER 
 
 
U 
T 
J 
E 
M 
G 
DEFENDER

4+ 
3+ 
2+ 
2+

4+

Example Skirmish Engine Statblocks 
The example statblocks have been adapted from the usual 
statblocks to show you how they might look if you were to use 
them in a Skirmish Engine. Similarly, it is encouraged that should 
you expect to use the Skirmish Engine during a session of Mud, 
Blood & Rats then you would be well-served pre-preparing such 
statblocks to ease the running of the game. 
 
Dragon – Level 24 
Type: Monster 
Size: Gargantuan 
HP: 9  Composure: G (1+)  Move: 40’ (60’ fly) 
STR: G (1+); AGI: M (2+); END: G (1+); INT: E (3+); PER: M (2+) 
Skills: Melee: G (1+) 
Resistances: Slashing, Piercing and Bludgeoning damaging. 
Actions:  
▶ 2 x Claws Attack; 1 x Bite: Melee (1+); 3 slashing, rend (1);  
Melee (1+); 3 piercing, rend (2). 
▶ Flaming Breath: [Recharge 5+]: The dragon exudes a 55’ cone 
of fire. All creatures in the cone take 5 fire damage, rend (2). A 
character may attempt a dodge test to halve the damage. 
Armour Save: 2+ 
Dramatic Actions: 5/round 
▶ 1 x Claws Attack: Melee (1+); 3 slashing rend (1). 
▶ 1 x Tail Whip Attack: Melee (1+); 3 bludgeoning, and the target

to make. It can do this five times per day. 
▶ Flying Vitality: If this creature is at half hit points or fewer, it 
cannot fly. 
▶ Superior Crush: This creature may walk over other creatures 
smaller than itself. If it does so, the creature that is stepped on suffers 
2 bludgeoning damage, rend (2). This ability may only affect a 
creature once per round. 
▶ Terrifying: Seeing this creature is utterly terrifying. When this 
creature is first viewed, the viewer must succeed a very hard (-2) 
Composure test or gain a frightened condition.

Mass Combat 
122 
 
The Battle Engine 
The Battle Engine is a set of rules that allows a game master run 
exciting battles with lots of choices on both sides of the conflict. It 
is best utilised in situations where the game master wishes the 
players to have individual choices regarding the placement of 
troops and the specific attacks and actions of individual units of 
soldiery.  
 
Units 
Within a Battle Engine combat, all combatants are organised into 
units, such as ‘20 orcs’ or ‘100 trolls’ and the statistics are 
calculated from the base creature as follows: 
 
▶ 1. Calculate Unit HP: Take the creature’s original HP and multiply 
it by the number of creatures in the unit. Then divide the total by 
the size modifier of the creatures in the unit, as stated below: 
 
Unit Hit Point Size Modifier 
▶ Tiny: divide by 5 
▶ Small, Medium and Large: divide by 4 
▶ Huge: divide by 3 
▶ Gargantuan: divide by 2 
 
▶ 2. Calculate Unit Armour Save: Take the creature’s armour save 
for its body hit location. This becomes the unit’s armour save as hit 
location rolls are not made in the Battle Engine. 
 
▶ 3. Calculate Unit Battle Damage: Take the creature’s original 
damage for each of its attacks and multiply it by the number of 
creatures in the unit. Then divide the total by the size modifier of 
the creatures in the unit, as stated below: 
 
Unit Damage Size Modifier 
▶ Tiny: divide by 10 
▶ Small, Medium and Large: divide by 5 
▶ Huge: divide by 2 
▶ Gargantuan: Do not divide 
 
▶ 4. Calculate Unit Wavering Limit: When a unit in a Battle Engine 
combat has lost half of its starting hit points it is wavering. A 
wavering unit only causes half of its original battle damage until it 
has left the battle and has been restored to full fighting strength. 
This can be represented by the WV initials in the top line of the 
statblock.  
 
▶ 5. Calculate Unit Speed: In a Battle Engine combat, each round 
becomes 10 minutes long. To calculate the speed of a unit, multiply 
its usual speed by 10. 
 
▶ 6. Calculate Power of Other Abilities: Some abilities, such as a 
troll’s ability to regenerate of a priest’s ability to cast healing 
invocations need to be upscaled to see the benefit of it. In this case, 
multiply the original number by the level of the creature with it.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
Area of Effect Attacks 
Sometimes a creature has an area of effect attack, such as a 
dragon’s breath. In these cases, calculate the number of medium 
sized creatures this attack could affect, and multiply the base 
damage by this to find the battle damage. Don’t forget to apply the 
Unit Damage Size Modifier. 
Units With Mixed Creatures 
Sometimes a unit may contain mixed types of creatures. A unit with 
mixed creatures may still only take one action per round. 
▶ HP: When calculating HP, simply calculate the Unit HP for the 
different creatures then add them together.  
▶ Damage: Calculate the damage for each of the creatures’ attacks 
and list them separately in the statblock. 
▶ Armour Save: The unit’s armour save becomes the body save of 
the majority of the creatures. So, a unit with 20 orcs with an armour 
save of 4+, accompanied by 10 knights with an armour save of 2+ 
would have a unit armour save of 4+. 
▶ Composure: Becomes equal to the best Composure value of all 
creatures in the unit. 
▶ Movement: Becomes the speed of the slowest moving creature in 
the unit. 
▶ Saves: If a unit is called upon to make a save, use the core 
attribute of the most common creature in the group.  
 
Units with Player Characters 
Obviously, it is possible that player characters will join units. If this 
is done, then the unit is modified in the following way: 
▶ HP: Add the character’s HP directly to the unit’s HP.  
▶ Damage: A character may add their level as bonus damage to one 
attack the unit makes per round. 
▶ Armour Save: The unit’s armour save is unchanged by the 
presence of a player character. 
▶ Composure: The unit gains a +1 bonus to compisure tests for 
each player character present. 
▶ Actions: Each round, character present may take an action instead 
of the unit taking an action, such a character casting cure wounds 
on his comrades or casting fireball into an advancing unit of orcs. 
 
Example Battle Engine Statblocks 
The example statblocks have been adapted from the usual 
statblocks to show you how they might look if you were to use 
them in a Battle Engine. Similarly, it is encouraged that should you 
expect to use the Battle Engine during a session of Mud, Blood & 
Rats then you would be well-served pre-preparing such statblocks 
to ease the running of the game.

▶ Superior Crush: This creature may walk over other creatures

▶ Terrifying: Seeing this creature is utterly terrifying. When this 
creature is first viewed, the viewer must succeed a very hard (-6)

123

▶ Orc Bow Attack: Ranged (15); 6 piercing (120’ range).
