Combat in the Battle Engine 
Combat takes place in a number of rounds during a battle engine 
conflict. However, unlike the usual combat round, one usually 
represents 10 minutes. A game master may decide to increase this, 
to one hour, one day or even more. Just remember that in doing 
so, you must increase the calculated movement rate of units if you 
do this. 
 
The Battle Engine Round 
A Battle Engine round is broken into the following Steps each round: 
 
Combat Steps Each Round 
1. Declare Actions Step 
2. Initiative Step 
3. Move Step 
4. Missile Attacks Step. 
5. Supernatural Abilities Step 
6. Melee Attacks Step 
7. Other Actions Step 
 
1. Declare Actions Step 
During this step all the players declare what their units intend to do 
that round, such as ‘I will send my heavy infantry up into those hills’ 
or ‘I will hang back and try and shoot at those advancing orcs from 
the castle walls’. While these descriptions can be fairly broad, the 
game master should insist on a few things: attacks must be declared 
as either Missile or Melee as well as the intended target; spells being 
cast must be specified (it is not enough to declare ‘I will cast a spell’ 
but the spell to be cast must be stated); all supernatural abilities must 
be declared. 
 
2. Initiative Step 
In this step, the players roll 1d6 and the game master rolls 1d6 for 
the enemies. The highest roll resolves their declared actions first 
(Steps 3 to 7), then the losing side does the same. A tie should be 
rerolled unless a character as a talent that affects this. 
 
3. Move Step 
Each unit may move up to its listed move value. 
 
4. Missile Attacks Step 
Ranged weapons can now be used, such a bows and pistols. 
 
 
5. Supernatural Abilities Step 
This is when units use special actions that are supernatural. This 
might be a priest’s Banish Undead ability or a wizard casting Bolt. 
 
6. Melee Attacks Step 
Melee weapons may now be used, such as swords and pikes. It is 
also when natural weapons such as claws are most commonly used. 
 
7. Other Actions Step 
This is when actions such as drinking potions, reloading a character’s 
revolver or trying to close the blast doors are resolved. A unit that 
took no other action may also move again, up to their listed move 
value. 
 
 
 
Attacks and Damage 
Below are the rules for making attacks and damaging opponents in 
a Battle Engine combat. 
 
Making an Attack in the Battle Engine 
If a unit has declared they are making an attack, then in the 
appropriate Combat Step they are able to do so. Resolve using the 
following: 
 
1. Make a Challenging (+0) test with the appropriate skill for the 
unit’s weapon (which is listed in the weapon entry) This is called 
the attack test. For example, for attacks with a longsword for 
example, this is the Melee skill. 
 
2. If the attack test is a success, then the attack has scored a hit, 
causing damage. If it has missed, then it causes half its usual damage 
which prompts an armour save (see below). 
 
3. If the attack test was a success, it causes full damage. Either way, 
the unit must make an armour save. Roll 1d6: if the roll meets or 
exceeds the armour save value of the unit, that damage is ignored. 
If it is lower than the armour save value, remove the hit points for 
the damage caused. Unlike normal combat, in the battle engine you 
do not roll for each point of damage caused, instead one d6 is rolled 
for the entire damage and it is either deflected or not.  
 
A natural roll of a 6 means that the attack has been stopped, 
regardless of any other modifiers. If a natural roll of 1 is made, then 
the damage is applied regardless of any other modifiers. 
 
Rend and Power Saves 
Many weapons and creature attacks will list a Rend value, such as 
Rend (1). This is a negative penalty applied to armour saves 
(including cover saves). For example, if a unit has a 4+ armour save 
and is struck by an attack that has Rend (1) then they would need 
to roll a 5+ to succeed. Remember, a natural 6 on an armour save 
always succeeds, regardless of other modifiers (and a natural 1 
always fails). 
 
Sometimes a creature will have access to a power save. This is a 
special type of save that works exactly like armour saves and is 
rolled in addition to armour saves. Any failed armour saves may 
be rerolled if the creature has a power save. Nothing ever modifies 
a power save, under any circumstances and therefore power saves 
cannot be affected by something like the Rend weapon quality. 
 


Mass Combat 
124 
 
Resistances and Vulnerabilities 
▶ Resistance: If a creature has resistance to a damage type then 
any damage it takes from an attack is halved, to a minimum of one. 
For example, if a creature with resistance to slashing damage is hit 
for two points of slashing from a longsword, then one point of 
damage is ignored automatically. The creature then rolls its armour 
saves against the remaining damage. 
▶ Vulnerability: If a creature has vulnerability to a damage type 
then any damage it takes from an attack is doubled. For example, if 
a creature with vulnerability to slashing damage is hit for two 
points of slashing from a longsword, then it must make its armour 
saves against four points of damage. 
 
Wavering, Composure and Destruction 
When a unit is reduced to its wavering limit, it must immediately half 
the value of its damage. In addition, it immediately makes a 
Composure test or breaks and flees as soon as it is able to do so. 
 
Saves 
Oftentimes a unit is called upon to make a save. This is almost 
always to avoid the effects of something unpleasant, such as a spell 
or trap. This will be stated as a ‘save vs’ and then a stated attribute, 
such as a ‘save vs PER’. A save is simply a test taken for that core 
attribute.  
 
Critical Hits 
If the attack roll is a natural 20 then the hit scored is ‘critical’. The 
damage from the attack is doubled. 
 
Block, Dodges and Parries 
A unit may not block, dodge, or parry. 
 
Cover 
Sometimes a unit is in cover, granting an extra save to reduce 
damage if an attack hits. This save is taken before any armour saves 
or power saves and is referred to as a cover save. A cover save is 
affected by anything that affects an armour save and similarly 
always succeeds on a natural 6 and fails on a natural 1. 
 
To benefit from a cover save, a unit must be at least 50% hidden 
from the would-be attackers. Similarly, in most circumstances a unit 
only gains the benefit of a cover save during the Missile Attacks 
Step. The best cover save a unit can hope for is a 4+.  
 
It is ultimately up to the game master as to what constitutes granting 
cover save, and some examples are given below: 
▶ 6+: hedges, plasterboard, etc. 
▶ 5+: ruins, debris, car doors, etc. 
▶ 4+: stone walls, castle battlements, etc. 
 
Surprise 
Sometimes one side in combat has the element of surprise over 
another. It is also possible that both sides blunder into each other 
without warning. When this is the case, the members of the side that 
might be surprised (which could be both) should save vs INT. On a 
failure, they are surprised and can make no actions during the first 
round of combat. 
 
Disengages and Attacks of Opportunity 
When a unit is adjacent to another unit in combat, they can be 
assumed to be ‘engaged’. This means that if an engaged unit leaves 
this adjacency, then the unit they are moving away from may make 
a free attack test with their melee weapons. This is called an attack 
of opportunity. A unit may declare it is using its action for a round 
to disengage, which means it may move away without provoking an 
attack of opportunity, but then it may not use its action to do 
anything else. 
 
 
 
Player Characters in Destroyed Units 
If a player character is in a destroyed unit, they must immediately 
save vs AGI or save vs END (player choice). A failure means that 
they have been badly injured, possibly knocked out and/or taken 
prisoner. If they fail this save, they must immediately save vs END 
again with a successive failure meaning that they have been killed 
in the fighting. If either save is passed, then the character survives 
the destruction of the unit. 
 
The War Engine 
The War Engine is a final set of mass combat rules that a game 
master can use when deciding how to mediate combats involving 
large volumes of troops. The War Engine differs from the Battle 
Engine in that is significantly reduces the player involvement and 
choice – the entire combat is handled by a single opposed dice roll.  
 
Force Value  
The force value is a value added to the combat test made to 
determine the outcome of a battle it. It calculated as follows: 
 
▶ 1. Calculate Force Value for Each Combatant: For each type of 
combatant in a force, multiply the number of them by their level.  
▶ 2. Add Each Combatant Value: Once you have completed step 
1, add all of the values together and then divide that total by 100. 
This will give you the base force value. 
▶ 3. Calculate Bonus Value: Take the base force value and 
divide it by 10. This will give you the bonus value. 
▶ 4. Add Bonus Value: For each of the following statements that 
are true, add the bonus value to the base force value. 
 
Add 1 x bonus value to the base force value for each of the 
following statements that are true. 
▶ Flying: The force contains aerial creatures, vehicles or units. 
▶ Cavalry Support: The force contains at least 10% cavalry units. 
▶ Cavalry Mainstay: The force contains at least 40% cavalry units. 
▶ Missile Support: The force contains at least 10% missile units. 
▶ Missile Mainstay: The force contains at least 40% missile units. 
▶ Missile Army: The force contains at least 80% missile units. 
▶ Monstrous Presence: The force contains at least one creature or 
vehicle of huge size or larger. 
▶ Monstrous Support: The force contains at least 30% creatures 
or vehicles of huge size or greater. 
 
Add 3 x bonus value to the base force value for each of the 
following statements that are true. 
▶ Entirely Flying: Every unit of the force has access to aerial 
vehicles or movement. 
▶ Cavalry Support: The force contains at least 10% armoured 
cavalry units. 
▶ Cavalry Mainstay: The force contains at least 40% cavalry units. 
▶ Artillery Support: At least 10% of the force contains 
creatures/vehicles whose main attack has the siege weapon quality 
(do not include the siege quality conferred on huge and larger 
creatures in this). 
▶ Line Artillery: At least 30% of the force contains 
creatures/vehicles whose main attack has the siege weapon quality 
(do not include the siege quality conferred on huge and larger 
creatures in this). 
 
▶ 5. Add Character Value: For each player character in the army 
add a bonus to the base force value that is equal to their level 
multiplied by their hit points. 
 
Example: Orc Army 
Below is an example of an army statblock created using soldiers 
and ogres. 
 
▶ 5,000 soldiers (halberds): Value: 250 
▶ 5,000 soldiers (handguns): Value: 250 
▶ 1,000 ogre brutes: Value: 80 
Base Force Value = 580 
 
Bonus Value = 58 
+ Missile Support + Missile Mainstay 
 
Total Force Value = 696 


Mass Combat 
125 
 
Resolving War Engine Battles 
To resolve a war engine battle, a game master should complete the 
following steps: 
 
1. Add Situational Modifiers 
The game master should add situational modifiers to each force’s, 
total force value as appropriate. Some examples are given below: 
 
▶ Heavily Fortified: If the army is heavily fortified, such as 
defending a castle or within a trench complex, then the game 
master should add 300% to the total force value. 
 
▶ Fighting Over Favourable Terrain: If the army is fighting over 
favourable terrain, such as hillmen defending tight mountain trails 
against outlander invaders, then the game master should add 50% 
to the total force value. 
 
▶ Surprise: If the army takes their foes by surprise, then the game 
master should add 100% to the total force value. 
 
2. Declare Battle Plan 
Each side declares its battle plan from one of five possible plans: 
▶ All-Out Defence: The army waits in place and does its best to 
take up tactical defensive positions. A force on the all-out defence 
will hang back and skirmish, rarely inflicting losses on their foe in 
order to minimise their own casualties. 
▶ Hold the Line: An army that is holding the line will fight 
defensively, not moving to engage the enemy but not giving 
quarter either. It will refuse to retreat and ensure it doesn’t give 
ground easily.  
▶ Measured Engagement: If an army engages the foe in a 
measured way, then they are carefully using their resources to 
maximise their attacks whilst minimising their own casualties. 
There is a real acceptance that losses must be taken to secure 
meaningful gains in the field. 
▶ Aggressive Advance: The army commanders through their 
decision making behind the concept of speed and aggression, 
hoping to overwhelm their foe with sheer hostility. 
▶ All-Out Assault: The commander of this army shows little 
regard for their own troops as they throw unit after unit into the 
fray with a simple order: destroy the enemy.  
 
3. Roll Battle Die 
Both side rolls 1d100 and adds their Total Force Value to the roll, 
This total is known as the side’s battle score. The side with the 
highest battle score is the victor. 
 
Overkill 
Before rolling the battle dice, divide the larger Total Force Value 
by the smaller Total Force Value. If the result is 5 or more, do not 
roll the battle dice. The larger force automatically wins with no 
casualties and losing force is annihilated. 
 
4. Apply the Result 
To work out the success of each side in the battle, consult the table 
below. Before you do this though, you must work out the base 
casualty rate (BCR). To do this divide the victor’s battle score by 
the loser’s battle score and multiply the result by 10. This is the 
BCR: a percentage referenced in the table below. The army suffers 
causalities equal to this percentage (which may be over 100%, 
meaning the destruction of the fighting force). 
 
What do casualties mean? 
We can assume that when part of a force is taken to be casualties 
that roughly half these combatants are killed outright, and the 
other half are injured in some way, taking them out of the fight 
(possibly as prisoners).  
 
Player Characters in War Engine Battles 
After a battle each player character must roll the appropriate BCR 
for their army’s results entry by using a d100. If the player rolls 
equal to or under the listed value (such as BCRx2) on the d100, 
then they have been taken as a casualty, suffering serious injury. If 
this is the case, they must immediately save vs END again with a 
failure meaning that they have been killed in the fighting.

126 
 
Crime and Punishment
The legal system within a grubby fantasy campaign is often as 
present as is it is impenetrable. Here you will find rules as a game 
masters on how to adjudicate how the setting should respond to 
characters who inevitably commit crimes. 
 
The Legal System in Grubby Fantasy 
The game master is encouraged to use any subsystem (or none at 
all) to represent the legal processes within their campaign world. 
This section is provided to help a game master with such moments, 
and the following perceptions of the legal system are provided to 
assist game masters in producing a setting appropriate to the 
grubby fantasy genre: 
▶: Characters are, at least at lower levels, of low status and often 
rubbing shoulders within the criminal class (if they are not already 
criminals themselves!) 
▶: The legal system is unfair, especially to those of low status. It is 
often exploited and corrupted by those with power. 
▶: Mutation, illegal magic and heresy (committing crimes that go 
against the setting’s established gods and churches) is consistently 
ranked amongst the most heinous of crimes. 
▶: The legal system is bureaucratic and Kafkaesque - those without 
training find themselves completely at the mercy of preening 
officials. 
▶: Characters of higher status often have some protections and 
benefits within the judicial system, such as a right to not be 
tortured and a right to demand a trial by combat or a jury trial 
composing only those of the same social class. 
 
Legal Representation and the Sentencing Roll 
When a character is arrested and placed on trial within the legal 
system, then a game master may wish to use the following steps to 
adjudicate what the sentence handed down is: 
 
1. Choose Defence 
The accused decides who will represent them in court. Characters 
will often have to pay a lawyer, but if they (or a party member) 
have some proficiency with Lore (Law) then it may be more 
beneficial to choose them instead. For the cost of one court 
session, a lawyer of different proficiency levels can be hired are 
available below: 
 
LAYWER COSTS  
Prof Level 
Cost/Session 
Trained 
1gc 
Journeyman 
2gc 
Expert 
5gc 
Master 
10gc 
Grandmaster 
25gc 
 
2. Determine Modifiers 
Once the accused has decided who will represent them, calculate 
what modifiers to the upcoming sentencing roll there will be: 
 
LAWYER MODIFIER TABLE

-5%

Examples of Petty Crime 
Petty crimes generally include: 
 
Being a nuisance or making trouble. 
 
Assault and affray amongst peasants. 
 
Public indecency. 
 
Conspiracy to commit any of the above. 
Examples of Moderate Crime 
Moderate crimes generally include: 
 
Threatening actions such as drawing weapons. 
 
Bar fights. 
 
Minor theft. 
 
Toll evasion. 
 
Failure to pay debts. 
 
Conspiracy to commit any of the above. 
Examples of Serious Crimes 
Serious crimes generally include: 
 
Threatening nobles. 
 
Tax Evasion 
 
Murder. 
 
Possession of highly illegal goods. 
 
Conspiracy to commit any of the above. 
Examples of Blasphemous Crimes 
Blasphemous crimes generally include: 
 
Heresy. 
 
Apostacy. 
 
Witchcraft, necromancy or diabolism. 
 
Being a mutant. 
 
Conspiracy to commit any of the above.

Crime and Punishment 
127 
 
3 The Sentencing Roll 
Once all the modifiers have been determined, roll a d100 to 
determine what sentence (if any) the court hands down. 
 
SENTENCING TABLE 
D100 roll 
Sentence 
0-20 
Acquitted 
21-40 
Slap on the wrist 
41-60 
Lesser Fine 
61-70 
Lesser Fine and the Stocks 
71-80 
Major Fine and the stocks 
81-90 
Amputation 
91-99 
Prison Sentence, 1d10+10 years 
100-120 
Prison Sentence, Life 
120+ 
Death Sentence 
 
Sentence Descriptions 
Below are full descriptions for each of the possible sentences. 
 
Acquitted 
The character is found not guilty and released without further 
issue. 
 
Slap on the Wrist 
The character receives some sort of barely formal caution and is 
basically told not to it again. They may have to pay an absolutely 
nominal fine, like 1d6 pfennigs. They otherwise get off scot-free. 
 
Lesser Fine 
The character has to pay for some of the hurt they have caused. 
They are fined 3d10 shillings or all the money they have, 
whichever is lesser. 
 
Lesser Fine and the Stocks 
The character has to pay for some of the hurt they have caused. 
They are fined 3d10 shillings or all the money they have, 
whichever is lesser. In addition, they are locked in the stocks for 
1d4 days for the local children and idiots to abuse them with rotten 
fruit and insults.  
Major Fine and the Stocks 
The character is made to pay for the suffering they have caused. 
They are fined 1d10+10 gold pieces or all the money they have, 
whichever is lesser. In addition, they are locked in the stocks for 
1d4 days for the local children and idiots to abuse them with rotten 
fruit and insults.  
 
Amputation 
The character is dragged from the courtroom and in grisly fashion 
has a hand or foot cut off. Roll a d6, on 1-2 it is a foot, 3-6 it is a 
hand. If one foot is amputated, their Move is halved unless they 
can purchase a pegleg for 1d10 shillings at any time in a 
settlement. In this case, their Move is lowered by 5’. If both feet are 
missing, they must retire from adventuring. 
 
If a hand is missing, then they cannot hold anything in that hand 
but can still strap a shield to it. They may also purchase a fighting 
hook for 1d10 shillings at any time in a settlement, which counts as 
a simple melee weapon that does 2 piercing damage. If they lose 
both their hands, then they must master hookfighting. A character 
with no hands cannot cast spells. 
 
Prison Sentence, 1d10+10 Years 
The character is locked away in for many years. 
 
Prison Sentence, Life 
The character is locked away for the rest of their days. 
 
Death Sentence 
The judge places on his black hat and sentences the character to 
death. This usually takes the form of hanging, but in the cases of 
heresy or witchcraft it may take the form of burning. 


 
128 
 
Sickness and Disease
No campaign Mud, Blood & Rats would be complete without the 
persistent menace of pestilence hanging over the party. There are 
many different conditions that may be contracted by a character 
within such dirty, grubby worlds and below game masters will find 
information as to how to handle them from a game perspective. 
 
A Note on Diseases in Games 
It is worth addressing immediately that illnesses in heroic 
roleplaying games are often mechanically over-involved and can 
become difficult to adjudicate. Anything that takes a character out 
of the action for extended periods of time can be frustrating for the 
players who feel powerless and game masters who feel that the 
unfolding story is stymied by a character’s loss of agency within 
the game world. 
 
Therefore, this note is simply to acknowledge the following: you 
do not need to use these rules, or any such rules, if you do not 
wish to. These are only provided to help you apply such narrative 
elements in your game if you so wish, and it is the designers belief 
that should you hand wave the inclusion of lingering diseases 
entirely in your game, then your campaign will not suffer for it. 
 
How Diseases Work 
All diseases in Mud, Blood & Rats work in exactly the same way. 
They have an Initial Phase and a Developed Phase. When a 
character first contracts a disease, it is considered to be in the 
Initial Phase.  
 
At the start of each day, the character must make a save vs END. If 
they accrue too many failures as defined by the disease, then it 
moves onto the Developed Phase. If they accrue the maximum 
failures for the Developed Phase then the disease will list what 
happens then, which is usually be either death or a return to the 
initial phase. A character who succeeds enough saves vs END will 
gain some benefit (usually they are cured of the disease or return 
to the Initial Phase if they are currently in the Developed Phase. 
 
Bedrest and Medicine 
For each disease the character must complete continuous saves vs 
END. There are two ways a character may increase their chances of 
succeeding these. First, a character who completes no activity 
during the day and instead rests for the whole time in bed gains a 
+3 bonus to any save vs END they must make. Secondly an 
attending character can act like a doctor and may replace and save 
vs END the character must make with their own Healing 
(Surgery) test. 
 
Disease Descriptions 
Below are the descriptions for four possible example diseases. The 
game master is encouraged to use and modify them as they see fit 
to best suit their game world. 
 
The Dripping Anvil 
Initial Phase 
▶ Symptoms: 2 permanent drained conditions, a -3 penalty to all 
Perception tests that rely on hearing. The character feels a 
continuous pounding feeling in their head and a ringing, endless 
tinnitus. 
▶ Progress: The disease is cured if the character succeeds 3 saves 
vs END, but the disease progresses to the developed phase if the 
character fails 3 of the saves vs END. 
Developed Phase 
▶ Symptoms: 2 permanent drained conditions and automatically 
fails all Perception tests that rely on hearing. The character feels a 
continuous screeching noise in their head and their nose bleeds 
every few minutes. The character’s maximum FP is halved. 
▶ Progress: The disease is cured if the if the character succeeds 4 
saves vs END, but the disease moves to the initial phase if they fail 
4 of the saves vs END. 
The Green Flu 
Initial Phase 
▶ Symptoms: 2 permanent drained conditions, they feel weak, 
their nose runs profusely, and they come out in weeping green 
sores. The character’s maximum FP is halved. 
▶ Progress: The disease is cured if the character succeeds 4 hard 
(-3) saves vs END, but the disease progresses to the developed 
phase if the character fails 3 of the hard (-3) saves vs END. 
Developed Phase 
▶ Symptoms: 3 permanent drained conditions, their speed is 
halved, their body is covered in scabby green sores, and their skin 
turns a sickly yellow-green. The character’s maximum FP is halved. 
▶ Progress: The disease is cured if the character succeeds 5 hard 
(-3) saves vs END, but the character dies if they fail 4 of the hard 
(-3) saves vs END. 
 
The King of Poxes 
Initial Phase 
▶ Symptoms: 3 permanent drained conditions, their speed is 
halved, and they feel weak and tired. The character’s maximum FP 
is halved. 
▶ Progress: The disease is cured if the character succeeds 4 hard 
(-3) saves vs END, but the disease progresses to the developed 
phase if the character fails 3 of the hard (-3) saves vs END. 
Developed Phase 
▶ Symptoms: 6 permanent drained conditions, their speed is 
reduced to 0, their body breaks out in thousands of red sores that 
are raised and pulse with the person’s beating heart. Each day the 
character loses 1HP and cannot recover HP while they are still in 
the developed stage of this disease. The character’s maximum FP is 
reduced to 0. They feel very weak and it’s quite possible that 
they’re aware that they are dying. 
▶ Progress: The disease is cured if the character succeeds 3 hard 
(-3) saves vs END, but the character dies if they fail 2 of the hard 
(-3) saves vs END. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The Stinging Trots 
Initial Phase 
▶ Symptoms: 1 permanent drained condition. The character’s 
maximum FP is halved and every time the character rolls a natural 
1 or 2 on a test or save, the character must immediately succeed a 
hard (-3) save vs END or defecate. 
▶ Progress: The disease is cured if the character succeeds 3 saves 
vs END, but the disease progresses to the developed phase if the 
character fails 3 of the saves vs END. 
Developed Phase 
▶ Symptoms: 2 permanent drained conditions. The character’s 
maximum FP is halved and every time the character rolls a natural 
1-5 on a test or save, the character must immediately succeed a 
hard (-3) save vs END or defecate. 
▶ Progress: The disease is cured if the character succeeds 3 hard 
saves vs END, but the character dies if they fail 2 of the hard 
saves vs END. 


 
129 
 
Campaign Customisation
All games built off Journeyman, Expert, Master emphasise 
customisation, and Mud, Blood & Rats is no different. In this 
chapter you, the game master, will find tools for creating the 
campaign you want and optional rules and systems to help you 
customise it to your taste.  
Creating Statblocks 
Designing monstrous horrors for a campaign can be some of the 
most fun a game master can have. Below are some guidelines to help 
you determine generally balanced statistics and experience point 
values. It is emphasised that these are guidelines and not hard and 
fast rules or limits. There will be exceptions to all of the following. 
 
1. Concept 
Firstly, a game master must have a concept or idea that they wish to 
achieve in order to design an interesting foe. They should start by 
considering the relative power of the creature: a variant of tunnel-
building goblin may only be level 1 or 2, but an antediluvian eldritch 
horror may be level 25+. Choosing the level of a creature is a good 
place to start. Remember, the level of a creature will determine its 
target numbers for skills as well as its hit points. 
 
2. Core Attributes and Skills 
The game master should decide what they want the proficiency level 
of the core skills of a creature to be. A point worth noting is that it 
is far more likely that a higher-level creature would have attributes 
and skills of Master and Grandmaster than a lower-level creature. 
Consult the table below for creature target numbers:

6. Dramatic Creatures 
Once a creature has been completed there is one additional way it 
may be upgraded further, and that is by making it a dramatic 
creature: 
 
Dramatic creatures are incredibly powerful, often creatures out of 
myth and legend or named, high-ranking ‘boss’ creatures. To make 
a dramatic creature, a game master should give the creature a 
number of dramatic actions that they can take out of sequence, 
such as attacks and movement.

Campaign Customisation

Option: Classic Dice 
Dark, grim and grubby fantasy games have a long pedigree in the 
world of tabletop roleplaying. Anyone acquainted with the genre 
will probably be aware that many of these games, particularly older 
games, often use a d100 as their task resolution dice. The Classic 
Dice optional rule lets you swap out your d20s for d100s. 
 
Tests in Classic Dice 
To resolve a test using the Classic Dice system works very similarly 
to the standard test system in Mud, Blood & Rats. However, where 
a character must normally roll a d20 and aim to equal or beat their 
target number, in the Classic Dice system a character rolls 1d100 
and aims to equal or score lower than their target number. In this 
system, a target number effectively becomes the percentage chance 
that a character will succeed at a particular action. For this system 
to work, characters require target numbers specific to this system, 
as listed below. The usual number required on a d20 is listed 
alongside in square parentheses. 
 
Bonuses and Negatives to Tests 
In the classic dice system, all bonuses are multiplied by 5 and 
applied to the target number, not the roll. For example, a character 
with a +3 bonus usually increases their target number by 15%, 
making it 15% easier to succeed. No bonus can raise a target 
number above 95%. 
 
Difficulty Levels  
As previously stated, all modifiers in this system are applied to the 
target number and not the roll. The difficulty levels for tests in the 
Classic Dice system are listed below on the updated table: 
 
CLASSIC DICE DIFFICULTY LEVELS 
Difficulty 
Modifier to Target Number 
Routine 
+45% 
Very Easy 
+30% 
Easy 
+15% 
Challenging

Critical Successes and Failures 
For the most part, the mathematical chances of success and failure 
have been preserved in the Classic Dice system. However, critical 
success and failures are accounted for slightly differently. If using 
this system, the following rules apply for critical successes and 
failures: 
 
Critical Successes 
If a character rolls a success on the test and the natural roll is 
either a double (such as ‘11’ or ‘33’) or a natural ‘1’ then they have 
scored a critical success. 
 
Critical Failures 
If a character rolls a failure on the test and the natural roll is either 
a double (such as ‘66’ or ‘88’) or a natural ‘100’ then they have 
suffered a critical failure. 
 
In using this system, characters who are higher level and more 
proficient in their skill are more likely to succeed on a critical 
success and less likely to suffer a critical failure. 
 
Burning FP 
A character who burns FP to increase a test gains a +5% bonus to 
their target number per FP burned. A character cannot turn a 
failure into a critical success by burning FP, even if they raise their 
target number high enough to score a success while also having 
rolled doubles. 
 
 
 
 
Talents Affecting Criticals 
Some talents and other abilities allow a creature to score a critical 
success on less than a 20. If this is the case, use the following table 
to determine what % roll they must make to score a critical success: 
 
CLASSIC DICE – CRITICAL TALENTS

Hardcore Rend 
This is a minor optional rule that serves to make combat far 
deadlier by adjusting the Rend special rule on attacks and 
weapons. In a usual campaign of Mud, Blood & Rats, a Rend value 
may never affect a 6+ armour save, as natural ‘6’s always succeed.  
 
In a campaign that uses Hardcore Rend, natural ‘6’s do not 
automatically succeed and an attack that has a Rend value may 
remove an armour save completely if it reduces the armour save to 
worse than 6+. For example, a creature with an armour save of 5+ 
is struck by an attack that has the Rend (2) quality. In this 
situation, the target has no armour save and instead instantly takes 
the damage.  
 
This has the effect of making combat more brutal and explosive for 
both the characters and their enemies. It emphasises the value of 
weapons with the Rend quality, such as black powder weapons 
and larger melee weapons.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Opposed Attack Tests 
Game masters who wish to have rules that account for the varying 
qualities of melee fighters when both attacking and defending may 
wish to use these optional rules. In this system, when making a 
melee attack test instead of using the usual target number for the 
character’s proficiency level, the attack test becomes and opposed 
test set against opponents own melee skill. As this system already 
requires more bookkeeping, you are encouraged to use the 
following opposed table, instead of the usual one as this does not 
account for the attacker’s or defender’s level, allowing for faster 
adjudication: 
 
 
OPPOSED MELEE SKILL TABLE

If such a system is used then characters may burn FP on opposed 
Melee attack tests if they have the appropriate talent, ignoring the 
usual prohibition on burning FP on opposed tests. 
 
More Options: Damage Every Atack 
Game masters who wish to push the Opposed Attack Tests system 
further may opt to allow damage every time a melee attack is 
made. If this is used, then if the attacker is successful, they cause 
damage as usual. But if the attacker fails their attack test, then the 
defender may cause damage with a melee weapon they are holding 
against the attacker. This system makes combat faster, more 
terrifying and favours characters who are already more proficient 
with melee weapons.


 
134 
 
Bestiary
 
Reading a Bestiary Entry 
Each entry contains a number of abbreviations that are defined 
below: 
▶Type: The type of creature affects certain talents and HP. 
▶HP: Hit Points. This represents the general vitality and strength of 
a monster. 
▶ Composition: Provides additional detail on how this creature is 
encountered. 
▶ Morale: Lists the morale value of the creature, representing how 
easily they flee from combat. 
▶Move: How far the creature can move in the move step. 
▶ Size: There are six different categories of size: tiny, small, 
medium, large, huge and gargantuan. Tiny creatures take up a 
negligible amount of space and up to four Tiny creatures can fit in 
a 5’x5’ area. Small creatures are about the size of a halfling and take 
up 5’x5’ space. Medium creatures are about the size of a human and 
take up 5’x5’ space. Large creatures are about the size of an ogre 
and take up 10’x10’ space. Huge creatures are about the size of a 
boreal worm and take up 15’x15’ space. Gargantuan creatures are 
about the size of a dragon and take up to or more than a 20’x20’ 
space. 
▶ STR; AGI; END; INT; PER: The core attributes of the creature, 
along with its proficiency level and target number. 
▶ Skills: The skills a creature has, along with its proficiency level 
and target number. 
▶ Block/Dodge/Parry: The following three numbers are the target 
numbers required for the creature to perform these reactions. A ‘-‘ 
means the creature cannot perform this reaction. 
▶ Resistance: If a creature has resistance to a damage type then any 
damage it takes from an attack is halved, to a minimum of one.  
▶ Vulnerability: If a creature has vulnerability to a damage type 
then any damage it takes from an attack is doubled.  
▶ Immune: If the creature is hit with this damage type, then the 
creature takes no damage. 
▶ Actions: Lists the actions that a creature may take, such as attacks 
or casting spells. Some actions will have a recharge. Once they have 
been used once, then a d6 is rolled at the start of each of the 
creature’s actions. On the listed value, the action is ready to use 
again. For attacks, see the weapon traits in the Equipment, Gear and 
Services section of this book as needed (p.53). 
▶Armour Save: It tells you what the creature’s armour save is for 
each location. If there is a second save listed, such as 5+/6+ then the 
second save is a power save. 
▶ Abilities: Lists other abilities that a creature may have. 
▶XP: Experience points. The number of experience points that 
killing or defeating this creature gives to the party.  
▶Encounter Notes: Any additional information that the game 
master should know when using this creature. 
 
 
A Note on Melee and Ranged Specialism 
In the following creature statblocks, creatures only have Melee and 
Ranged listed without specialisms. It is assumed that whatever the 
type of Melee or Ranged a creature has, is the one required for its 
attacks. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Animals 
The world is full of all sorts of fauna that may be useful for a game 
master. At the very least, a campaign full of ratcatchers are likely to 
have a high attrition rate on small dogs!

Demons 
Demons are inimical monsters native to the plane of hell. Such 
creatures are sometimes brought forth by the designs of diabolists 
while others find ways into the material world through more 
accidental or horrifying means. 
 
 
Demon of Covetousness – Level 14 
Demons of covetousness may appear as corpulent merchants, reedy 
pipers or enchanting women. In all cases, they lead the goodly folk 
into a mire of jealousy and corruption. 
Type: Demon 
Size: Medium 
HP: 15  Composure: F (1)  Move: 40’ 
STR: T (10); AGI: G (6); END: T (11); INT: E (8); PER: J (9) 
Skills: Dodge: G (6); Melee: M (7) 
Block/Dodge/Parry: -/6/7 
Resistances: All damage from non-magical attacks. 
Vulnerabilities: Holy.  
Actions:  
▶ 3 x Talon Attack: Melee (7); 8 piercing, rend (1). 
Armour Saves: 
L-Leg: 5+/4+ (Skin + Demonic) 
R-Arm: 5+/4+ (Skin + Demonic) 
R-Leg: 5+/4+ (Skin + Demonic) 
Body: 5+/4+ (Skin + Demonic) 
L-Arm: 5+/4+ (Skin + Demonic) 
Head: 5+/4+ (Skin + Demonic) 
Abilities:  
▶ Aura of Jealousy: Whenever a creature comes within 10’ of this 
demon, the creature must succeed a hard (-3) save vs PER or 
immediately be filled with visions of jealous desire. A creature is 
subject to this jealousy for 1 turn (10 minutes) but may repeat the 
hard (-3) save vs PER at the start of each round. A creature affected 
by the jealousy does not take their usual actions, instead they do roll 
on the below table and complete the listed action: 
Aura of Jealousy Table 
d6 
Action 
1 
The subject attacks their friends if able. 
2-3 
The subject does nothing. 
4-5 
The subject moves in a random direction. 
6 
The subject may act normally 
▶ Corrupting Aspect: Seeing this creature is corrupting. When this 
creature is first viewed, the viewer must succeed a very hard (-6) 
save vs PER or gain 1d4 grime points. 
▶ Fearsome: Seeing this creature is frightening. When this creature 
is first viewed, the viewer must succeed a hard (-3) Composure test 
or gain a frightened condition.

creature is first viewed, the viewer must succeed a very hard (-6)

humans. They can be aloof, pretentious and arrogant but their 
guiding principles of devotion and preservation have helped steer 
the known world through more calamities than most people will 
ever realise.

the children of the woods.

Halflings 
Halflings are stout humanoids with a penchant for rich foods. Their 
love of gastronomy is almost religious and they defend their shires 
with as much fierceness as any human army. 
 
Halfling Cook – Level 8 
In halfling societies, cooks and chefs are valued members of the 
community, often passing recipes down from one generation to the 
next.

Monsters 
Monsters are real and terrifying. They exist in the dark places 
where sensible people do not go. Occasionally, some monsters 
may find themselves heading towards settled locations and polite 
societies. Sometimes humanity can quickly band together to deal 
with these creatures, other times, in the cases of the more 
awesome and horrific monsters, an untold level of destruction can 
be wreaked.  
 
Basilisk – Level 17 
Most folk don’t even really believe that basilisks exist anymore – they 
are six-legged giant lizards that haunt children’s nightmares and 
can petrify with a look. 
Type: Monster 
Size: Huge 
HP: 24  Composure: E (7)  Move: 30’ (30’ swim) 
STR: E (7); AGI: E (7); END: E (7); INT: U (11); PER: U (11) 
Skills: Melee: E (7); Stealth (Any): J (8) 
Block/Dodge/Parry: -/11/- 
Resistances: Slashing, Piercing and Bludgeoning damage from non-
magical attacks. 
Actions:  
▶ 2 x Claws Attack; 1 x Bite: Melee (7); 7 slashing, rend (1);  
Melee (7); 6 piercing, rend (2). 
Armour Saves: 
L-Leg: 4+ (Thick Skin) 
R-Arm: 4+ (Thick Skin) 
R-Leg: 4+ (Thick Skin) 
Body: 4+ (Thick Skin) 
L-Arm: 4+ (Thick Skin) 
Head: 4+ (Thick Skin) 
Dramatic Actions: 3/round 
▶ Claw Attack: Melee (7); 7 slashing, rend (1) 
▶ Slither Away: The creature moves up to 30’ without provoking 
attacks of opportunity. 
Abilities:  
▶ Crush: This creature may walk over other creatures smaller than 
itself. If it does so, the creature that is stepped on suffers 3 
bludgeoning damage, rend (1). This ability may only affect a creature 
once per round. 
▶ Fearsome: Seeing this creature is frightening. When this creature 
is first viewed, the viewer must succeed a hard (-3) Composure test 
or gain a frightened condition. 
▶ Greater Brutal Smash: [Recharge 5+]: The creature makes a 
melee attack action. If the attack hits, it deals +4 damage. 
▶ Petrifying Gaze: At the start of a round, any creature that can see 
the basilisk must make a hard (-3) save vs END, gaining a slowed 
condition on a failure. If the creature already has a slowed condition, 
then they turn to stone. 
 
Cave Maw – Level 5 
Cave maws are cannibalistic creatures with reptilian skin. Their 
name comes from the fact that to most people they look like nothing 
more than large, toothy mouths on quadrupedal legs.

R-Arm: 4+ (Thick Skin)

Dragon – Level 24 
Dragons are creatures of nightmare and corruption. At one time they 
may have been a noble race, but the grime of this plane and the 
grinding madness of time has reduced them to mere behemoths of 
untold destruction. 
Type: Monster 
Size: Gargantuan 
HP: 36  Composure: G (4)  Move: 40’ (60’ fly) 
STR: G (4); AGI: M (5); END: G (4); INT: E (6); PER: M (5) 
Skills: Melee: G (4) 
Block/Dodge/Parry: -/10/- 
Resistances: Slashing, Piercing and Bludgeoning damaging. 
Actions:  
▶ 2 x Claws Attack; 1 x Bite: Melee (4); 11 slashing, rend (1); 
Melee (4); 10 piercing, rend (2). 
▶ Flaming Breath: [Recharge 5+]: The dragon exudes a 55’ cone 
of fire. All creatures in the cone take 20 fire damage, rend (2). A 
character may attempt a dodge test to halve the damage. 
Armour Saves: 
L-Leg: 1+ (Scales) 
R-Arm: 1+ (Scales) 
R-Leg: 1+ (Scales) 
Body/Wings: 2+ (Scales) 
L-Arm: 1+ (Scales) 
Head: 1+ (Scales) 
Dramatic Actions: 5/round 
▶ 1 x Claws Attack: Melee (4); 11 slashing rend (1). 
▶ 1 x Tail Whip Attack: Melee (4); 9 bludgeoning, and the target 
must save vs STR or be knocked 30’ from the dragon. 
Abilities:  
▶ Creature of Myth: Automatically passes a save it was called upon 
to make. It can do this five times per day. 
▶ Flying Vitality: If this creature is at half hit points or fewer, it 
cannot fly. 
▶ Superior Crush: This creature may walk over other creatures 
smaller than itself. If it does so, the creature that is stepped on suffers 
6 bludgeoning damage, rend (2). This ability may only affect a 
creature once per round. 
▶ Terrifying: Seeing this creature is utterly terrifying. When this 
creature is first viewed, the viewer must succeed a very hard (-6) 
Composure test or gain a frightened condition. 
Encounter Notes:  
▶ If a dragon is encountered as part of wilderness travel and 
exploration, there is a 95% chance that it is asleep.

Fomorian – Level 18 
A fomorian is to forestborn what a giant is to humans. It is a vast 
monstrous humanoid whose very existence is tied to the woods. Some 
of these ugly giants are one-eyed while others have horns or even a 
baleful gaze. 
Type: Monster 
Size: Huge 
HP: 30  Composure: M (7)  Move: 40’ 
STR: G (6); AGI: T (10); END: M (7); INT: T (10); PER: J (9) 
Skills: Melee: E (8); Athletics (Throwing): J (9) 
Block/Dodge/Parry: -/11/8 
Actions:  
▶ 2 x Club Attack: Melee (8); 9 slashing rend (2), Sweep (15’). 
▶ Devour Person Attack: [Recharge 5+]: The fomorian makes a 
melee attack test against a single foe of medium or smaller size. On 
a success, the creature is grabbed. As an action a grabbed creature 
may attempt a very hard (-9) AGI save to escape on their turn as 
an action. A grabbed creature will also be dropped if the arm holding 
them suffers 6 points of damage or more in one round. If the creature 
is still in the fomorian’s clutch on the fomorian’s next action, the 
fomorian will eat them whole, often by starting with the head. 
▶ Throw Boulder Attack: Athletics (9); 10 piercing, rend (1), (80’ 
range). 
Armour Saves: 
L-Leg: 4+/6+ (Thick skin/Aura) 
R-Arm: 4+/6+ (Thick skin/Aura) 
R-Leg: 4+/6+ (Thick skin/Aura) 
Body: 4+/6+ (Thick skin/Aura) 
L-Arm: 4+/6+ (Thick skin/Aura) 
Head: 4+/6+ (Thick skin/Aura) 
Dramatic Actions: 3/round 
▶ Club Attack: Melee (8); 9 slashing, rend (2), sweep (15’). 
Abilities:  
▶ Creature of Legend: Automatically passes a save it was called 
upon to make. It can do this three times per day. 
▶ Superior Crush: This creature may walk over other creatures 
smaller than itself. If it does so, the creature that is stepped on suffers 
6 bludgeoning damage, rend (2). This ability may only affect a 
creature once per round. 
▶ Superlative Brutal Smash: [Recharge 5+]: The creature makes a 
melee attack action. If the attack hits, it deals +6 damage. 
▶ Terrifying: Seeing this creature is utterly terrifying. When this 
creature is first viewed, the viewer must succeed a very hard (-6) 
Composure test or gain a frightened condition.

Bestiary 
150 
 
Mutants 
Mutation is a present fear of the good folk of the world, for as 
soon as the stigmata of corruption begins to show an individual 
can expect nothing but hatred and violence from society. Mutants 
are those wretches whose bodies and minds have become warped, 
leaving civilisation to live desperate lives in the wilderness, far 
from the prying eyes of interrogators and witch hunters. 
 
Optional Rule: Random Mutations 
Combating mutants often forms a significant part of a campaign of 
Mud, Blood & Rats and therefore a game master may wish to roll 
on the following table for any mutants the characters encounter in 
order to add flavour and varieties to those encountered. 
 
RANDOM MUTATION TABLE 
D20 
Result 
1 
Goat legs (cosmetic) 
2 
Dog legs (cosmetic) 
3 
Pinhead (PER and INT Untrained) 
4 
Baby hands (Can’t carry a weapon) 
5 
One arm is absurdly muscled (+2 to melee damage) 
6 
Dog-headed (cosmetic) 
7 
Tentacles sprout from mouth (cosmetic) 
8 
One arm is a tentacle (cosmetic) 
9 
Covered in weeping sores (cosmetic) 
10 
Blood is acidic (2 acid damage against any target that 
deals melee damage to the mutant) 
11 
Pig-headed (cosmetic) 
12 
Extra arm (Can attack twice as one action) 
13 
Fish-headed (Can breathe under water) 
14 
Covered in sharp spines (Can perform a melee attack, 
hugging an opponent: 5 piercing damage. 
15 
Giggles constantly like a child (cosmetic) 
16 
Has a leech mouth (cosmetic) 
17 
Smells horrendous (Anyone coming within 5’ must save 
vs END or gain 2 stunned conditions once) 
18 
Mouth is a proboscis (Can perform a melee attack: 3 
piercing damage, rend (1)). 
19 
Iron-like skin (+1 to Armour saves on all locations) 
20 
Chest has a giant mouth (+1 extra melee attack that 
causes 4 piercing damage) 
 
Mutagenic Monstrosity – Level 14 
Some creatures become so mutated that they no longer resemble who 
they once were. These rolling nightmares of mutated flesh, grasping 
arms and gibbering mouths are corruption manifest. 
Type: Mutant 
Size: Large 
HP: 21  Composure: F (1)  Move: 35’ 
STR: M (7); AGI: T (10); END: E (8); INT: U (12); PER: U (12)

▶ Crush: This creature may walk over other creatures smaller than 
itself. If it does so, the creature that is stepped on suffers 3 
bludgeoning damage, rend (1). This ability may only affect a creature 
once per round. 
▶ Fearsome: Seeing this creature is frightening. When this creature 
is first viewed, the viewer must succeed a hard (-3) Composure test 
or gain a frightened condition. 
▶ Greater Brutal Smash: [Recharge 5+]: The creature makes a 
melee attack action. If the attack hits, it deals +4 damage.

Bestiary 
151 
 
NPCs 
Here is a collection of the ever-varied inhabitants of a grim and 
grubby fantasy world. From preening nobles, grimy bandits, and 
other colourful weirdos, a game master should be able to find 
statistics for any such NPC they require. 
 
A Note on NPC Heritages 
The following statblocks do not list a heritage under type. They 
have been created with humans in mind, but should you wish to 
use any of these statistics for say dwarfs, elves, haflings, or ogres, 
you are more than welcome to do so. 
Dwarf/Halflings 
If you alter the statblocks to refer to dwarf or halfling NPCs, just 
remember that these creatures only move 25’ per round. Similarly, 
halflings count as ‘small’ creatures. 
Ogres 
If you alter these statblocks to refer to ogre NPCs, just remember 
that they are ‘large’ size category and should deal +1 damage with 
all their melee attacks.

hunters. 
Type: Humanoid, Heretic, Spellcaster 
Size: Medium 
HP: 17  Composure: E (9)  Move: 30’ 
STR: U (11); AGI: E (7); END: J (8); INT: E (7); PER: E (7) 
Skills: Dodge: M (6); Melee: T (9); Sorcery: G (5) 
Block/Dodge/Parry: -/6/- 
Actions:  
▶ 2 x Cast Spell: A witch has 6 sorcery dice and counts successes 
on a 3+. They know the following spells and have a +2 spellcasting 
damage modifier:  
 Arcane Arrow 
 Baleful Domination 
 Blight 
 Cure of Thorns 
 Pricking Thumbs 
 Sepulchral Bolt 
 Whispering Grimalkin 
 Wilt and Wither 
 Witch’s Tickle 
Armour Saves: 
L-Leg: 6+/5+ (None/Magick) 
R-Arm: 6+/5+ (None/Magick) 
R-Leg: 6+/5+ (None/Magick) 
Body: 6+/5+ (None/Magick) 
L-Arm: 6+/5+ (None/Magick) 
Head: 6+/5+ (None/Magick) 
Dramatic Actions: 5/round 
▶ Command: The creature allows one ally it can see to make a move 
or an attack. 
▶ Cast Spell: The creature casts a spell as described above. 
▶ Shift: The creature moves up to its move speed without provoking 
attacks of opportunity. 
Abilities:  
▶ Master of Magic: This creature may reroll up to three failed 
sorcery dice per round. This does not allow them to reroll natural 
‘1’s.

Ogres 
Ogres are huge creatures which some consider to be monsters. 
They are a canny race of creatures with a love of food similar to 
that of halflings. Their brawny frame and size means few creatures 
are likely to get in their way.

presence on others around them.

the anathema to everything that is right and just about the 
universe.

Banshees haunt marshes and fens and have a terrible cry that kills 
those who hear it.

creatures within 30’ of the banshee that can hear the shriek must 
make a hard (-3) save vs END or drop to 0HP and be unconscious 
and dying.

sewers and darker places. They are a strange type of mutant that

Appendices

Appendix 2: Virtues and Vices 
The following tables can help you to determine a character’s virtues and vices.
 
APPENDIX TABLE 7: RANDOM VIRTUES 
D20 
Virtue 
1 
Familial Bond: I love my family, perhaps a particular such as a sibling or parent. I will do anything to protect them from the harsh 
realities of this world. 
2 
Knowledge: I believe that a curious mind and the nourishment of one’s intelligence is a paramount quality. Whenever I can I seek 
out knowledge and attempt to share my education with others. 
3 
Discipline: I am a disciplined person. I have a strong sense of what makes a proper person, and I will do my absolute best to adhere 
to this ideal, especially in a world of such chaos. 
4 
Piety: I know the gods are real – their work is evident. I will do my best to promote the ideals and values of my patron god, in 
whom I belief all of life’s problems may rectified. 
5 
Hopeful: Though hope is rare and fragile, I refuse to let it die. I nurture what little optimism is in others and keep watch for the 
slightest possibility of a better future. 
6 
Born into Hardship: I know what it is like to live in abject penury. As a result, I sympathise with the poor and destitute. I will never 
turn someone down if they beg of me for food and I do my best to make sure individuals have a dry place to sleep. 
7 
Leadership: Whether I am a natural leader or not, I fully understand that when people are in a difficult situation they will look to 
others for leadership and guidance. I am happy to be that person and will always step in if required. 
8 
Guardianship: I feel personally responsible for someone or something fragile: be it a child, an elder, a relic, or even a forlorn 
promise. I will protect what I guard with unwavering devotion. 
9 
Courage: I am a courageous person in the truest sense. I will always act to face down evil and confront my own fear where possible 
even when everyone else falters. 
10 
Honesty: Lies have destroyed kingdoms and families alike. I strive to speak plainly and truthfully, even when the truth may put me, 
or my companions in danger. 
11 
Mercy: Even in this wretched world, I believe in mercy. I will spare those who show remorse, and I will offer comfort where I can, 
even if I cannot truly save them. 
12 
Temperance: Excess leads only to ruin. I keep my desires in check and refuse to indulge in distractions that would cloud my 
judgment or weaken my resolve. 
13 
Oathbound: I honour every promise I make. My word is unbreakable, even when keeping it puts me at risk or my companions at 
risk. I will not break a promise even if to keep tests who I think I am. 
14 
Defiance: I refuse to bow to tyrants, monsters, or even destiny itself. Even when hope is gone and the demons are here, I will stand, 
I will resist, and I will fight. 
15 
Justice: I cannot tolerate cruelty or oppression. When I see suffering caused by the callous or powerful, I am compelled to act, even 
if vengeance costs me dearly or endangers me. 
16 
Dignity: Even in filth, fear, and ruin, I hold myself to a standard of personal honour. No matter what horrors I face, I strive to 
conduct myself with quiet pride that the world cannot strip away. 
17 
Companionship: I deeply care about those who I travel with. I will not allow them to come to harm if I can help it and when the 
chips are down, I would risk death or madness to protect them. 
18 
Remembrance: I carry the memories of those who came before: fallen comrades, lost people, nameless dead. I strive to honour their 
stories and ensure they are not forgotten by time or decay. 
19 
Ingenuity: The world offers little, so I make do with less. I pride myself on cleverness, improvisation, and resourcefulness, finding 
solutions where others see only dead ends. 
20 
Reject Ignorance: I will promote the value of knowledge and scholarship, especially in a world of such fearful ignorance. I cannot 
countenance decisions being made based on superstition, quackery or fear. 
 
 
 


Appendices 
169 
 
APPENDIX TABLE 8: RANDOM VICES 
D20 
Vice 
1 
Greed: I am incredibly avaricious. I cannot turn down the chance to increase my wealth, even at the expense of friends. At all times I 
am looking to gain my next bit of coin. 
2 
Cowardice: When the chips are down, I am a coward. I will run away and abandon my friends when the going gets tough, especially 
if the tough is frightening! 
3 
Treachery: I will happily sell out my own grandmother to advance my own position. I cannot be relied upon to help others if there 
is possibility that I could progress without doing so. 
4 
Gluttony: I love food and drink too much. I am constantly pestering my companions as to when we will next me eating. I find it 
incredibly hard to resist anything edible. 
5 
Bloodlust: I have low self-control towards hurting others and often turn to violence as the answer. Leaving prisoners with me is a 
bad idea, unless you want them roughed up a bit. 
6 
Miserly: I find it incredibly hard to spend money on anyone, including myself. I persistently bemoan the cost of things, the nature of 
business and have genuine fear of the great evil: economic inflation. 
7 
Pretentious: I love to use words I don’t understand, talk over others in conversation and generally act better than I am. The reason I 
do this is either insecurity or a genuine belief in my own superiority. 
8 
Short-Tempered: I have a short temper. It really doesn’t take much for me to lose my patience and start saying (and doing) things 
that are simple not in my best interest. 
9 
Liar: I am a liar. I lie a lot. I lie so much that it becomes difficult for my companions to tell if I’m speaking the truth or fabricating 
another story. 
10 
Arrogant: I am absolutely convinced I am the most capable person in any room. I dismiss the opinions of others, even when they 
clearly know more than I do. 
11 
Sadistic: I delight in the suffering of others. When someone is at my mercy, I do not show restraint and often come up with 
inventive ways of tormenting the victim. 
12 
Addiction: I latch onto whatever gives me the slightest bit of comfort and this comes out as an addiction. This could be anything 
from drink, drugs, charms, rituals, risky behaviours. Once I start with something, stopping is almost impossible. 
13 
Vain: My appearance is of supreme importance to me. Even in a festering pit of slime and filth, I worry about my clothes, my scent, 
or whether my hair is sitting right. What is victory if you get blood stains on your new doublet? 
14 
Jealous: I cannot stand others’ successes or victories. When someone has something which I lack, such wealth, status, affection I 
become bitter, resentful, and sometimes quietly vindictive. 
15 
Nihilistic: I believe nothing truly matters. Morality, duty, even life itself often feels pointless to me. As a result, I often act recklessly 
or apathetically.  
16 
Machiavellian: I constantly scheme and pull strings to get my way. Whether by flattery, guilt, or deceit, I twist others’ emotions to 
suit my needs. 
17 
Superstitious: I am ruled by omens, signs, and bizarre rituals. I refuse to act until I’ve completed whatever odd superstition grips 
me, and I often halt the group over something as trivial as cattle staring at me inappropriately. 
18 
Hoarder: I compulsively collect things whether they are useful or not. Broken charms, scraps of cloth, odd bones, shiny rocks, etc. I 
insist they might be valuable ‘one day’. My pack is impossibly heavy, and I refuse to throw anything away. 
19 
Grudging: I hold onto every slight, insult, or injury, no matter how small. I stew in resentment and bring up old grievances at the 
worst possible moments, poisoning relationships and group morale. 
20 
Impulsive: I act first and think later (sometimes never). Whether running headlong into danger, blurting out a secret, or accepting a 
cursed object, I repeatedly make snap decisions that create more problems than they solve.
